He found himself drawn gradually, against 
his will, deeper and deeper into the shifting 
sands of Sicilian politics. Protracted at- 
tempts to remove the Queen, to impose a 
new constitution (modelled on British lines) 
upon an almost feudal society, and to free 
Sicily from Neapolitan domination led to 
Bentinck’s becoming a virtual dictator. 
Benevolent though his rule was, it began to 
crumble when he conceived an ill-timed plan 
to establish Sicily as a British Colony. This 
was resented, naturally enough, by the 
Sicilian Royalists, but also by his own 
Government, whose foreign policy at the 
time did not favour the move. Bentinck’s 
irresponsible behaviour as commander of 
an expeditionary force in Italy brought 
matters to a head, and he was recalled in 
disgrace in 1815. 
The war ended; Britain ceased to take an 
interest in Sicily’s domestic troubles, and all 
traces of the abortive Parliamentary system 
gradually disappeared. 
Such, in outline, is Dr Rosselli’s theme; 
and his telling of it is no less entertaining 
than the full story itself. His book will 
interest not only students of European 
history and British foreign policy, but all 
those who are concerned with the moral 
issues that arise when a great power inter- 
feres in the affairs of a smaller power.

<<<PDFPAGE 13>>>
PREAACE 
This book—there is no disguising it—started out as a dissertation 
for the Ph.D. degree in the University of Cambridge, and later 
turned into a Thirlwall Prize essay. But I have revised and in 
part rewritten it for publication. As it stands it rests on a good 
deal of earlier work by British and Italian historians, notably 
Sir Charles Webster’s great work on Castlereagh. But I have 
tried to tell from the point of view of both countries the story 
of events which have to do at once with diplomatic and with 
local political history, as well as—in their abortive way—with 
the history of British colonial expansion. 
His Grace the Duke of Portland allowed me to use Lord 
William Bentinck’s papers, now on loan to Nottingham 
University Library. Professor Walter Maturi, of Turin Universi- 
ty, let me read his transcript of King Ferdinand’s Diary, which 
together with the other Bourbon papers in the Naples State 
Archives was destroyed in 1943; he also gave me much helpful 
advice. Miss H. M. Lackland (Mrs F. Best) lent me books and 
notes which she had made for her earlier study of the subject. 
The late Senator Benedetto Croce also let me see copies of 
documents in his possession. Professor Gaetano Salvemini read 
an early version of the MS., questioned a number of points, and 
so led me to do all I could to strengthen my argument. Mr G. 
Ellis Flack, librarian, and Miss J. Renshaw, archivist of Notting- 
ham University Library, gave me much practical help; so did 
Mr R.H. Ellis and other officials of the Public Record Office, 
and Dr E. Librino and Dr L. A. Pagano of the Palermo State 
Archives. I have also had advice and encouragement from 
Mr C. W. Crawley, Mr D. Mack Smith, and others. 
I am 
thankful to them all. What I owe the Master of Peterhouse the 
dedication tries to say. 
March 1955

Fs GRIPE 
When the Bourbons were driven out of Naples in 1860 they left 
behind one collection of State and private papers and took another 
with them into their Bavarian exile. The first collection, after 
having been made public only in 1930, was destroyed in 1943. 
The second has now been bought by the Italian State and returned 
to the Naples State Archives. Most of it-was there made available 
to students in the late summer of last year, when this book was 
already in page-proof. Through the courtesy of Count Filangieri, 
Superintendent of the Naples Archives, I was able on a recent 
visit to read some of the papers and to go through the inventory. 
Much of the collection, as it turns out, duplicates that which was 
lost in 1943 (and which several historians had previously studied). 
The new material concerning the Napoleonic period seems likely 
to yield some interesting details rather than to alter anyone’s 
interpretation of important matters. 
January 1956 
1h.

NOTE * 
In transcribing from English documents of the period I have 
modernised the spelling and punctuation: with this exception, 
that to keep the flavour of Bentinek’s Journal I have left his 
punctuation (or the lack of it) unchanged, as well as his spelling 
of foreign words. 
An asterisk in the text refers the reader to the notes at the 
end of the book. 

<<<PDFPAGE 17>>>
INGRODUCGTION 
I 
When a Great Power defends a small one in time of war the 
Great Power often finds it hard not to meddle in the domestic 
concerns of the small Power. At the same time the Great Power 
is often less interested in these domestic concerns than the small 
Power (or the later historian) imagines. The British occupation 
of Sicily between 1806 and 1815 
illustrates both these truths. 
What is surprising is not that Britain interfered, but that her 
Governments twice decided to interfere in full and deliberate 
strength. The first attempt, in 1806, came to nothing. The 
second, in 1811, the Government entrusted to Lord William 
Bentinck to carry out as Minister to the Sicilian Court and 
Commander-in-Chief in the Mediterranean. This time the con- 
sequences were far beyond what anybody—Government or 
envoy—had had in mind at first. 
At the end of 1805 the Court of Naples, for the second time 
in seven years, abandoned their continental possessions to the 
French and fled to Sicily. From then on British Governments 
provided money, troops, and ships to keep the island—but for 
Sardinia the only piece of Italian soil outside French control—in 
friendly hands. For most of the next six years this was all they 
cared for. It was a negative policy: the highest stakes lay else- 
where. To people in Sicily, of course, things looked very 
different, and in particular to the British agents there. A month’s 
voyage separated them from home and left them mostly to 
their own devices. The problems they faced were thorny and 
seemed urgent. They distrusted the Court and the Court dis- 
trusted them. As the years wore on relations became steadily 
worse. By 1811, when the British Government made up its 
mind to intervene, and stuck to its decision, a succession of 
Ministers and Commanders had long been urging it to do so. 
What made this intervention notable was that it became 
inextricably mixed with a crisis in the domestic politics of Sicily. 
The internal struggle that led to the revolution of 1812 could 
not have taken the course it did without British help; nor could 

<<<PDFPAGE 18>>>
2 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
Britain have intervened as she did had that struggle not provided 
both occasion and warrant. 
The British Government, which thought it was trying to 
achieve immediate military ends, found itself forwarding a 
liberal revolution in Sicily and plans for independence and unity 
in continental Italy. This happened not merely because the chief 
agent, Bentinck, behaved as he did, but because (as so often 
before and since) diplomatic and domestic affairs could not be 
kept separate: they depended and acted on one another. 
What happened in Sicily to help along this interplay was the 
rise of a movement of resistance to royal power which in the 
end became the ‘English party’. But what was this movement? 
It has not even an accepted name: the ‘patriotic’ movement, a 
name used at the time, must do as well as any. Some historians 
have seen in it a straightforward liberal opposition to despotism, 
some (more recently) the embodiment of class privilege and 
insular tradition. But this is too simple. For it was their very 
particularism that led the more advanced patriots to attempt a 
reform, along English lines, of the country’s institutions; and if 
they had succeeded they would have ended by jettisoning many 
of the privileges which their movement as a whole had begun 
by defending. But although this attempt at making reform 
grow from an enduring root of conservatism had (not quite by 
chance) an affinity with English practice, it did not work in 
Sicily: the outcome was not a resolution but a lasting contra- 
diction which the movement was never able to get over. 
The movement began and ended as the affair of a small 
number of nobles and their dependents, mostly lawyers and 
clerics. Useless to look for political consciousness or strength 
elsewhere; useless, too, in a country where land-ownership 
conferred the highest prestige and tended to draw what wealth 
there was away from industry or trade, to look for a middle 
class apart from these people and from a number of landlords’ 
agents or chief tenants.* This aristocracy faced two dangers. One 
was external—the inroads of a monarchy bent on centralisation 
and authoritarian reform; the other internal—the gradual worsen- 
ing of the barons’ own economic position. The one scratched 
the juridical shell of Sicilian feudalism; the other threatened to 
eat it away from within. 

<<<PDFPAGE 19>>>
INTRODUCTION 
3 
The policy of the central Government—it was the typical 
enlightened absolutism of the time—was launched by the 
Viceroy Caracciolo in the 1780's; his successors then carried it 
forward with more or less determination. 
Caracciolo tried to restrict the extremely wide powers of 
alienation and succession which the barons had enjoyed ever 
since the period of feudal anarchy under the Aragonese kings; 
he investigated dubious titles; he attacked feudal jurisdictions; 
and above all he attempted to deprive the barons of their virtual 
freedom from taxation. All these measures constituted the first 
serious threat to the aristocracy as the ruling class, and led to 
a constitutional conflict between Crown and Parliament.* The 
conflict never came to a head in the 1780’s and 90’s, because the 
Crown chose to yield or compromise; but it was only put off; 
and meanwhile Parliament had begun to appear, for the first 
time, as the focus of baronial resistance to the Crown. This 
Parliament, the only one of its kind in Italy, had until then been 
no more than an Estates General, meeting once every four years 
to vote supply—an automatic process—and present petitions. 
But now its one real power, that of distributing taxation—a 
power of the greatest importance to the barons—had been 
challenged. From then on its members gradually became better 
aware of their constitutional rights than they had ever been 
while those rights were still unquestioned.” 
At the same time the barons were beginning to notice a more 
radical threat to their predominance. It has lately been pointed 
out* that the growth of absentee landlordship in the 18th century 
was accompanied by a sharp rise in prices: so that while the 
barons’ expenditure increased, the real value of the fixed rents 
they drew from their estates tended to fall. In other words, the 
barons, while they gradually surrendered to intermediaries their 
functions as feudal landlords, began to lose the benefits which 
those functions had once brought them. The greatest families 
were often unable to avoid huge burdens of debt and mortgages; 
some even went bankrupt. Hence the restrictions which the 
feudal system still placed on the alienation of lands began to 
appear burdensome, and the system itself to lose its raison d étre. 
This helps to explain why, in 1812, the barons’ triumphant 
assertion of the constitutional rights of Parliament went together 

<<<PDFPAGE 20>>>
4 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
with a spontaneous renunciation of feudal privileges—that is, of 
the very privileges in whose defence the rights of Parliament 
had in the first place been invoked. 
But these economic straits alone are not the whole explanation. 
It will not do to assume that men are always aware of their own 
interests. In fact, conservatism and esprit de corps often led a 
majority of the barons to act against what might have seemed 
their economic interest; and, in contrast, another reason for the 
growing concern with reform was the spread among the small 
class of educated Sicilians of the commonplaces of Enlightened 
thought. These motives in turn were often mixed. It is striking 
to see how the particularism and attachment to tradition charac- 
teristic of an island on the edge of Europe infused into the 
Enlightenment a distinct local colour. It is curious, for instance, 
that the historian and jurist Gregorio, writing at the end of the 
18th century, should have based his assertion of the supremacy 
of public law over private, and of the monarchy over particular 
privilege, on an appeal to the past—to the Norman Conquest 
and to the golden age of strong central government that followed 
it, as against the Aragonese period of baronial anarchy. Reform, 
in Gregorio’s view, was to be achieved not by wiping the slate 
clean, but by restoring the Sicilian Constitution to its pristine 
purity, shorn of later and ‘unnatural’ growths.* Similarly, the 
Abate Paolo Balsamo, a professor of agriculture and political 
economy, while he followed the teaching of Arthur Young and 
Adam Smith in these subjects, looked, when he came to deal 
with constitutional issues, to the Aragonese period as to the 
fount of Sicilian liberties; and when he turned to Blackstone 
for guidance in drafting the 1812 Constitution, he drew not 
merely on Blackstone’s paraphrase of Montesquieu, but on his 
interpretation of the English common law—between which and 
the inherited mass of Sicilian law a parallel could be drawn.* 
One may see as another sign of the Sicilians’ insularity and 
traditionalism the scant interest they took in the egalitarian 
ideas of Rousseau and the French Revolution, and, in contrast, 
the sympathy they shewed with English institutions. At a time 
when educated Sicilians were taking greater notice of local 
economic problems, it was natural enough that, comparing the 
backward condition of their country with England’s, they should 

<<<PDFPAGE 21>>>
INTRODUCTION 
S 
ascribe England’s wealth to her political constitution, and should 
see in that constitution a possible panacea for Sicilian ills. 
“Were we happy enough’, a Sicilian nobleman told the English traveller 
Brydone as early as 1770, ‘to enjoy the blessings of your constitution, you 
might call us rich indeed, many hidden doors of opulence would then be 
opened which now are not even thought of, and we should soon re- 
assume Our ancient name and consequence; but at present we are nothing.”* 
English institutions took on an added prestige in Sicilian eyes 
from the common origin of both nations in the Norman 
Conquest. The example of the English Constitution could there- 
fore be quoted in defence of Sicilian liberties; it seemed an 
attractive model to those who wished to mend rather than end 
their ancient institutions.* 
But Sicilian particularism took yet another form, and this the 
most deep-rooted, most widespread, and most lasting: resent- 
ment at seeing the country ruled in the interests of Naples, by 
Neapolitans who were too familiar to be foreigners, too foreign 
to be compatriots.* This resentment, fed by centuries of past 
rivalry, was re-awakened by Caracciolo’s attacks on local insti- 
tutions; it could only be exacerbated by the presence in Sicily, 
in 1799 and again from 1806 onwards, of a Court bent on 
recovering Naples and seemingly indifferent to Sicilian concerns, 
and of a Neapolitan Ministry hostile to Sicilian privileges. The 
revenues of the island were being used ‘to recover a kingdom 
which, once reunited to the Crown, would again reduce their 
own country to the harsh condition of a province’; and to 
support a flock of ‘foreign’ pensioners and officials. Desire for 
independence and an end to Neapolitan meddling was the one 
feeling that could be counted on to unite the Sicilians; it was 
shared even by staunch conservatives, devoted to the monarchy.” 
This local jealousy contributed more to the growth of the patri- 
otic movement than some Italian historians—with the later 
unification of Italy in mind—have been willing to allow. 
Thus the French Revolution and the ensuing wars widened 
and intensified the conflict between Crown and Parliament. 
True, the Revolution at first frightened the Court into calling 
a halt to the attempt to make Sicily a modern absolutist State; 
and the loss of Naples at the end of 1798 forced it once again 

<<<PDFPAGE 22>>>
6 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
to give in temporarily on the recurrent matter of the distribution 
of taxation, which had looked like bringing it into conflict with 
the Parliament of that year.* But the times fed the spirit of 
‘speculation and independence’ which Caracciolo’s reforms had 
aroused; and the presence, after 1805, of both the Court and the 
British helped to bring together all the causes for dissatisfaction 
and gropings towards reform already described, until in the end 
what had begun as a baronial protest against the loss of privileges 
took on more and more the appearance of a national movement 
of revolt. 
So much for the outline of a growing conflict. But when we 
come to look beyond outlines, classes, or abstract forces for the 
motives of individual men who took part in it, we find some 
curious blanks. The fact is that (for reasons that have less to do 
with Sicily’s backwardness at the time than with its backward- 
ness since) we are worse informed about the leading Sicilians 
of a century and a half ago than we are about many ancient or 
medieval characters. Most of them are names to which we can 
tack a few anecdotes. But even what we know shows clearly 
enough how hard it would be to fit these men into a convenient 
mould. Among the ‘patriots’ motives and actions were as various 
as the influences that set the movement going, and often no less 
contradictory. 
Take, for instance, the Prince of Cassaro: A leader of baronial 
resistance in the 1798 Parliament; later reconciled with the 
Court; re-emerging to head the patriots in the 1810 Parliament; 
following afterwards a line of conduct equally ambiguous and 
variable—this enigmatic figure seems nevertheless to have acted 
from some consistent principle besides mere opportunism: one 
may perhaps see in him a man attached to the old aristocratic 
constitution and equally opposed to royal interference and to 
liberal reform.* No such consistency, however, can be found in 
his sometime henchman the Prince of Aci, who throughout 
his various and violent shifts of allegiance seems to have been 
little more than a hothead.* As for the two most prominent 
patriotic barons, the Prince of Belmonte and his uncle the Prince 
of Castelnuovo, character and convictions differentiated and, in 
the end, divided them. 
Belmonte, rich, handsome, witty, a man of parts as well as a 

<<<PDFPAGE 23>>>
INTRODUCTION 
We 
grand seigneur, had made a grand tour through England and the 
Continent, and had been one of the first Sicilian noblemen to 
mix on equal terms with men of learning. Although ‘his worst 
enemies dared not deny him eloquence, dignity, courage, liber- 
ality and other heroic virtues’, even his hagiographer had to 
admit his unbounded vanity and occasional lack of judgement.” 
Castelnuovo, like Belmonte, had travelled and read widely: but 
while Belmonte was for a time on intimate terms with the Kin 
and Queen, and remained a fast friend of Louis-Philippe, Duke 
of Orleans, Castelnuovo led an austere and retired life, shunning 
the Court, which, indeed, thought him inclined to democratic 
ideas; while Belmonte had the confidence and the gifts of an 
active politician and party leader, and seems to have been some- 
what shallow and volatile where issues of public policy were 
concerned, Castelnuovo was rather the political theorist, the 
conscientious administrator, the dispassionate and often despond- 
ent observer, little suited for the rough-and-tumble of everyday 
politics. His friend Balsamo, though he praised ‘the uprightness 
and ready perspicacity of his mind, the firmness of his character, 
his delicate sense of honour, the purity of his morals and his 
disinterested devotion to public affairs’, nevertheless had to 
admit more than once that Castelnuovo was liable in moments 
of crisis to throw in his hand, with deplorable results, rather 
than compromise his Roman firmness in the cause he held right.* 
In both Belmonte and Castelnuovo an aristocratic, authoritarian 
streak came out often enough: but if Belmonte may be com- 
pared to a great Whig nobleman, Castelnuovo’s convictions 
sprang rather from an idealisation of Republican Rome and 
America; and his concern for equity led him into policies which 
could only undermine the existing aristocratic system. 
These men, and their no less varicoloured friends, the British 
representatives came to know very well. But in the early years 
of the occupation they were more familiar with disunity and 
inconsistency at Court. Here too several persons or cliques 
meddled in affairs of State, often at cross purposes—the Queen 
and her friends; the King when he took the trouble; the King’s 
favourite Ascoli; the Ministers. Jealousy and intrigue had endless 
play: it is not surprising to hear that the Sicilian Ambassador 
in London, Castelcicala, once received three conflicting instruc- 

<<<PDFPAGE 24>>>
8 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
tions on the same subject; let one person instruct him, he begged, 
else ‘que peut faire votre dévoué serviteur 
?” 
Ferdinand, or ‘Nasone’, with his great gawky body, his 
plebeian manners, his superstitious, diffident mind and his bursts 
of Neapolitan bonhomie—here was (the British representatives 
who dealt with him agreed) a man who might be trusted to act 
sensibly and loyally if only he was not bedevilled by intrigue. 
And if he took no more than a momentary interest in affairs, 
that was mere weakness and indolence. Certainly the King 
preferred to lead a simple life at one of his country villas, hunting 
or tunny-fishing. But his ‘indolence’ was rather a propensity 
for leaving others to do the dirty work of government and take 
the consequences. He always tried not to commit himself on 
important issues, and if he had to, he preferred to do so through 
members of his family or through subordinates who might 
later be repudiated. He could on occasion prove a master of 
delaying tactics in defence of his prerogative; and when he did 
take a hand in affairs he shewed a good deal of native shrewdness 
and common sense.” 
His wife, Maria Carolina, then in her fifties, had given birth 
to seventeen children, and the vicissitudes of her life had affected 
her health and mind, never very stable at the best of times. 
Bentinck was not far wrong in his impression ‘that with ex- 
ceeding good abilities, she probably never had any common 
sense and that her mind enfeebled by age, by vast quantities of 
opium and the operation of violent passions has reached a state 
little short of insanity’. She talked volubly and endlessly; she 
poured out her lamentations in innumerable letters whose 
breakneck style outran spelling, punctuation, syntax, and often 
sense. Occasionally she struck the dignified attitude of the 
daughter of Maria Theresa; but she generally lived at an extreme 
pitch of exultation or—more often—of despair; she saw treach- 
ery on every side; she would, she knew, perish at the block like 
her sister Marie-Antoinette. And since all opposition seemed to 
her disloyal, she surrounded herself with compliant French 
émigrés, Neapolitan adventurers, spies and dubious characters.* 
Through them she directed a traffic of intrigue and espionage 
which cost much but produced little; and as she lavished money 
on this and on charity she was perpetually insolvent. Her one 

<<<PDFPAGE 25>>>
INTRODUCTION 
9 
great desire was to recover Naples; she hated the Sicilians and 
they her.* Very likely the Queen had less to do with bringing 
about the critical state of affairs than her contemporaries be- 
lieved; but she herself, by appearing to do so much, gave them 
every reason to believe it.* 
Between these two poles of Court life swung the several 
members of the Royal Family. Somewhere about the middle 
stood the Hereditary Prince (later King Francis I), whose wari- 
ness and submission kept him as inconspicuous before Bentinck’s 
arrival as they were to make him unhappy afterwards; while 
a curious outrider was Louis-Philippe, Duke of Orleans, who, 
though Ferdinand’s son-in-law, was still a little suspect for his 
antecedents. But he shewed signs of what he was to become 
when interest and conviction led him to side with the British 
and with his friend Belmonte. As for the man the British saw 
most of, the Marquis of Circello (Foreign Secretary from 1806 
to 1812), he had to put up with remonstrances against measures 
he knew little about and had had no share in concerting. An 
elderly, affable Neapolitan courtier of the old school, he com- 
plained only on occasion of ‘all the tracasseries which really make 
my life very hard’.* Neither he nor the British agents had a happy 
time of it during the years of attrition between Great Power 
and small Power. Maria Carolina, indeed, was not the only Red 
Queen in Palermo. 
II 
The story of Anglo-Sicilian relations from 1806 onwards opens 
with a bang and goes on for five years as an extended whimper. 
It was after the failure of the 1806 peace talks with France that 
the Ministry of All the Talents decided to intervene. At first 
they tried to exhort without threatening; but very soon they 
decided on measures which, had they been enforced, would have 
brought about then what did not in fact take place until 1812. 
These measures would have turned Sicily into a virtual British 
protectorate in peace as well as war. Howick, then Foreign 
Secretary, proposed to remove the Queen’s Franco-Neapolitan 
faction from the Government, replace them by Sicilians, and 
put the Sicilian army under the British Commander-in-Chief. 

<<<PDFPAGE 26>>>
Io 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
The Court’s improvidence and ambiguity, Howick thought, 
had already lost Naples; if they were allowed to go on as before 
they might lose Sicily as well. But he and Grenville, it seems, 
also wanted to get value for money during the war and to secure 
Sicily as a useful naval and trading station in peace-time. What 
all this might have led to we cannot tell: the necessary instruc- 
tions were drawn up, but the Talents fell just in time to stop 
them from being sent out.” 
Canning, when he came to deal with Sicily, made no demands 
on the Court beyond vague ones for their confidence and co- 
operation. Later on he did insist—as Howick had—that Britain 
could not guarantee the return of Naples at a peace, though she 
would do her best. He also asked that the Sicilian army should 
come under the British Commander if the island was attacked; 
but he seems not to have been particularly worried about this, 
for the question still stood unresolved when he left office.” 
Otherwise Canning’s Sicilian policy, so far as he had one, was 
conciliatory; he wished to set at rest any suspicion the Court 
might entertain of England’s abandoning Sicily or meddling in 
her internal affairs. So far, indeed, was Canning from thinking 
of the British subsidy as a possible means of coercion that when 
a new treaty raised it to £400,000 a year he removed its payment 
from the hands of the British Minister at Palermo to those of 
the Sicilian Ambassador in London—and this on no more urgent 
grounds than that whoever paid it out bore the loss on the 
exchange.* 
The fact was that the strategic situation had changed. In 1806, 
after Maida, one could still think of using Sicily as a springboard 
for an invasion of Italy. In 1807, after the Peace of Tilsit, that 
hope was, for the time being, as good as lost. Sicily was now of 
little positive importance. The British Government accordingly 
treated the garrison as a reserve which might be drawn upon 
for service elsewhere, and whose task was the merely negative 
one of holding the place.* 
As for Howick’s envoy to Palermo, William Drummond, 
he had approved and even urged ‘measures which would have in 
fact rendered Sicily a province of Great Britain’, and had at 
first gone so far as to suspend the subsidy. But he changed his 
mind even before he knew of Howick’s fall. Drummond had 

<<<PDFPAGE 27>>>
INTRODUCTION 
II 
lost his early hopes of the Sicilians’ fitness to regenerate their 
country under British tutelage; but he came to believe that he 
could by his own influence neutralise the Queen and lead the 
King along the path of reform and cooperation. He therefore 
resumed payment of the subsidy, and took up a conciliatory 
attitude which, on the whole, he maintained until his recall in 
1808, despite recurrent friction and disappointment.* 
Occasions for mutual ill-feeling were not wanting. The Court, 
the Queen especially, wanted above all to recover Naples: not 
only would the British Government not guarantee Naples, but 
their generals had year after year to put off, for one reason or 
another, the expedition on which the Court pinned their hopes. 
And when an expedition was at last attempted in 1809, its failure 
coincided with the disastrous defeat of Austria. The Court were 
plunged into deeper dismay than ever. They could not be sure 
that England would win the war; and if she did, they might 
still be left stranded in Sicily at a peace, or worse, be deprived 
even of that. The Queen remembered with horror how close 
they had come to such a fate during the 1806 peace negotiations.” 
Perhaps, too, she knew something of the drastic measures 
Howick had planned: at any rate Lord Amherst, Drummond’s 
successor, found it difficult to remove from her mind ‘an im- 
pression to which she willingly recurs that it is still the intention 
of the British to render themselves masters of Sicily’.* She filled 
her letters with complaints of the English who ‘overpowered, 
humiliated, ill-treated’ her, and their least request seemed to her 
the beginning of subversion.* 
The British representatives had no less cause for anxiety. 
Invasion was always a possibility. The British forces were often 
depleted, and the subsidy seemed to bear no fruit whatever: 
Drummond had hoped to see the Sicilian army put into fighting 
shape, but three years later Amherst found it still ‘weak, ill- 
provided... dissatisfied’, and he urged that since the Court 
refused to grant a united command, the British Commander 
should at least be given control of the subsidy.” The Queen’s 
influence seemed the root of the trouble. She could not be kept out 
of affairs, as Drummond found after a premature announcement 
that she and her faction ‘ruled no longer, either in appearance 
or in reality’.* But the worst was not her meddling and suspicion. 

<<<PDFPAGE 28>>>
I2 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
From time to time alarming reports came in of the Queen’s 
intrigues with one or other of the Powers: Russia, or Austria, 
or even France or Murat. Her object was always the same: to 
restore her family to Naples by some arrangement, if necessary 
by the surrender of Sicily. These reports, though convincing, 
did not amount to proof; they grew still more alarming after 
the Austro-French 
alliance, for was not Marie-Louise 
the 
Queen’s granddaughter? But protests only drew counter-protes- 
tations of loyalty from the King and Circello, and furious re- 
criminations from the Queen.” 
Drummond had thought the Queen a mere dupe of the 
French faction, and Amherst would not at first believe the re- 
ports. But the military had no such scruples. As early as 1807 
Sir John Moore—then second-in-command—had urged that the 
Queen should be expelled and Sicily annexed to the British 
Empire. Even Sir John Stuart (commander 1808-1811), though 
at first more favourably disposed towards the Court, eventually 
lost his temper and blurted out in public: ‘as for the King, his 
cause is desperate; he has lost one kingdom, and he must lose 
the other. There is no alternative for England but to take Sicily 
for herself, or to abandon it altogether.” To Amherst he wrote 
that “we may (as eventually we must do) dictate to the Govern- 
ment its measures as well as its policy’. The staff officers at the 
Messina headquarters, long convinced of the Court’s treachery, 
derided Amherst and Stuart as fatuously blind to the perils that 
threatened them, and clamoured for drastic action.* 
This state of anxiety and frustration might perhaps have gone 
on indefinitely, had present danger not broken in on it. In May 
1810 Murat came down to the Straits of Messina to attempt an 
invasion. But not only did the Court profess a ‘strange incre- 
dulity’ as to the danger; not only did they persist in refusing 
the united command: they failed, for all Amherst’s remonstrances 
throughout the summer, to provide a single soldier for the 
defence of the Straits. Murat’s landing, when it came in Sep- 
tember, was repulsed by British soldiers and by Sicilian peasants 
armed with rifles, axes, and sticks.* Only with the greatest 
difficulty did Amherst induce the Court to make even a verbal 
stand, and to deny Murat’s published assertion that they awaited 
him as a liberator. Fresh evidence of the Queen’s traffic with 

<<<PDFPAGE 29>>>
INTRODUCTION 
13 
Naples came tumbling in to confirm the general belief that the 
Court were in league with Murat. Even the cautious Amherst 
became at length convinced that ‘jealousy of the designs of 
Great Britain predominates in the Queen’s mind over the hatred 
she may entertain for Bonaparte’: it was high time, he declared, 
‘to put an end to a system of duplicity and intrigue the existence 
of which I have been almost the last person in Sicily to believe’. 
He too now preached the need for interference. Britain, he said, 
could no longer afford to leave her troops open to defection in 
their rear: if the Queen continued to ‘separate her interests from 
England’, then England had better “direct the concerns of those 
who are themselves unequal to the task, and... administer by 
force the remedies on which the salvation of this kingdom will 
ultimately depend.”* 
Amherst had another ground for this opinion: the Sicilian 
people, he thought, detested their government to the point of 
welcoming foreign interference; if the English did not help 
them they might turn to the French. How much stronger, then, 
was the contrast that summer between the Court, who had 
‘trifled and temporised, and sought to palliate ambiguous con- 
duct by plausible but unsatisfactory explanation’, and the people, 
who had ‘shewn themselves boldly and openly the foes of 
France and the friends of England’. Their feeling for national 
independence seemed to provide the only alliance England could 
count on. One finds the official British paper appealing to it as 
the surest means of rallying the population: Murat’s design on 
Sicily, it said, was ‘to make her an integral part of the Kingdom of 
Naples; as they have destroyed law and religion, so they wish 
to do away with history as well.’ Sicily’s independence had been 
recognised by every King since Roger, but ‘Signor Murat now 
wishes .. 
. to humiliate the good Sicilians, and strip them of all 
the rights and prerogatives they have ever enjoyed’. So when 
the ‘patriots’ appealed to Amherst for help in defending these 
same rights, he was much more inclined to listen to them than 
before. 
What had happened was that a political crisis coincided with 
the military emergency of 1810; and that the conflict between 
Crown and Parliament now shifted decisively from merely 
fiscal to political and institutional ground. But taxation remained 

<<<PDFPAGE 30>>>
I4 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the first matter for dispute. The Court,were badly short of money 
when Parliament met for its regular session in January 1810.” 
The Speech from the Throne—whether as a sop to constitutional 
sentiment, or a warning against insubordination—exhorted 
members to safeguard from the democratic peril their Consti- 
tution and privileges, ‘institutions lost by other nations for lack 
of public spirit and national energy, and preserved only in the 
two most famous islands in the world, Great Britain and Sicily’.* 
The Government let it be known that they meant to obtain not 
only an extraordinary subsidy (donativo) of 360,000 ounces a 
year (about £ 235,000), but also a fairer distribution of taxation. 
The Barons, alarmed, and led by Belmonte and Cassaro, countered 
by voting to pay a larger share of a smaller sum. On the one 
hand they cut the donativo down to 150,000 oz.; on the other 
they adopted a plan for tax reform far more radical than the 
Government had intended. This plan, drawn up by Balsamo, 
swept away the ‘monstrous multiplicity’ of the old donativi and 
the cumbersome machinery for their collection; instead it estab- 
lished a single tax on every kind of real property, to be assessed 
according to a new survey. Thus ‘every Sicilian’, said Balsamo, 
‘was according to strict equity to pay the Exchequer in pro- 
portion to what he owned and consumed’; and the barons could 
no longer be accused of merely defending their own privileges. 
When the Ecclesiastico followed suit it came about that Parlia- 
ment, by a curious reversal, passed in 1810, against the Court’s 
wishes, the very reforms it had rejected when Caracciolo pro- 
posed them in 1783.* 
But after taking this first step Parliament was still unready to 
go any further. Belmonte, Castelnuovo, and Balsamo tried at 
the end of the session to carry several political resolutions: the 
most important of these would have made supply run for one 
year only, thus establishing an annual Parliament. But most of 
the resolutions failed.* This helps to explain why Belmonte and 
his friends then shifted their ground and started negotiating 
with the Court. 
These negotiations went on for several months after Parlia- 
ment had risen. They were complex and obscure. Belmonte’s 
friends, it seems clear, feared that the Court might take to 
unconstitutional measures if it was left badly short of money. 

<<<PDFPAGE 31>>>
INTRODUCTION 
I$ 
With Orleans’ support they approached the Queen and pro- 
posed a bargain. The Court was to dismiss the Neapolitan 
Ministers and put in Sicilians—among them, apparently, Bel- 
monte and Cassaro. Orleans was to command the army. Parlia- 
ment would meet again and effect a certain amount of consti- 
tutional reform; for their part, Belmonte’s friends would make 
sure that enough additional supply was passed to meet the 
Government’s needs. The Queen hesitated and toyed for some 
months with this proposal.* When, however, Parliament did 
meet for a second session in August, the Court—it is not clear 
from what motives—chose to secure the political defeat of 
Belmonte’s party by sacrificing the hope of increased supply. 
That is to say, they bought off Cassaro and a majority of the 
Barons—first with a reshuffle which made Cassaro a Councillor 
and two Sicilian nonentities Ministers; then with an undertaking 
that no more donativi would be asked for. Belmonte and his 
friends thus found themselves left out of the Government and 
outvoted in Parliament: oddly enough, they voted for more 
taxes than the Government majority.” 
At this point Belmonte’s party secretly appealed to Amherst 
for his and England’s help in getting concessions from the 
Crown. The old Sicilian Constitution, they told him, was in 
theory enough to maintain Sicilian liberties; but in practice it 
was constantly violated. 
What was needed was a reform of the Constitution inspired 
by that of England, but not copied from it; a genuine Sicilian 
administration; and the control of the Army by the British 
Commander. Parliamentary demands, they foresaw, would 
meet with no success. If England did not intervene, Sicily might 
yet rebel and call in the French. 
Ambherst told them his instructions did not allow him to do 
anything of the kind; he dissuaded them from taking action. 
But—as he wrote to Wellesley privately—he agreed with them 
entirely. The Court’s behaviour during the invasion scare made 
intervention of some kind necessary. If that intervention were 
not only to remove the Queen’s influence but to establish a 
native administration and a free Parliament, with a Sicilian 
Constitution remodelled along English lines, then one could 
expect as ‘natural consequences’ such reforms as would give 

<<<PDFPAGE 32>>>
16 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the Sicilians ‘the comforts and the blessings intended for them 
by nature, and of which nothing but a faulty and ill-adminis- 
tered government deprives them’. True, the government was 
not alone to blame: the Barons would have to give up some 
vexatious privileges; but he believed the patriots knew this. 
The King and Queen would not easily adapt themselves to free 
institutions, but a British Minister backed by the authority of 
his Government might induce them to do so: 
‘I think that if the language of earnest and friendly representation 
offered in respectful terms, and urged for His Sicilian Majesty’s benefit 
should fail of effect, the blind and interested counsels of certain weak 
and unprincipled advisers should not be suffered to stand in the way of 
the universal wishes of the country... I am aware, my Lord, how 
repugnant it is to the principles and practice of the British Government 
to interfere in the concerns of an independent sovereign; but if His 
Sicilian Majesty is to be preserved on his throne, he must be induced to 
listen to the admonitory voice of his disinterested ally.’ 
There might be advantages for England too in sucha reform: 
for the nation was ripe for resistance, and 
‘to become a province of England would not, I believe, be looked upon 
as a misfortune by the major part of its inhabitants. But an independent 
Government and a free Constitution would be considered as a boon, the 
attainment of which would irrevocably bind the Sicilians to the nation 
which should procure it for them.”* 
Amherst, however, was not to carry out the intervention he 
recommended: both he and Stuart resigned their posts in disgust.* 
For nothing ever seemed to go right after the summer of 1810. 
Even a fairly simple matter—a dispute over a ship under British 
licence which Sicilian privateers has seized—now took on a 
suspicious tinge and led to a string of acrimonious Notes.” 
But these diplomatic tensions mattered little amid the political 
storm that blew up in February 1811. The Court had won a 
hollow victory in Parliament, since it had cost them the chance 
of adequate supply. They could get no more out of the British.* 
At a loss for money, the Government in February 1811 issued 
three decrees imposing, by the King’s authority alone, a tax of 
1 % on all money payments whatever, a sale of Church and 

<<<PDFPAGE 33>>>
INTRODUCTION 
ny, 
common lands, and a lottery. There was little doubt that this 
was an unconstitutional act.* The Decrees at once rallied and 
strengthened the patriotic party, especially among the Barons, 
disunited since the last Parliament. Belmonte’s house became 
the headquarters of a group including not only the reformers 
but also a number of conservative Barons: Orleans gave them 
Open support. On March Ist a majority of the Peers then in 
Palermo addressed a petition to the delegate body which was 
supposed to watch over the enforcement of laws between 
Parliamentary sessions—the Deputation of the Realm. They 
asked the Deputation to remind the Crown of the ancient and 
unquestioned privileges of Parliament; protested against the 
Decrees; and asserted, nevertheless, their willingness to provide 
all necessary supply by constitutional means.* The Deputation 
dodged the petition for some weeks by failing to meet. But 
delay only increased the political tension. Amherst, about to 
leave, feared ‘the most fatal consequences’. The Decrees, he 
foresaw, would not solve the Court’s financial problems, while 
‘general discontent’ would certainly be produced, and ‘ultimate 
resistance’ might perhaps be expected.* 
It was at this point that the Foreign Secretary, Lord Wellesley, 
offered the mission to Lord William Bentinck. 

<<<PDFPAGE 34>>>

OE

BRITAIN INTERVENES 
‘A brilliant and unbalanced egoist,’ writes Sir Charles Webster, 
‘all the more dangerous because he was also imbued with a 
species of idealism’; but then the second son of the Duke of 
Portland ‘could not be denied a high military or diplomatic 
post’. That is one view, though an authoritative one; there are 
others. Was the Cabinet so careless, or its motive so irrelevant, 
when it chose Major-General Lord William Bentinck to fill at 
once the Palermo mission and the Mediterranean command? 
At 36 Bentinck had experienced a good deal of responsibility 
as a soldier and administrator; and he had already been in 
trouble. His Governorship of Madras (1803-1807) had ended 
in his recall amid bitter controversy; in earlier years (1799-1801) 
he had served as a young officer in Northern Italy and had learnt 
something of the country; earlier still he had fought in the 
Netherlands. More recently, at the start of the Peninsular War, 
he had carried out a mission to the Spanish Junta and held a 
command under Moore in the retreat to Corunna. If what the 
Government wanted was an orthodox diplomatist he was not 
their man. But if, as now seems clear, they had made up their 
mind to intervene in the domestic concerns of Sicily and needed 
a proconsul for the purpose, then their choice seems less arbi- 
trary.” 
It was indeed an arduous task. In Sicily Bentinck was to be at 
once diplomatist, politician, even for a time dictator, besides 
commanding two separate armies, one of them scattered as far 
abroad as Lissa, the Ionian Islands, and Catalonia. We have a 
picture of him at Palermo, immersed in a roomful of plans and 
documents and waving aside as so much waste paper the letters 
of recommendation brought by a young newcomer from Eng- 
land—let him work hard for promotion, he said bluntly.* We 
hear, too, from another who, just arrived from occupied Italy, 
found Bentinck a shrewd questioner: ‘he sucked me so dry that 
I came home in the shape and likeness of'a squeezed lemon. . .”.* 
Neither a careful subordinate nor a place-hunter of the kind 
that made up his father’s and other “connexions’, Bentinck had 

<<<PDFPAGE 35>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
I9Q 
about him something of the energy and forthrightness of the 
Victorian public servant. However one may judge what he did, 
he did it thoroughly and, in the circumstances, efficiently. A 
soldier, he shewed a rough but undeniable skill in the tactics of 
political management: where immoderate sentiments prevailed, 
his was often the moderating influence. Just as at Corunna, seated 
on his “quiet mule’, he had talked to Charles Napier through 
the heavy gunfire ‘as if we were going to breakfast... with 
perhaps an increase of good-humour and placidity’—‘this chap 
takes it coolly or the devil’s in it’, Napier exclaimed—so now in 
Sicily he often got his way by cool persistent bullying, stopping 
short of the limit beyond which he would have lost control.* 
Thus far the public man in Bentinck—and the public man is 
nearly all we can see of him. This figure, as it appears in official 
papers, is almost extravagantly simple. We see a man who must 
be doing; to whom le mieux est P ennemi du bien meant little; who 
had such an appetite for responsibility that, while he could 
rise to situations that would have tried most men hard, he in the 
end took too much upon himself and caused his own downfall. 
Through his eyes, again, we see men and events in black and 
white: character sharply defined, right and wrong sharply di- 
vided. Yet this is not the whole truth. There is a private Bentinck, 
who sometimes disconcerts us by confessing to ‘a great deal of 
irritable shyness’-—such a great deal that to have to wear the 
K.C.B. newly awarded him strikes him as ‘the infliction of a 
positive misery’.* There is a Bentinck who, far from being the 
doctrinaire Whig one would expect, shews in his political 
dealings a variety of moods—empirical, diffident, even des- 
pondent—which he none the less banishes from his official 
despatches. Altogether there seems to have been in him a divorce 
between ‘official’ beliefs and private feelings—feelings of which 
he was perhaps not quite aware, or which he hesitated to define 
clearly even to himself. It is a human condition, and among some 
men a peculiarly English condition. Bentinck cannot be charged 
with duplicity towards others. But because he wanted to see his 
world steadily he failed to see it whole: he deceived, if anyone, 
himself* 
This, then, was the man the Foreign Secretary had chosen. 
But exactly how did Wellesley judge the state of affairs in Sicily, 

<<<PDFPAGE 36>>>
20 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
and what kind of intervention had hein mind? It is not easy to 
tell. Until then he had taken virtually no interest in Sicily. Even 
now he apparently set little store by the reports of the Queen’s 
‘treachery’. Though he later claimed more general motives he 
may have been moved by a simple fact: the resignation of 
Ambherst and Stuart. Both men had from the start, most ex- 
ceptionally, done their best to placate the Sicilian Court; now 
that Court’s behaviour in the face of the threat from Murat had 
led both of them to throw up their posts in disgust. After that 
any Foreign Secretary must have found it difficult merely to go 
on with a policy of hopeful conciliation, or to avoid reaching 
some kind of decision.” 
But whatever his motives Wellesley, it seems clear, acted to 
meet a specific military and diplomatic crisis: he took no more 
notice than Canning had ever done of the demands for inter- 
vention on general grounds which some people in England had 
been putting forward for years. The Opposition regularly used 
the annual Commons debate on the subsidy to press for such a 
change of policy: the defence of the island, they said, would be 
surer and easier if its people could be relieved of an oppressive 
system of government; some of the London newspapers echoed 
them, one or two going so far as to contemplate the annexation 
of Sicily to the British Empire.* More important than these 
casual references were the writings of one or two publicists, 
particularly G.F. Leckie: with some knowledge of Sicilian con- 
ditions, he called for a wholesale reform under English tutelage: 
this was to induce the Italians to rise and unite against the French. 
At the same time Leckie looked forward to an expansion of 
British power all over the Mediterranean, such as would simul- 
taneously benefit British trade and prove to the world that 
‘while France conquers to devastate, Britain conquers to do 
good’.* Finally, the British merchants who at this time carried on 
most of Sicily’s foreign trade asked many times for redress of 
what they said were discriminatory practices by the Sicilian 
Government: but the Board of Trade turned a deaf ear to their 
petitions, and Wellesley seems to have given little thought to 
commercial advantage." 
Instead of settling from the start on a detailed policy of 
intervention, Wellesley asked the commander- and Minister- 

<<<PDFPAGE 37>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
21 
designate to submit his own proposals. Bentinck, after studying 
the diplomatic files, wrote a memorandum in which he reviewed 
at length the precarious situation of Sicily. The Court, he 
admitted, by failing to cooperate, had shewn only 
a 
negative kind 
of hostility; but so persistent had they been in this that ‘treason 
itself could scarcely have taken any other course’. Still it was not 
on the score of treachery—though it existed—that he proposed 
the British Government should act, but ‘upon the broad basis 
of mutual self-defence and welfare’. Everyone said the Sicilians 
were basically friendly to England, though disaffected to their 
own government almost to the point of revolt: their help would 
be essential if the island was ever to be safe. Britain should 
therefore demand: the united command, carrying with it control 
over the subsidy; a Sicilian administration; and a reform of 
abuses. Without a Sicilian administration the Queen’s party 
would never be curbed; as for reform, one might think it a 
‘natural consequence’ of the change of administration, “but the 
demand will please the people, and it may have a very great 
political effect in Italy, whenever ... the Italians shall be pre- 
pared to assert their independence’—for Bentinck, like Leckie, 
- already thought of Sicily as a stepping-stone towards the lib- 
eration of Italy. Should the Court reject these demands, he 
would withdraw to his camp, proclaiming his determination to 
defend the King and the country in accordance with the alliance 
and ‘in concurrence with the Sicilians’. Such a policy, Bentinck 
admitted, amounted to interference in the domestic affairs of 
Sicily, ‘almost a forcible seizure’ of the government; but it 
would be justified and disinterested, and its effect would be 
‘to place the King safely on his throne, to give happiness to an oppressed 
people, to make their island forever independent of French invasion, 
and to exalt the British character’. 
This course, he said, should prove ‘plain and easy’ if the 
British Government agreed to it; but if they still preferred a 
policy of mere representation, then he was not the man to carry 
it out.” 
Wellesley did not altogether accept Bentinck’s proposals, and 
Perceval objected to them strongly; they were, he said, neither 
justifiable nor necessary. The soldiers all claimed that Sicily could 

<<<PDFPAGE 38>>>
22 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
not be held without reform, and yet it was still held. Reform 
would in any case be a hazardous undertaking, and would have 
to deal with feudal as well as governmental abuses. The Queen’s 
‘treachery’ he discounted, commenting that her distrust of 
England was only natural after the revolution which Grey had 
planned and which the British in Sicily had freely canvassed 
ever since. Eventually, after some discussion, Bentinck yielded 
to Wellesley’s arguments, and to those of his friend Sir Edward 
Pellew (who was going out to the naval command in the 
Mediterranean): he agreed to carry out what instructions the 
government thought fit to give him.” 
These instructions, when they were drawn up, embodied an 
uneasy compromise between Bentinck’s view and Perceval’s. 
Bentinck was to represent that ‘the evident tendency of the 
measures now pursued being not only to destroy the resources 
of the island, but to exasperate the people to acts of violence 
against their Government’, Britain must warn the Court of the 
dangers to which they exposed themselves and their allies. If 
on the one hand, the Court treated the island as they ought—if 
they granted the Sicilians a greater share in the government, 
attended to the wishes of Parliament, and remedied abuses—then 
the British Government would do all in its power to maintain 
them in Sicily and to bring about their restoration to Naples. 
But if on the other hand they persisted in their present course, 
‘neither the terms nor the spirit of the alliance would require that the 
British arms should be employed to assist . 
. 
. in enforcing such a system 
of government against the resistance of the people of Sicily’. 
It was ‘not however inconsistent’ with these warnings to 
assert that not for a moment did the British Government mean 
to interfere in the domestic affairs of Sicily, or to change or 
upset the Constitution. Bentinck was not allowed to go beyond 
discreet representation: he might threaten to recommend the 
suspension of the subsidy if it seemed ill-spent; but the decision 
would rest with London. Wellesley’s instructions were like 
Howick’s of 1806—those which he gave Drummond before the 
Talents chose to intervene in full strength—in that they coupled 
unwelcome advice with professions of delicacy, and provided 
no means of enforcing it.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 39>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
23 
So closely, indeed, did Wellesley’s policy during the next 
few months follow Howick’s in its change from good advice to 
the threat of force that the British intervention in 1811-12 
reminds one of a theatrical performance, once rehearsed, then 
put off and given only after a lapse of five years. But those five 
years of mounting tension within Sicily and between the two 
allies made the final clash look inevitable, as it could scarcely 
have done in 1807. 
Bentinck landed at Palermo in July 1811 to find the Court 
in no mood to listen to advice. The Queen, apparently having 
got wind of the British Government’s intentions, had decided 
that only the most unbending resistance could foil what she 
saw as a plot to annex Sicily. For two months past relations 
between the Court and the British Legation had been poisoned 
by a matter trivial in itself—yet another dispute over the re- 
cognition of British shipping licences—but inflated out of all 
proportions in the tension of the moment. At the same time 
the Court had dealt with the Barons’ protest against the Febru- 
ary Decrees: they had got the Deputation to say that the decrees 
were legal, and the protest invalid; and three days before 
Bentinck’s arrival, five of the protesting Barons—Belmonte, 
Castelnuovo, Aci, Anjou and Villafranca—had suddenly been 
arrested and imprisoned on various islands off the coast of Sicily.* 
Bentinck, though he had no hope of achieving anything by 
persuasion in such a state of affairs, followed his instructions to 
the letter. Circello’s reply to his first representations was simple: 
if the British Government said they did not wish to interfere, 
then why did they in fact interfere? Besides, they must have 
been misled by malicious gossip, for Sicily was perfectly con- 
tented: so there was no case to answer. Bentinck could make 
no headway against a refusal even to discuss the situation. For 
this obduracy he blamed the Queen: her mania for Naples and 
distrust of England would wreck any chance of an improvement 
until she was removed from the government. “The Court’, he 
declared, ‘must be frightened into their duty to themselves and 
to us. Otherwise we shall all be sacrificed in the ruins of one of 
the best people upon earth’—by which he may have meant the 
Sicilians, but more likely the Italians, who might be freed if 
Sicily was once set in order. Meanwhile all he could do was 

<<<PDFPAGE 40>>>
24 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
to return home for fresh instructions. This he did, after lecturing 
Circello on the great chances thrown away by the Court's 
improvidence: had they respected the liberties of the people 
and of Parliament and won their support, Sicily would have 
been an inspiration to the Continent of Italy, and an army might 
have been spared for service there: 
‘Those times are past when personal attachment to a chief or a family 
was the greatest incentive to human action. At this time the world is too 
enlightened not to have discovered that the only security for individual 
liberty or national greatness is a free Constitution where the interests of 
the Sovereign and his People are happily blended and identified.”* 
The chance that Italy might rise against the French and win 
her independence with English help was another reason for 
returning to London. In June, Wellesley and Bentinck had 
discussed a proposal lately received from the Archduke Francis 
of Este—then in Sardinia—for the formation in the Mediter- 
ranean of a force made up of Italians and of Austrian officers 
unemployed since the Austro-French peace treaty. Its intended 
purpose was to raise the standard of revolt in Italy or in the 
conquered Austrian provinces, and eventually to establish an 
Italian Kingdom under the Archduke, at least as large as 
that founded by Napoleon. Bentinck had supported this plan 
warmly: Italy, he said, was thoroughly discontented with French 
rule and might prove another Spain; the country offered not 
only a long coastline accessible to British seapower, but also 
‘a people equally brave with the Spaniards, and equally en- 
thusiastic in the cause of liberty, but much more intelligent and 
more tractable’. All Wellesley had given him was a vague 
authorisation to look into the matter: the Government, taken 
up as they were with the Peninsula, could spare little attention 
for Italy, and Bentinck had had no illusions on the point.* Now, 
however, he had met the Archduke, and intelligence from the 
Continent confirmed his enthusiasm for the plan. On his return 
to London he pressed for, and got, an authorisation to promote 
it actively: he might form an Italo-Austrian force and make 
ready for a possible rising in Italy by getting in touch with the 
patriotic party there; for these purposes he might spend up to 
£100,000. At the same time it was ‘not the intention... to 

<<<PDFPAGE 41>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
25 
excite the people of Italy to any exertions which they may not 
think necessary’ or to act before the time was ripe: any plan for 
an expedition must be concerted with Wellington and other 
British agents in the Mediterranean.* 
To Bentinck’s mind intervention in Sicily was clearly con- 
nected with this plan for the liberation of Italy: the one must 
precede the other. Whether it was the same view that led Welles- 
ley to agree to stronger measures in Sicily is not clear.* At any 
rate he allowed Bentinck to press not only for the changes 
already asked for, but also for the united command, the recall of 
the imprisoned barons, and the removal from the government 
of persons hostile to the alliance. Bentinck was given great 
freedom of action: the only real limitation to his powers was 
that he must not resort to force so long as he had no proof of 
the Court’s treachery—but if such proof should come to hand 
it would justify ‘the most decided measures’. Meanwhile he 
could control and suspend the subsidy on his own authority; 
beyond this, the utmost retaliation to which the British Govern- 
ment would agree if the Court rejected their demands was the 
withdrawal of the garrison.* 
Bentinck now sailed again to Sicily, confident of success. His 
mood was not unlike that of a proconsul setting out on a puni- 
tive expedition: writing to Wellington, he compared the Court 
to a native Indian Durbar; to Wellesley he had already hinted 
that what he was now carrying out was ‘the same enlarged and 
enlightened system of policy, which has already saved another 
Empire... * He was by no means alone in his confidence or 
in his high hopes of Sicilian and Italian regeneration. He sailed, 
as it were, amid a chorus of good wishes from high-ranking 
generals, admirals and diplomatists. These men detested the 
Queen—‘the she wicked one’, they called her, or ‘the Lady’, 
or ‘the very high author of so much mischief’, or simply ‘“She’— 
and they had no doubt that she would be worsted in ‘a contest 
where treachery and deceit are pitted against virtue truth and 
honour’. Some hoped the birth of Sicilian happiness would 
‘take its date from the time of your arrival among them’. Leckie 
had sent Bentinck his books and his blessing. Amherst sent him 
suggestions for reforms, culminating in the maxim ‘Assimilate 
generally as much as possible to the British Constitution’. Others 

<<<PDFPAGE 42>>>
26 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
shared his view that Italy might soom rise, and Sicily therefore 
take on an ‘incalculable importance’. One voice alone preached 
caution, Wellington’s: he advised Bentinck merely to ‘maintain 
our position’ in Sicily; as for Italy, ‘trust nothing’, he wrote, 
‘to the enthusiasm of the people. Give them a strong and a just, 
and, if possible, a good government; but, above all, a strong one, 
which shall enforce them to do their duty by themselves and 
their country...’. Still, Bentinck might well believe, as he 
approached Palermo, what Pellew wrote to him at the time: 
‘The battle you have nobly fought, and the conquest will reward you 
amply . . 
. I think every obstacle will now fall prostrate and the Q... . be 
very happy to put herself 
in your harness. You have, or will now have, 
quite a smooth path and see your way clear.” 
Bentinck landed at Palermo on December 7 to find things 
no better than he had left them. His departure had at first startled 
the Court into a more cautious frame of mind; but they were 
unable to settle on any definite policy against his return. As 
usual the Queen and Ascoli were at odds. Ought they once 
again to try calling in the Russians? (The Queen wrote off to 
Petersburg.) Ought they to release the five Barons and so draw 
Bentinck’s teeth? (Afterwards the Queen and Ascoli each claimed 
to have advised this and to have been thwarted by the other’s 
influence.) Anyhow nothing was done.* At the same time all 
the old suspicions of treachery had flared up more strongly than 
ever during Bentinck’s absence: fresh correspondence came to 
light between the Court’s agents and the enemy, as well as 
various plots seemingly designed to bring about new Sicilian 
Vespers, this time against the British. The whole business was 
tangled and obscure, but Ascoli and, by inference, the Queen 
seemed to be involved. If the British staff and legation were 
highly alarmed, poor General Maitland, left in charge by 
Bentinck, was in despair: ‘I am quite sick of spies and devils... 
Where is Lord William and when will he come—I am groping 
in the dark ...’. Bentinck, hearing this news on his arrival, 
considered that it entitled him to take those ‘most decisive 
measures’ contemplated in his new instructions—that is, to use 
force if necessary.” 
At first, however, he attempted to bring the Court to heel 

<<<PDFPAGE 43>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
27 
by milder means. He began at once by suspending the subsidy. 
The Court were in no position to resist: if the ensuing crisis 
lasted as long as five weeks that was no doubt because they 
could not agree among themselves. The King fled at once to 
La Ficuzza, his shooting-lodge in the hills, leaving his wife and 
Circello to cope with Bentinck’s demands as best they could. 
In his fright he tried several times to abdicate: it was all the 
Queen could do to stop him. Bentinck, when consulted about 
this unexpected resolve of the King’s, advised abdication: it 
would save the King from a humiliating change of front, which 
coming from the Prince would be more like an act of grace. 
But he had to make it clear that he could not and would not 
ask for such a step.” The Queen, for her part, swung between 
despair and defiance. She knew she could not count on the King 
or on Ascoli, and she feared—rightly—that they might use her 
as a scapegoat.” At times she tried to conciliate Bentinck—she 
had always been English in feeling, ‘and now, she added smiling, 
I must be English par nécessité, vous voyez comme je vous 
parle franchement’; once she actually asked for a ship to take 
her away from Sicily. At other times she railed at him, accused 
the British of having always planned to seize Sicily, and refused 
to make the slightest concession. She sought for ways of doing 
without the subsidy; she harangued the Guards from the Palace 
window, bidding them resist the King’s enemies. They might 
use force, she cried, ‘but she would never yield, she could but 
die in the contest’.* 
The hubbub was at its height. There was talk of invasion; 
there were rumours of assassination. Some feared for Bentinck’s 
life, some for the five barons who where still in exile.* Altogether 
Bentinck’s negotiations with Circello could scarcely follow a 
coherent course. With some difficulty Bentinck won two points: 
the recall of the barons and the command of the Sicilian army. 
On the first he was willing to help the King by letting the 
barons’ recall look like a spontaneous act of grace: he wished, 
he said, to act as ‘a mediator between the violence of parties’. 
But the command was useless to him without a trustworthy 
Ministry. The Court, however, stood out longer against the 
change of administration than against any of his other demands. 
To Bentinck’s mind, the exiled Barons would make the most 

<<<PDFPAGE 44>>>
28 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
satisfactory Ministers; when Circello asked him whether he 
meant to impose them on the King, he replied that ‘those... 
who had created the embarrassment must take the Court out 
of it as well as they could... to the feelings of the King, the 
interests of the Nation were not to be sacrificed’. Still he tried 
not to be drawn into actually dictating a specific Ministry; in 
the end, after long bargaining, he and Circello reached a com- 
promise. Cassaro was to become a sort of interim Prime Minister 
(for he now appeared in the guise of a patriot and a man who 
could withstand the Queen’s influence); Circello might stay, 
while the other departments would be left to under-secretaries 
until such time as some of the exiled barons might decently 
come into them. No sooner had this arrangement been accepted 
in principle than the Court changed its mind—apparently after 
the usual tussle between the Queen and Ascoli, who was the 
more frightened of the two. A partial reshuffle was announced, 
which left the existing Ministry almost unchanged—the very 
arrangement to which Bentinck had objected most strongly in 
previous discussions. At the same time Circello stated in a Note 
that the Court could listen to no more demands. 
Bentinck now decided to use force: he ordered part of the 
British garrison in eastern Sicily to sail to Palermo. In a long 
conversation with the Hereditary Prince he recapitulated Britain’s 
grievances, and at the same time assured him of her good 
intentions: 
‘The fact of our never having assumed the possession of the island, when 
it had been in our power for six years together, and when it had been the 
general wish of the inhabitants, was the best proof of our intentions. .. 
. 
I concluded... by saying that I should use force if my representations 
were not attended to. This force was not against, but for the benefit of 
the King and his family. . 
. 
. If he himself at the head of the army should 
resist me unsuccessfully, I should, if his father still persisted in his retirement, 
be the first to place himself on the throne, and if he declined, to support 
the succession for his son. It was against the enemies of the Crown that 
my opposition would be directed. He was much struck with this statement 
and made me repeat it.’ 
In the face of this threat the Court’s resistance collapsed. On 
January 16th the King announced that ill-health compelled him 

<<<PDFPAGE 45>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
29 
to hand over power temporarily to the Prince, who was to be 
Vicar-General for Sicily and to act as his alter ego. The Prince 
at once repealed the 1° tax and recalled the barons. British 
troops garrisoned Palermo. The King stayed on at La Ficuzza 
and the Queen repaired to a villa in the suburbs. 
Bentinck had got a delegation of powers that he had not 
asked for, but he had not got a change of Ministry. The King, 
he was aware, had secretly arranged to keep the diplomatic 
correspondence in his own hands. He did not, however, realise 
as yet how little real power the King and Queen meant to 
delegate to their son; besides, he had formed a very favourable 
first impression of the Prince’s character and good intentions. 
He therefore resumed payment of the subsidy without exacting 
an immediate change of men or measures: ‘I was disposed’, he 
said, ‘to place all reliance in the Prince’s word and his assurance 
that certain things would be done would be satisfactory to me.’ 
At the same time, he agreed to give a personal guarantee of the 
Royal Family’s possession of Sicily (though not of Naples); he 
tried to moderate the novel anti-Neapolitan zeal of the Prince, 
who claimed that ‘now it was necessary everything should be 
Sicilian’; and he refused the offer of a seat on the Council: 
‘T thought it would not look well... and would not be pleasing to 
my Government—our principle had always been not to interfere in the 
internal concerns of our neighbours. We had only done so now because 
we were compelled.” 
Bentinck sent his government complete and detailed accounts 
of all these transactions, as he was to do with hardly any excep- 
tion throughout his mission. By the time they were received, 
Wellesley had resigned and Castlereagh was Foreign Secretary.” 
Castlereagh hastened to send Bentinck the government's ap- 
proval of his conduct in the ‘very delicate and arduous circum- 
stances’ he had been faced with. In a private letter he asked for 
proofs of the treachery which had regrettably compelled Ben- 
tinck to use his full powers: the proceeding might have to be 
defended in Parliament. Although his approval was unqualified, 
there was perhaps a slight hint in his expression of satisfaction 
at Bentinck’s having declined a seat on the Council: it shewed, 
he wrote, ‘that Your Lordship’s views were strictly confined to 

<<<PDFPAGE 46>>>
30 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the removal of evils incompatible swith the defence of the 
island, the safety of the British troops and the preservation of 
the alliance’.* 
The guarded and conciliatory policy which these words of 
Castlereagh’s implied was just what Bentinck himself had in 
mind immediately after the proclamation of the Vicariate. So 
eager was he to let the Prince and the Patriots carry on the 
government with the least interference possible from the British 
that he even decided to leave Palermo for a while. Yet Bentinck 
could neither think his task completed nor refrain from making 
plans for the future. As early as January 31st he noted 
‘my plan for Sicily—Parliament—Constitution and national force to 
protect it....’ But, he added, ‘I wish to go on gently and to accomplish 
by degrees and by fair measures rather than by force.”* 
Parliament, he thought, must meet in any case, as the govern- 
ment could scarcely go on without additional supply. But a new 
Constitution was rather more than the mere reform of abuses 
which he had been instructed to achieve. No doubt the patriots 
were convinced that the present Constitution would no longer 
work: it must be made anew; only thus could Sicily be restored 
to her ancient glory, and her inhabitants enabled to cry out one 
happy day “We too are a Nation!’ Bentinck met them more 
than half-way: when he saw Belmonte and Castelnuovo on 
their return from the islands he was the first to broach the 
subject, and he is said to have told the Queen: “Constitution or 
revolution: there isno middle way.” But itseems likely that what 
he wanted was rather a Constitution than a particular collection 
of reforms; that is to say, a political gesture which might have 
a resounding effect in Italy. Sicily was still to his mind a stepping- 
stone to the Continent; and he was stating his own opinion 
when he told the Prince that Sicily could 
‘contribute most powerfully to the conquest of Italy, not by its arms, 
but by the example of its happiness. The Italians are an intelligent people. 
They have long desired a Constitution which should give them national 
liberty. They never will submit to be transferred from one despotism to 
another. . . 
. If by the desire of the Prince a Constitution is given to Sicily 
which shall assure a moderate and reasonable freedom to its people, and 
shall promote its happiness and prosperity, from that period I should con- 

<<<PDFPAGE 47>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
31 
sider the conquest of Italy and the restoration of Naples as half achieved. 
All eyes on the Continent will be turned to Sicily’—and, he threw out 
as a further inducement—‘possibly not only Naples but many of the sur- 
rounding States may desire the blessings of the same Chief and the same 
Government’.* 
To achieve all this, Bentinck had to set up the Prince as a 
constitutional monarch, and the patriots as a responsible Minis- 
try. Both these ends, as it turned out, were harder to achieve than 
Bentinck had expected: indeed it took two months merely to 
change the Ministry. The Prince seemed at first disposed to 
make Bentinck his confidential mentor; he professed the most 
laudable constitutional sentiments, and he may even have meant 
them at the time, for it soon became clear that he could not 
think or act decidedly on any matter. He was afraid of his 
parents, of the British, of the barons. To Bentinck he ‘confessed 
his doubts and fears... they turned his head’—but, he added 
apologetically, ‘perhaps they are the effect of my anciennes 
habitudes which in time I shall overcome’. Bentinck combated 
these vacillations, assuring him that ‘no Prince ever had so fine 
a game’; but after a few weeks’ inconclusive discussion, his 
early esteem for the Prince began to wilt. This, he decided, was 
not the mere hesitancy of a novice; for when ‘a truth so evident 
was not acted upon’, must there not be ‘some secret cause which 
got the better’? Still he excused it, and rather blamed the influ- 
ence, past and present, of the Queen.* The Prince’s good in- 
tentions ought at least to be put to the test, as he told the barons: 
for they were inclined to stand on their dignity and refuse office 
altogether. “Why was I alone to act?’ he asked them: 
‘ 
.. take office and see if your advice is followed—if not go out and 
then I shall have ground to go on. Belles Paroles will not do—we must 
take things as we find them and act accordingly’. 
The use of force against the Royal Family, which Belmonte 
rather favoured, might achieve quick results, but no lasting 
settlement; whereas 
to persuade a stupid distrustful man would be a work of time and 
difficulty, but not impracticable—the good if done would be permanent.” 
Even so it was difficult to form a Ministry combining the 

<<<PDFPAGE 48>>>
32 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
right principles with the political skill needed to guide the 
Prince. Castelnuovo was too stiff, Aci too violent, Cassaro too 
conservative. Belmonte, on the other hand, though somewhat 
glib and transparently vain, seemed by his intelligence, boldness 
and fluency the only man able to hold the Prince and the barons 
together. Orleans—Bentinck’s new friend and counsellor— 
pressed Belmonte’s claims; and Bentinck set out to build him 
up into a Sicilian Prime Minister. Yet this course had disad- 
vantages which he could not perceive at the time. Belmonte 
united a desire for quick and forcible action (by the British) 
with a high regard for ‘public opinion’. By this he meant some- 
thing akin to ‘face’: without doubting the sincerity of his po- 
litical convictions, one may say that he had something of the 
typical Southern Italian faction leader of later times—he and 
his personal followers must achieve power, and be seen to 
achieve power, to the exclusion of all others.* Bentinck thought 
little of Sicilian public opinion, judging on past performance, 
though he appealed to it often enough when urging the Prince 
to carry out the desired changes; in any case, he told Belmonte, 
‘the only way to retain it was to deserve it and be indifferent 
to it’.* Nevertheless he gave in to Belmonte on several important 
points. The new ‘system’, he had thought at first, should start 
out by alienating as few sympathies as possible: advances from 
the sitting Councillors and Ministers (and there were plenty) 
should therefore not be rejected out of the hand, and some 
provision should be made for them. But Belmonte would not 
have it: he was sure he could manage both Government and 
Parliament alone; and he persuaded Bentinck to demand a
