Ministry and Council made up of none but supposedly safe 
Patriots. Now the Prince wished, as he said, to “unite all parties’ 
and consult all shades of opinion: Bentinck had therefore to 
expound to him the doctrine of Cabinet solidarity, a doctrine 
which, though he might hold it good in itself, he had not 
previously thought of applying so drastically in Sicily. Bentinck 
was similarly induced, against his better judgment, to foist Aci 
on the Prince as one of the proposed Ministers: this was a man 
whose incompetence everyone admitted, but whose recent exile 
gave him, according to Belmonte, a claim to favour. 
In any case Bentinck was steadily losing patience with the 

<<<PDFPAGE 49>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
33 
Prince, who plainly had no wish either to call Parliament or 
change his advisers. He had been mistaken, he admitted, in 
trusting him in the first place: now he exacted the new Minist 
and Council from the Prince by reminding him that he had 
merely delayed, not given up, carrying out the British Govern- 
ment’s instructions. The new Ministry, consisting of Belmonte 
(Foreign Affairs), Castelnuovo (Finance), Cassaro (Justice), and 
Aci (War), came in at the end of March; but only after the 
Prince had so far abandoned his constitutional professions as to 
insist on Bentinck’s signature to a document urging the change 
and so in a sense taking responsibility for it. ‘He was indeed 
acting very magnanimously towards me’, Bentinck told the 
Prince on this occasion. ‘I had wished to give him all the credit 
and none of the blame. He was now making me a return, by 
wishing to make it appear that he was compelled to the change, 
that he was always attached to the old system and to its agents.’ 
Bentinck was irritated at having lost two valuable months, 
at a time, too, when the rumoured approach of Franco-Russian 
hostilities promised a chance of action in Italy. Conciliation, he 
concluded, would not work after all: stronger measures would 
be needed, and steady support from home: 
‘Nothing but the firm and just exercise of our superiority will... 
secure to this country that better administration from whence its own 
happiness and greatness and our safety can alone be derived. I beg the 
British Government not to be deceived on this point.’* 
In three months Bentinck had come to see the need for nothing 
less than a continuing British protectorate over Sicily. His early 
hope that the new ‘system’ might go on almost by itself had 
rested on several assumptions: now these props were falling out 
one by one. He had wanted the Patriots to carry on the govern- 
ment by themselves, with the British Minister a mere paternal 
counsellor in the background: he soon found that they could 
not; by sheer force of circumstance, as will be seen, he himself 
had to become an active political leader. He had thought the 
Royal Family little more than a spent force: presently he found 
that he could not impose on Sicily a policy acceptable to the 
British Government without engaging in a running contest 

<<<PDFPAGE 50>>>
34 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
with the King and Queen; and the.contest went on almost 
without intermission for a whole year. 
But of all broken reeds the Prince was the most obvious. Not 
only had he delayed bringing in a new Ministry; he would not 
modernise the Army. In January he had accepted Bentinck’s 
plan for a smaller, efficient, national Sicilian Army, fit for service 
anywhere in the Mediterranean. But he was, after all, no less 
a Neapolitan at heart than his parents: he had no desire to lose 
his large inefficient Neapolitan army, and with it (as he thought) 
the chance of recovering Naples: try as Bentinck might, he 
could not get the Prince to act. The Prince proved equally 
reluctant to call Parliament, as his new Ministers at once urged 
him to do. Bentinck did his best to support them; he played on 
the Prince’s hopes of Naples, declaring that although the day 
for action in Italy might be near at hand, he could not take a 
single soldier out of the island until a Sicilian Parliament had 
given Italy the example she was waiting for. But the Prince 
feared a Long Parliament, and Bentinck’s guarantees did not 
reassure him. Even when he resigned himself to it, he objected 
to the mention of constitutional reform which Castelnuovo 
had inserted in his draft of the Royal Despatch summoning 
Parliament: and although Bentinck hammered away at the 
familiar tack of Italy, the magic word Constitution had to be 
omitted from the Despatch when it was finally issued on 1 May.* 
Bentinck’s regard for the Prince had by then quite evaporated. 
The man, he concluded, was inherently weak, distrustful and 
dilatory, at bottom as despotically-minded as his parents, super- 
stitiously afraid of them and incapable of taking an independent 
line. Fear and fear alone would make him go on in the path of 
constitutional monarchy.* 
Yet the Prince remained an ambiguous figure. The fact—more 
obvious now than at the time—was that although he had a 
policy of his own, he had not strength enough to be a free agent. 
He wished to conciliate the British, not out of conviction—his 
constitutional fervour, if sincere at all, was only momentary— 
but out of fear, a realisation of his own impotence, and a desire 
to save what could be saved for his family. Bentinck certainly 
frightened him into 
a wish to comply—‘gettiamo acqua! 
gettiamo acqua!’ he would cry on receiving one of Bentinck’s 

<<<PDFPAGE 51>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
35 
peremptory Notes.* But what pressure from one quarter did, 
pressure from another undid. The King had ostensibly delegated 
power to his son. But, he asked Bentinck on one occasion, ‘if 
I had appointed my son to manage my Estate [should I] not 
expect him to be subordinate to me?’ This was the King’s 
candid opinion: he regarded his kingdom as his personal proper- 
ty; he was ‘displeased at the Nation being mentioned . 
. 
. it was 
a new mode which did not exist in former times . . 
. his power 
came from God’.* His view of the Vicariate—like the Queen’s— 
was simple. Their position at the centre of affairs was for the 
moment inconvenient: the Prince must therefore take it over, 
and with it the embarrassment of dealing with Bentinck; but he 
was to be ‘like an egg in [the King’s] hand which he might 
preserve as long as he pleased and crush’ or rather set aside when 
it suited him.* This device was not new to the Royal Family; 
it had served at Naples in 1799, and it was to serve again in 1821. 
The King and Queen, then, considered that the government 
was still theirs, with the Prince as their puppet, and that the 
Ministers ought to be their Ministers: indeed, they dropped 
circuitous hints to Belmonte and his friends that their accession 
to the Ministry would be invalid unless they asked for and 
got the King’s approval.* The government ought to do nothing 
of importance (so the King thought) without first obtaining 
his consent: his insistence it was that delayed the summoning 
of Parliament until May, and then caused the text of the Despatch 
to be altered.* He rejected the Prince’s view that conciliation 
was safest: for a long time he refused to believe that Bentinck 
was not exceeding his instructions; stubborn and suspicious, he 
went on hoping that the British Government would recall or 
disown their Minister even when his own intelligence from 
London gave him no grounds for comfort.* He and the Queen 
therefore wanted the Prince to resist Bentinck at every point; if 
he deviated in the least from their commands they castigated 
him as an ‘enfant pervers’, an ‘ambitious and ungrateful son’.* 
He, for his part, was too weak either to assert or to resign his 
authority, though Bentinck often forced him to the former, and 
he himself often threatened the latter. 
In this dilemma, the Prince took to playing off one party 
against the other. If Bentinck wanted something to which the 

<<<PDFPAGE 52>>>
36 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
King and Queen objected, he would ask Bentinck to ‘make a 
Note of it’, preferably a strong one which he could produce to 
his parents as evidence of the British Minister’s terribilita and in 
justification of his own compliance. If the measure was im- 
portant—like the change of Ministry or the meeting of Parlia- 
ment—he would get Bentinck not only to press it in strong 
terms, but also to guarantee the Crown against any dangers to 
which it might give rise.* In this way he presumably thought to 
escape responsibility; but at the same time he played into 
Bentinck’s hands: for Bentinck could afterwards maintain that, 
since he had pledged himself to see that a particular measure did 
no harm, he must demand that it should be carried out as he 
wished. Thus the Prince bought present comfort at the cost of 
future trouble. Caught between two forces greater than his own 
(sometimes, to his despair, between three, when his parents 
were at odds)—or, to put it on a higher plane, with “Hell on one 
side and the reproach of Posterity on the other’*—the Prince 
swung to and fro, anxious to please everybody, unable to please 
anybody, and rightly accused by all of weakness and duplicity. 
Bentinck knew the Prince’s subjection to his parents, but not 
the full extent of it; he thought the Prince more responsible for 
his own actions than he was, and he did not at first guess how 
far the King interfered. This error was easy to fall into: Ferdinand 
had been notorious for decades as one of the idlest monarchs in 
Europe; in his present retreat at La Ficuzza he assiduously fed 
this reputation, declaring to all and sundry that he was suffering 
for his sins and wished to be considered a dead man. Bentinck 
naturally assumed that he cared no more for affairs than formerly, 
and—when he did act—that someone else was pulling the 
strings. Who then was to blame? Obviously the Queen.* 
Much has been written on the long struggle between Bentinck 
and Maria Carolina, perhaps too much: for the personal conflict, 
though dramatic in itself, was really no more than an outgrowth 
of the essential political conflict, a somewhat irrelevant descant. 
Bentinck, it is true, spent needless time and energy in his cam- 
paign against the Queen, only to discover too late that her 
expulsion—first from Palermo, then from Sicily altogether— 
was no remedy for the island’s political troubles.* But then all 
circumstances told against the Queen. She was the perfect scape- 

<<<PDFPAGE 53>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
37 
goat for everyone, above all for her own family. She was 
notorious, to begin with, as an insatiable meddler. The King 
had the sense to lie low; but she could not stop the flow of her 
wild talk and fruitless activity. To all who would listen she 
confided her fears of the block or of a trial before Parliament; 
her tirades against the ‘bestia feroce’ Bentinck; her sudden and 
ephemeral resolves to retire to Catania, to Vienna (where her 
nephew the Emperor had invited her), even to England.* She 
ever clung to the belief that Bentinck must shortly be recalled 
for exceeding his instructions, and she clutched at straws to 
justify it—a platitute of Castlereagh’s in a Commons debate, or 
the prattle of a visiting Englishman.* She dabbled in preposterous 
intrigues to bring the King back to power as a constitutional 
monarch; and the riffraff of spies and agents continued to infest 
her antechamber.* Then rumour amplified the whir of her 
activity; she was alleged to be fomenting popular disorder, 
plotting a counter-revolution, appealing to Austria and Russia 
for help.* Naturally those whom her fall from power had 
compromised now did their best to blame her. Her son, even 
while he was begging Bentinck not to ‘act horribly’ towards 
her, shewed plainly that he thought her the skeleton in the 
family cupboard, beloved but utterly irresponsible. “Ma mére 
a une telle téte, une telle téte’, he would cry. Her husband did 
the same; and his two confidants, Cassaro and his confessor 
Father Caccamo, scarcely let slip an opportunity of setting 
Bentinck against her. She was, they told him again and again, 
the arch mischief-maker; the King was under her thumb, though 
he would willingly be rid of her: ‘he had been married 44 
years .. 
. they were so many years of Martyre’.* So general was 
this attitude that when the Prince at one time became dangerously 
ill, she was suspected of having poisoned him: Bentinck 
suggested to the Prince’s physician that heat might be the cause, 
but ‘he cried out shaking with palsy all over, Ce n’est pas la 
chaleur—c’est sa mére—sa mére’.* 
In fact the Queen, as her letters shew, did interfere in affairs 
as much as she could. Her husband and son may on occasion 
have found her a convenient scapegoat, but there can be little 
doubt that they were often genuinely harassed and influenced 
by her outcries. It was quite natural that Bentinck should fasten 

<<<PDFPAGE 54>>>
38 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
on her the blame for the delays and difficulties he experienced. 
He therefore concluded that only her removal to a safe distance 
from Palermo could put an end to her evil influence over King, 
Prince and populace. She herself talked of going away; she even 
said she would leave Sicily altogether if England paid her debts. 
But Bentinck set no store by this, for, he said, ‘human nature 
was the same everywhere—She could not divest herself of habits 
of forty years’ continuance, that of governing ...’.” What he 
needed was a pretext. This came to hand unexpectedly in March 
1812, when one Cassetti, once a spy of the Queen’s, and now 
in prison, confessed to having acted as go-between to her and 
Murat and revealed her so-called plot for new Vespers against 
the English.* Bentinck did not take this information very seri- 
ously: ‘I did not believe that she had any serious intention of 
giving up the island. She had been instigated by her love even 
to madness of intrigue . . . this I believe to be the truth.’ But he 
could use the ‘proofs’ to attack what he really feared, the Queen’s 
political influence: he wrote her a letter respectful in manner if 
not in matter, suggesting that after this discovery—which he 
hoped to keep secret—she would do well to withdraw to a 
remoter part of Sicily.* Six weeks’ negotiations ensued; letters 
flew back and forth between the Queen, the King, the Prince, 
Bentinck and Caccamo. The King wanted Bentinck to shew 
his proofs to the Prince, to Cassaro, to anyone but himself; 
while Bentinck refused to shew them to anyone else. Finally, 
at the end of April, the Queen agreed to join the King (much 
against his wish) at La Ficuzza. The distance from Palermo, 
about 25 miles, still seemed too short to Bentinck; he contem- 
plated using force, but—reflecting that ‘the pushing things to 
an extremity might have had an appearance of cruelty —he let the 
matter drop for the moment. He could still return to the charge 
if necessary, brandishing his proofs, the validity of which the 
King had tacitly admitted by refusing to see them.* 
Castlereagh, whom Bentinck informed of all these trans- 
actions, took the proofs very seriously indeed: they justified, he 
said, all that Bentinck had done in Sicily; and he left it entirely 
to Bentinck’s discretion how far they should be disclosed, and 
‘what conditions with respect to Her Majesty’s residence and 
her abandoning all interference in the Government it may be 

<<<PDFPAGE 55>>>
BRITAIN INTERVENES 
39 
expedient to require’. In the Commons he had already described 
Bentinck’s conduct as ‘not less remarkable for mildness and 
forbearance than for a rigid execution of his instructions’. Now, 
in giving Bentinck fresh instructions, he agreed that there was 
still a lot of work for him to do: 
‘To recommend such necessary reforms of the Sicilian Constitution 
as may ensure the affections of the people, and make the Neapolitans 
anxious to receive equal advantages, together with the return of their 
lawful sovereigns: these will continue to be the objects of Your Lordship’s 
attention.”* 
With the Queen’s influence apparently out of the way, 
Bentinck could now address himself to the task of constructing 
a new political ‘system’ for Sicily. 

<<<PDFPAGE 56>>>
CHAPTER*II 
THE\PROTEGCTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
The theme of British policy in Sicily—so Bentinck had written 
home at the end of March—must be ‘the firm and just exercise 
of our superiority’. What exactly this might mean in practice 
was Bentinck’s chief problem throughout the ensuing year. 
By her intervention Britain had established a de facto protec- 
torate over Sicily; but it was a protectorate born under an am- 
biguous star. Apprehension and distrust, carefree optimism and 
good intentions, had all gone into the original decision to 
intervene; it is not surprising that the result was a diplomatic 
muddle. Britain could not very well appear to adopt Napoleon’s 
methods: the fiction had therefore to be kept up that the two 
nations were equal and independent allies. Castlereagh allowed 
Bentinck to go on using the language of force—though not 
force itself—to achieve British aims; but he repeatedly insisted 
that the ‘honour’, ‘dignity’, and ‘security’ of the Sicilian Royal 
Family must be safeguarded.* There was a pretence that some- 
how it was all for the good of the Crown and people of Sicily. 
Not the least reason for muddle was that this was not wholly 
a pretence. Bentinck really believed it; “an interference merely 
to carry some British object,’ he said, ‘might not be justifiable’; but 
‘listening to the call of the whole country and having no view but 
their benefit and most of all of the Royal Family themselves, the British 
Government will surely be considered by the present and future gener- 
ations as only fulfilling the duties of a good and great nation.”* 
Castlereagh, for his part, seems to have taken the fiction for 
granted until it became too inconvenient to keep up. Besides, 
no one had attempted to think out the possible consequences of 
intervention: Bentinck, Amherst and other British observers, 
by inference Wellesley himself, all had assumed that, the first 
crisis over, the ‘machine’ would run on smoothly without 
further interference. The negotiations over the new Ministry 
and the calling of Parliament had begun to undeceive Bentinck; 
they also provided a pattern for that vicious circle of demand, 
delay, mutual irritation, and finally grudging concession leading 

<<<PDFPAGE 57>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
AI 
to further demands, which was to bedevil nearly the whole of 
Bentinck’s mission.* 
Yet, in spite of the constant difficulties he experienced, 
Bentinck’s power seemed great, almost viceregal. He did, after 
all, control Sicily’s finances through the subsidy, and her army 
through his Captain-Generalship. His house was the headquarters 
of the Patriotic party.* He conferred with Orleans and Belmonte 
daily; saw the other Ministers, nobles, and politicians frequently; 
discussed military business with the Prince and the Sicilian staff. 
He raised public loans, distributed corn when it was short, 
arranged the redemption of Sicilian slaves from Tunis, and 
gave magnificent entertainments, some of which the Prince 
attended.* Noble families asked him to arbitrate their contested 
marriage settlements; the very Ministers he had driven out of 
office—one, indeed, into exile: Ascoli—now cringed to him and 
solicited his favour.* He did not hesitate to rebuke the Prince of 
Butera in strong terms when that first baron of Sicily became 
involved in riots against the government;* if a monk betrayed 
to the Queen a confidential letter of his, he made the government 
deport the monk to Lipari.* The supporters of the ‘good cause’ 
he marshalled and encouraged: true, his diplomatic position 
prevented him at first from appearing quite openly as the leader of 
a party: he refused, for instance, to see the masters of the Palermo 
guilds when, in the election of May 1812, they chose to flout 
tradition and the Prince’s wishes and came to him for advice; 
but secretly he sent a message urging them to stand firm. He was, 
in fact, already playing a dual role as British Minister and Sicilian 
politician; the new ‘system’ was patently his creation. To the 
Patriots he seemed, anyhow at first, the redeemer of Sicily, and in 
his praise they wrote a great mamy stanzas, satires and sonnets.* 
It may seem strange that with all this display of power 
Bentinck constantly complained of delay and frustration. The 
chief trouble was, of course, his running battle with the Royal 
Family; another was the peculiar character of the British inter- 
vention, which at once made Bentinck’s power irresistible, and 
prevented him from exerting it to the full. Furthermore, the 
weakness of the Patriots—which their accession to the Ministry 
soon revealed—hindered him even while it forced him to behave 
like an active local politician. 

<<<PDFPAGE 58>>>
42 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
When the new Ministers came in at the end of March, 
Bentinck conceived of himself as their friend and confidential 
adviser; he did not then mean to lay down the law. True, he and 
Belmonte were so close that it is difficult to speak of their acting 
independently of one another: a later but typical entry in 
Bentinck’s journal runs, “He [Belmonte] wrote me a Note 
asking it and helped me to write the refusal of it.’* Even so, 
Bentinck allowed Belmonte, as newly installed chief Minister, 
considerable play: he had previously seen and exhorted the 
Prince nearly every day, but he now left to Belmonte the task 
of managing both Prince and—later—Parliament. This Bel- 
monte was confident he could do; and his confidence outlasted 
that of all his friends. 
Everyone had assumed that the Ministry would be united 
and that they would be responsible, in the sense of being in 
actual control of the government. United they certainly were 
not. Aci proved a liability from the start: he and Bentinck 
clashed, and went on disputing for months, over details of 
day-to-day army administration. 
To Bentinck’s mind the 
Minister for War ought not to concern himself with this any 
more than the Secretary of State at home. Aci had larger views, 
and quoted a French Encyclopaedia (art. Ministre de la Guerre) 
in support. He promoted officers, moved troops without 
Bentinck’s knowledge, quarrelled with the Sicilian Chief of 
Staff, and generally displayed what Bentinck thought intolerable 
wrongheadedness and vanity, until Bentinck decided that he 
‘would not permit’ such behaviour any longer. But the Prince 
had taken a fancy to Aci, probably because of his very dis- 
agreements with Bentinck; if asked to dismiss him, he could 
retort that Bentinck had imposed Aci on him in the first place. 
Belmonte, whose nominee Aci had been, could neither control 
nor dislodge him. Indeed he asked Bentinck on occasion to 
write a Note to the Prince and so prevent some step which Aci 
was about to take and which he, Belmonte, disapproved.* 
Similarly Belmonte, finding that Cassaro was giving the 
Prince constitutional advice at variance with that of the other 
Ministers, asked Bentinck to tell him that ‘England would not 
allow any opposition to its views for the interests of both 
countries’; and Cassaro himself wanted Bentinck to intervene 

<<<PDFPAGE 59>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
43 
and prevent what he apprehended as revolutionary develop- 
ments.” 
The fact was that nothing like a Cabinet system had ever 
existed in Sicily or Naples. The Ministers were still very much 
the Prince’s secretaries; so true was this of Belmonte that the 
Prince did not even allow him to read the foreign despatches, in 
spite of his and Bentinck’s protests.* They worked independently 
of each other, meeting officially only in Council, with the Prince 
in attendance; and though they might hold informal councils of 
their own—which Bentinck sometimes attended—no discipline 
could be imposed on an individual Minister such as Aci or 
Cassaro. Furthermore, though there had been powerful chief 
Ministers at Naples in the past—Tanucci, Acton—it gradually 
became clear that Belmonte was not, after all, the man to emulate 
them: but it took Bentinck some time to realise his friend’s 
shortcomings. 
Altogether, the relations between Bentinck and the Sicilian 
patriots could only be described as peculiar. He must try to lead 
them, and most of them still wanted his advice and support; 
yet he was not of them. He was inevitably a foreigner, had 
still something of the East India official, observing with detached 
amusement the antics of the natives.” ‘I began to know my 
compatriots the Sicilians’, he said sarcastically; ‘vanity was the 
great defect of the country—I never had seen anything like it 
in any part of the world’. And he thought he detected in the 
blustering Aci the vices of a nation: ‘the Sicilians in general are 
bullies and are only to be managed by fear. They take advantage 
of the least concession and always construe conciliation into 
weakness’.* What, then, led him to persist in doing what he 
could (as he conceived it) to help the Sicilians? “Bonaparte made 
Kings, England makes Nations’, he is supposed to have said at 
one time; at another, that ‘England ought to give liberty to 
every country she could reach’.* Perhaps, too, pride forbade 
him to abandon an attempt the success of which he had confi- 
dently prophesied in his first despatches: he seems to have 
realised quite early how unlikely the new ‘system’ was to survive 
the withdrawal of British force—though this was probably the 
kind of truth which he could perceive but did not dwell upon.* 
Yet he seems also to have been moved by a genuine concern for 

<<<PDFPAGE 60>>>
44 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the Sicilians’ welfare—a concern of*which his indignation at 
their shortcomings might well be the revers de la médaille. This 
it was that led him to lecture the uninterested Prince on the 
greatness and prosperity which Sicily might recover through 
a better road system and a colonisation of the land; to oppose 
the grain-tax that crushed the peasantry; to realise (what many 
of the patriots did not) that the aristocracy were as unpopular 
as the former Neapolitan government, and their abuses no less 
burdensome.” He could so far shed insular intolerance as to 
attend Mass, kneeling side by side with Orleans in Alcamo 
church (they were on an excursion to Segesta) and feeling 
embarrassed because he did not know enough to bow at the 
Elevation.* Altogether his ironic detachment was not complete, 
his exasperation with the Sicilians not permanent; in an odd, 
intermittent way his affections were becoming engaged. Later 
on this emotional commitment was to lead him into steps both 
drastic and damaging to his own career; meanwhile he could 
only do his best to shore up and hold together his friends in office. 
In these circumstances, then, Bentinck set out on the more 
specifically political part of his programme. By May 1812 he 
had got several of the things his Government and he himself 
had wanted. The Queen’s mischief seemed temporarily checked; 
the Prince, though a disappointment, did not yet seem irre- 
trievably lost to Bentinck’s and Belmonte’s influence; and on 
the first of the month the Royal Despatch had at last gone out 
to summon a new Parliament. The time had come to launch 
that constitutional reform which Bentinck ardently desired as 
a rallying-cry for Italian patriotism, and which Castlereagh was 
at that very moment directing him to strive for. 
The Sicilian Constitution of 1812 has won some notoriety as 
that “which for the first time laid down in writing the glorious 
principles of the unwritten constitution of Britain’.* But this 
deliberate copy of an elusive foreign model came about partly 
through a chapter of accidents; Bentinck, far from imposing it, 
deplored it before he came to acquiesce in it. 
The Patriotic leaders began to discuss constitutional reform 
in February, soon after the establishment of the Vicariate. 
Balsamo undertook to draw up, in consultation with Belmonte 
and Castelnuovo, proposals for specific reforms. Bentinck saw 

<<<PDFPAGE 61>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
45 
this draft in April; he had not helped to prepare it except in the 
way of general conversation; it was only later that he discussed 
specific proposals with Castelnuovo and Belmonte, and that 
some of them were amended at his suggestion. Nobody at this 
early stage thought of drawing up an entirely new constitution; 
Balsamo and his friends agreed that the old one should be ad- 
hered to as much as possible. They wished on the one hand to 
restore it to its original state in the Aragonese period, and on 
the other hand to adapt it to modern conditions. To this end 
the reformers took as their guide the English constitution, 
‘recommended by the experience and success of centuries’, while 
they rejected the Spanish and French, ‘which are too democratic, 
and therefore tending to anarchy; and which assume in society 
that wisdom and virtue, which may be imagined, but are not 
to be found in man’. But when Balsamo produced his draft late 
in April, it was already clear that the Ministers and their followers 
were anything but unanimous, either as to the reforms them- 
selves or as to the means of carrying them out.* 
All were agreed on two points: first, the power of the Crown 
—on the increase for the last thirty years, and lately asserted in 
defiance of the Constitution—must be curtailed; second, Sicily 
must shake free of Neapolitan domination. A return to the 
Aragonese capitularies, such as Balsamo’s draft provided for, 
would serve both these purposes. But beyond this there were 
many points of disagreement. 
Constitutional reform had to resolve the crisis of 1810-1812. 
Not only had the conflict between Crown and Parliament then 
come to a head, but the barons’ standing as a class had been 
called in question. To some it might seem that Bentinck’s defeat 
of the Crown meant the setting up of a baronial oligarchy: 
that was what the Queen feared and Cassaro, for one, desired: 
he would be quite content if the clock was merely turned back 
to the status quo ante Caracciolo. He had formerly stood up to 
the Crown in defence of the barons’ privileges; now that the 
Crown was beaten he feared any innovations which might 
weaken or destroy the feudal system. This fear led him to ally 
himself with the King, and to advise the Prince that he had no 
right to call Parliament or set about reforming the Constitution 
without the King’s consent.* At the other extreme from Cassaro 

<<<PDFPAGE 62>>>
46 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
and those barons who, like him, had*resisted the Crown from 
purely conservative and class motives, stood another group with 
Castelnuovo as its head and Balsamo as its purveyor of political 
doctrine. Castelnuovo realised that the barons’ defence of 
Parliament had lately come to involve interests wider than those 
of their own class: though he wished to avoid the French ‘spirit 
of theory and system’, he declared that the barons would have 
to ‘make some significant sacrifice of their interests and preroga- 
tives for the sake of the Nation’s general welfare’.* Some of his 
friends went further: Airoldi, for instance, combined a vague 
philosophic republicanism with a desire to concentrate most of 
the powers of government in the legislature; in this his fear of 
the Crown led him to follow that very French theory and 
practice which he professed to dislike. Apart from these two 
groups, the rest of the patriotic party was typified rather than 
led by Belmonte, who seemed to have no very clear notion of 
what the reformed constitution should be like. He too wished 
to diminish the power of the sovereign, who was ‘now a Sultan’; 
one guesses that he inclined to an aristocratic form of govern- 
ment: “H.R.H. could not suppose’, he asked the Prince, ‘he 
wanted revolution and that born to the situation he was he 
could wish to lower himself to an equality with his groom.’ 
He talked at one time of reverting entirely to the old constitution, 
at another of having it ‘revised, well washed and cleaned and 
put into an English dress, habillé 4 anglaise’. Yet he must have 
been won over temporarily to Castelnuovo’s views, for Bal- 
samo’s draft—which they both approved—included a clause 
abolishing feudal privileges.* 
Bentinck deprecated this somewhat conservative attitude of 
Belmonte’s: he agreed with Castelnuovo that the day was past 
when the nobility could claim a monopoly of power. Yet he 
once again gave in to Belmonte on the important question of 
how constitutional reform was to be carried out. Castelnuovo, 
no less than Belmonte, feared democracy and the backward 
mob: for the very reason that he wished to establish political 
liberty and a measure of social change, he dreaded a recru- 
descence of faction and class egoism in the new Parliament; he 
therefore proposed that Parliament should be presented with 
a Constitution octroyée. Belmonte, fundamentally much less of 

<<<PDFPAGE 63>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
A7 
an innovator, preferred to please ‘public opinion’ by letting 
Parliament set itself up as a Constituent Assembly. Bentinck 
sided at first with Castelnuovo and the Prince in this matter; 
but Belmonte eventually persuaded him that Sicily should be 
allowed ‘spontaneously to give herself a constitution, without 
the interference of any authority, much less that of Sicilian or 
English bayonets’.* 
This decision made the election of a new Braccio demaniale 
or Third Estate all the more important. It took place in May, 
against a background of general economic confusion. The 
Ministry, faced from the outset with a running deficit of 40,000 
ounces a month,” could scarcely pay the army, let alone their 
creditors, for all Bentinck’s loans and advances. Corn had been 
short for months: and Castelnuovo made things worse by 
removing the traditional controls on the sale of bread. Popular 
discontent failed to move him: he knew the Palermitans, he 
said—‘it did not answer to caress them’. In this doctrinaire 
policy he was supported by Balsamo with the arguments of 
economic liberalism, and opposed by Bentinck with those of 
common sense. As a result there were bread riots at election-time 
in Palermo, and later on in other parts of the island.* 
The election itself was marked by a few incidents. In Palermo 
the masters of the guilds traditionally elected to the Demaniale 
their Lord Mayor (Pretore). Now, however, Aci and others 
—from motives of personal jealousy, it seems—induced them 
to break with custom. This was a mistake, for the Pretore, the 
Count of San Marco, though aristocratically inclined could have 
been made into a useful ally. Besides, independence went to 
the masters’ heads; they threatened to elect one of their own 
number instead of the pocket candidate whom Aci and his 
friends had provided for them. But in the end they fell into line. 
Elsewhere too the Government, like its predecessors, influenced 
the elections—to what extent it is impossible to tell.* 
Parliament was due to meet on June 18th. One month before 
that date the Ministers were still divided on constitutional 
reform; and the attitude of the Crown was most doubtful. 
Cassaro continued to assert that Balsamo’s draft went too far, 
and that in any case Parliament was not entitled to change the 
constitution without the King’s leave. Fear of the King appar- 

<<<PDFPAGE 64>>>
48 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
ently moved him, as well as conservatism— ‘they might suffer 
with their lives’, he said.* As the King’s confidant he had reason 
to know that his master was opposed to any change. To the 
King the word Constitution was as meaningless and distasteful 
as the word Nation: he was ‘a little displeased’ to learn from 
Cassaro what he professed not to have known before—that he 
had been a constitutional monarch in Sicily these 53 years. As 
early as January 29th, a fortnight after his delegation of power, 
he had warned the British Government through Castelcicala of 
the dangers which constitutional reform would entail. There is 
no sign that his attitude had changed in the meantime; and it 
was certainly not he who (as has been stated) first thought of 
adopting the English Constitution—quite the contrary. In fact 
he was the last to reconcile himself to reform of any kind; only 
later did he set himself up as the champion of the pure English 
Constitution.* The Queen, now her husband’s unwelcome 
companion, was said to be plotting for his return to power. 
The Prince wavered: he did not know exactly what reforms 
his ministers proposed, for Belmonte, fearful of future harm, 
would not shew him Balsamo’s draft without a written order; 
and this the Prince, no less fearful, refused to give. He and 
Belmonte both tried, for different reasons, to draw Bentinck 
into taking part in discussions with the other Ministers and so 
achieving an agreed draft. Belmonte wanted Bentinck to silence 
Cassaro’s feudal opposition; while the Prince still hoped for a 
Constitution octroyée as the lesser of two evils, and probably 
wished to throw the burden of responsibility for it on Bentinck. 
Bentinck, however, had come round to Belmonte’s view that 
Parliament should be given a free hand; besides, he said, ‘I 
would rather be left out... it was an interference which my 
Government might not like’. Still he gave in to Belmonte’s 
argument that Cassaro could not be persuaded otherwise, and 
that his interference would be quite unofficial. No sooner had 
he done so than the Prince decided, on May 20th, to put 
off the meeting of Parliament, on the pretext that it might 
lead to popular commotion. Bentinck, plausibly suspecting the 
influence of the King and Queen, riposted by holding up a 
badly needed loan. But with the Crown plainly hostile, and 
Cassaro still unreconciled, the prospect for reform was bleak.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 65>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
49 
At this point came the proposal to adopt the English Consti- 
tution in its entirety. It came from the unlikeliest quarter—Aci 
and the Prince of Moliterno—and it came linked with a plan 
for an immediate expedition to Italy. Aci, volatile as usual, seems 
to have acted on his own. He merely conceived a brilliant 
notion and put it to the Prince: if Sicily adopted the English 
Constitution, Naples would want a similar boon; an expedition 
would most likely succeed. Then Sicily would be independent 
with the Prince as her King; or, if Ferdinand persisted in his 
retirement, the Prince would rule at Naples and his brother at 
Palermo.* Moliterno, for his part, had long been full of wild 
schemes for the unification of Italy into one kingdom, with 
—of all people—Ferdinand as its constitutional ruler. He had 
approached Bentinck in March, proposing the formation of an 
‘Armée de la Constitution Italique’ to be subsidised by Britain, 
and vaunting his connections with the Italian patriots; but 
Bentinck had thought him no more than a crank and an ad- 
venturer. Now, however, Moliterno once again urged his plan 
on the King and Queen. An expedition meant a constitution: 
his own ideas on the subject were rather vague; since the 
prestigious English Constitution was now in the limelight, why 
not take it as a model and a slogan ?* 
These proposals of Aci’s and Moliterno’s did not spring fully 
armed from the inventors’ brains. Anglomania, as we have 
seen, already prevailed in Sicily at this period. It had not abated 
by 1812: the very terminology of British politics seemed to bear 
some magical virtue. The new constitution, when it came to 
be written, talked of bills and budgets; Aci thought to entice the 
clergy with the offer of a Convocation; those who considered the 
new constitution not ‘popular’ enough complained that “it even 
established Star Chamber’. The learned Balsamo himself won- 
dered for a moment whether Sicily had not better adopt the 
elaborate comedy of ‘fine and common recovery’ as a means 
of getting round entails.» When Aci spoke to the Prince, the 
English Constitution had already been chosen as an ‘inspiration’; 
Aci merely changed ‘inspiration’ to ‘model’. The state of Sicilian 
thought explains why he could do so. The circumstances of 
the moment explain why his proposal pleased nearly every- 
body. 

<<<PDFPAGE 66>>>
50 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
It pleased the Patriots for several reasons. A successful expe- 
dition would mean a wonderful chance to get rid of the Nea- 
politans and wrest from the King the independence of Sicily 
under a separate dynasty. The English Constitution they ee 
admired; even so they took to it—as will be seen—not so muc 
with the intention of copying it in toto as with a desire to gloss 
over their present differences. It suited Castelnuovo’s moderate 
reformism, as well as Belmonte’s aristocratic leanings and his 
hazy preference for the popular solution. It even suited Cassaro 
and Butera: on first hearing it proposed Cassaro ‘grew pale’, 
but presently his son ‘translated’ it—from what text we do not 
know— and he ‘read it with great pleasure’; Butera too thought 
‘anything was better than the speculations of those wild poli- 
ticians’.* 
The Prince ‘jumped at it’ naturally enough, since he saw a 
chance of moving against Murat with the support of both the 
British and the Sicilians. This the Bourbons could not do 
without; and they must not let Bentinck move without them, 
else he might throw them over in favour of the Archduke 
Francis and Naples might then slip out of their grasp. Perhaps, 
too, the Prince was ready to contemplate with momentary 
favour the separation of Sicily from Naples, since it would 
give him a kingdom to himself. But if there was to be an expe- 
dition there must be a Constitution; that was Bentinck’s price. 
And if there must be a Constitution, the English was safer than 
most: it would be something like a Constitution octroyée; it would, 
he said, calm the hotheads; Parliament would soon be over, 
‘and we can then turn our views to other matters’. Besides, it 
would keep Bentinck and his friends busy, and draw their 
attention away from more perilous matters. Above all the 
English Constitution had the great advantages of ‘the devil you 
know’: the Prince was so ready to accept it (Bentinck concluded) 
because 
‘he [had] always thought he would have less power than the King of 
England. ... He had often remarked to me, when I had talked of the 
Civil List and of other parliamentary interference—mais le Roi d’ Angle- 
terre a ce et ce pouvoir, not adverting to the particular point in question 
but generally as if it was intended to make a complete cypher of him.’* 

<<<PDFPAGE 67>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
51 
So the Prince took up Aci’s twin notions of expedition and 
English constitution with great zeal; he decided not to prorogue 
Parliament after all, and undertook to convert his parents. The 
King was suspicious from the first. The Prince’s ideas, he noted 
at the beginning of June, were ‘diametrically opposed’ to his 
own; in his son’s enthusiasm he thought he detected a mask 
for unfilial ambition. He still objected in principle, he told 
Cassaro, to any kind of new Constitution, and he wanted the 
meeting of Parliament put off until September, by which time 
he hoped Bentinck would have been recalled. But, he added, if 
Bentinck promised an expedition to Naples ‘he would consent 
to some Novita being introduced’. For a moment it seemed the 
Prince and Cassaro had persuaded him.* The Queen, however, 
was up in arms against Aci’s proposals, which she denounced 
to her husband as ‘malicious’ and ‘erroneous’. She urged him 
to set his face against a new Constitution and above all against 
the ‘rebellious proposal’ of the separation of Sicily from Naples. 
The King replied cautiously that he agreed with her but that 
British pressure might force him to accept some kind of Consti- 
tution for fear of ‘fatal consequences’. But he changed his mind 
once again: two days later (June 9th) he sent Bentinck, on his 
Own initiative, a questionnaire which shewed that he still thought 
Bentinck’s conduct revolutionary and probably unauthorised; 
he hinted at a desire to resume power, and demanded a pension 
for himself and his family (not including the Prince) in case 
Bentinck really meant to ‘annihilate’ him. At the same time he 
plunged the Prince into dismay by again refusing to countenance 
the opening of Parliament, which was now only a week off.* 
Bentinck was most annoyed. He replied that he had faithfully 
followed his instructions; if the King resumed he expected to 
see those engagements fulfilled which the Vicar had entered 
into—that is, Parliament, reform, and army reorganisation; and 
he seized the chance once again to press his case against the 
Queen, including the famous proofs. Encouraged by Cassaro, 
he blamed her as usual (and not altogether wrongly) for the 
King’s recalcitrance: ‘I had been justly punished for my moder- 
ation towards the Queen... it was only since her sejour with 
the King that H.M. had become uneasy, had interfered in the 
public business, and was now induced to resume the Govern- 

<<<PDFPAGE 68>>>
§2 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
ment.’ The King, to be sure, had good reasons of his own for 
balking at the separation of Sicily from Naples; and Bentinck’s 
plans for Italian independence were not such as to reassure him. 
But the Queen’s loud presence at his side may well have helped 
to goad him into action. However, this latest proof of Bentinck’s 
inflexibility swayed the King: he appeared to give way to his 
son’s views. But he did no more than suspend his obstruction: 
he did not commit himself to outright approval of the proposed 
constitution. The Queen went further in her sudden reversal. 
Persuaded, apparently, by Moliterno of the overriding ad- 
vantages of an expedition to Naples, by June 15th she was 
writing to her son in favour of the English Constitution for 
Sicily and a constitutional settlement for anything they might 
acquire on the Continent. “A most sensible letter,’ Bentinck 
thought; ‘... it was much to be regretted that she could talk 
and write so well and acted always so ill. Her misfortune is to 
have all sense but common sense.”* 
One person was not at all pleased with Aci’s plan—Bentinck. 
To him both the English Constitution and (just then) the 
proposed expedition came as unwelcome surprises. He was still 
ready to use the prospect of an expedition as a bait and so get 
the Royal Family’s consent to a Sicilian Parliament and Consti- 
tution; but he thought Aci was ‘pushing’ them too much, and 
he did not wish the Patriots to use his own influence to impose 
on the Crown the independence of Sicily and the English 
Constitution ‘as if I had insisted on it which I had not and when 
I even thought it would not suit them’. Independence might 
be good for Sicily, but not necessarily for Naples, Italy, or 
Britain; he had no powers to secure it. As for the constitution, 
he told Belmonte, 
‘I was very sorry that they had adopted the English Constitution 
which no one understood or could define. ... It would cast a ridicule 
upon their measures. . . . I infinitely preferred his plan which was founded 
upon the principles of the English Constitution.’ 
So slavish a copy might be dangerous as well as absurd—‘some 
leading principles might be omitted by mistake, an omission 
which might be fatal to their liberties by being construed into 
an intention’. He greeted the ‘translation’ by Cassaro’s son with 

<<<PDFPAGE 69>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
53 
sarcastic congratulations; when Aci read him ‘the leading princi- 
ples of the British Constitution as compiled from Delolme, 
Pinkerton(?) [sic] Blackstone in the course of two days’, he 
refused to pronounce on them—'I was no legiste’. But in the 
end he relented. Apparently Balsamo came to the rescue by 
putting together yet another draft which followed the English 
system but modified it to suit Sicilian conditions—and Balsamo 
was much the most reliable draftsman at hand. Anyhow when 
Bentinck wrote home he had so far changed his mind as to say: 
‘I must confess that at first I 
was very much against the adoption of the 
English Constitution. I doubted very much whether the people have 
sufficient steadiness, wisdom or virtue to execute it. I was however much 
better disposed to it when I saw that no other plan presented so few 
difficulties; and my fears were very much lessened when I read the outline 
of a Constitution formed upon that of England, but with great moderation 
and wisdom modified and adapted to the debased state of this society, 
by certain salutary restraints upon the liberty of the Press, and by sus- 
pending for the moment the Trial by Jury.’ 
For this great work he gave credit to Castelnuovo and Bel- 
monte, ‘men who would do honour to the most enlightened 
and virtuous nation’.* 
Did Bentinck really believe all this? Or had he perhaps made 
up his mind to stand by the new ‘system’ even though an im- 
portant element was brought into it against his better judgment? 
At all events his enthusiasm did him no good: by commending 
the English Constitution (however modified) he implanted in 
the minds of Ministers at home the notion that it was all his own 
work; and so enabled them later on to ‘cast a ridicule’ on his 
mission. 
Aci’s other main proposal—the expedition to Italy—was one 
which Bentinck himself had often talked of since his arrival; 
he had used it effectively as an inducement to reform in Sicily. 
But coming from the Prince at this particular moment the 
proposal was most embarrassing. Bentinck certainly wanted 
nothing so much as to liberate Italy. But the British Government 
had allowed him only to look into the possibility of doing so, 
and had told him to move only after consulting with Wellington 
and with themselves. Finding, on his return to Sicily in De- 
cember 1811, disappointingly little evidence that Italy was ripe 

<<<PDFPAGE 70>>>
54 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
for revolt, Bentinck told Liverpool so in a despatch of January 
25th. To him and to Wellington he suggested various uses to 
which part’ of his troops might meanwhile be put; one of them 
was making a temporary diversion in Catalonia. Nothing could 
have suited Wellington better; and Liverpool ordered Bentinck 
on March 4th to carry out the diversion, provided that the 
Sicilian situation allowed it and Italy remained quiescent. On 
May 17th Bentinck decided to send 6,000 men to Spain, though 
by then he would rather have kept them on hand; ‘we all think’, 
he noted, ‘that this force will do no good’. But Murat’s departure 
for the Russian campaign had left Sicily in safety; and Italy 
continued disappointing. The Archduke Francis’ plan to raise 
and liberate the country depended on slow and tenuous commu- 
nications with Vienna via the Balkans; months passed and 
nothing was done. Overtures to the Italian Patriots were more 
easily thought of than made: ‘I am taught to believe’, com- 
plained one of Bentinck’s agents, ‘that there is no more commu- 
nication with Italy than with China.’ Overtures from Italy there 
were none. As Bentinck told the Archduke, 
‘Le malheur est que I’Italie est trop humiliée par la police francaise. 
Elle ne fait aucune démonstration de cette décision sans laquelle sa cause 
n’intéressera jamais beaucoup le public anglais. Ils sont mécontents sans 
doute, et deviendraient puissans contre les Francais. Mais ils sont passifs. 
Je ne recois aucune proposition de l’'Italie, une circonstance que ne 
marque ni une inquiétude trés pénible avec 
leurs souffrances, ni le 
caractére de Grandeur d’ Ame qu’on a vu dans les Espagnols. Ce sont les 
plaintes d’esclaves plutét que les murmures d’un peuple magnanime qui 
veut vaincre ou mourir pour la liberté et l’indépendence.”* 
Bentinck wrote this in October, and it expressed the accumu-- 
lated disappointment of several months. At the beginning of June, 
when the Prince proposed an expedition, he was still fairly 
hopeful. None the less he now found himself committed to 
acting in Catalonia; and he had no evidence from Italy that 
would justify a change of plan. Just as the Court was ready to 
cooperate he could not. Perplexed, he asked for time.* 
At this very moment, decision came to Palermo in the shape 
of one Lucius Concannon, an Irishman connected with the 
Archduke’s party, who had travelled from Vienna by way of 

<<<PDFPAGE 71>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
55 
Italy. He reported the country to be at a high pitch of discontent, 
ready to rise for religion, peace, an end to conscription, and a 
British alliance—‘That cry from Venice to Naples... would 
not leave one Frenchman alive in three weeks . . .’. Switzerland 
and Tyrol were also ripe for revolt. Riper than any were the 
Neapolitans, but they would bear anything sooner than the 
return of the Bourbons. Concannon’s enthusiastic reports might 
normally have made little impression on Bentinck; in his present 
dilemma, however, they seemed to justify that change of plan 
which he personally inclined to. Even so Bentinck was not 
going to take so much upon himself unless he could run the 
expedition on his own terms. These he put to the Prince in an 
interview of June 8th—he had, indeed, mentioned most of 
them in earlier exchanges, but only hypothetically. Now he 
told the Prince outright that ‘the only chance of success was to 
declare at once to the people that the deliverance of Italy’ (and 
not merely of Naples) ‘from the French yoke was our sole 
object. The choice of a constitution and a chief must be left to 
the free will of the people’. He would neither mention the 
Royal Family in his proclamations nor allow any of them to 
accompany their own forces; for, he said, to thrust them upon 
Naples from the first would alienate the people. All that Ben- 
tinck would concede to the Prince, either then or later in the 
month, was that he would ‘do all in his power to persuade the 
Neapolitans to accept the Hereditary Prince for their sovereign’, 
and that he would take no other prince—meaning the Archduke 
or Orleans—with him to Italy. Bentinck’s terms naturally dis- 
mayed the Prince, though they were not entirely news to him; 
still he did not at first reject them.* Bentinck accordingly counter- 
manded the Catalan expedition on June 9th. 
In doing so he exposed himself for the first time to the charge 
that he had disregarded his instructions without sufficient cause. 
Wellington was of course much annoyed at the news, and so 
were Liverpool and Mulgrave. Bathurst, however—who was 
Bentinck’s only friend in the Cabinet—was more indulgent. 
Apparently he was not averse from Bentinck’s Italian plans in 
principle, and thought a descent on Venice a promising possi- 
bility. His Under-Secretary, Henry Bunbury—an old Sicilian 
hand—was more favourable still: if Russia held, he thought, 

<<<PDFPAGE 72>>>
56 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
‘the time is not far distant when we shall see all the nations 
from the Lake of Constance to the sea of Malta up in arms 
against Buonaparte’. Between them they passed over Bentinck’s 
step as an exercise of discretion which was permissible if ill- 
judged. Castlereagh, for his part, warned Bentinck privately 
that the Government would not hear of his encouraging the 
Neapolitans to ‘elect’ Ferdinand, whose recall, “however volun- 
tary on the part of his continental subjects, should be in fact a 
restoration rather than an election’. To which Bentinck replied 
that the Neapolitans would have resisted a plain restoration: 
‘I... thought that the expedition proposed to me could not succeed 
without the cooperation of the people and that this cooperation could 
not be obtained without the inducement of getting rid of the French 
Government and of substituting Freedom for Tyranny. Upon my own 
responsibility I can proceed on no other grounds... though the con- 
dition required seemed contrary to the King’s interest, yet in my opinion 
it was directly the reverse.’ 
Of Bentinck’s wider visions of Italian independence and self- 
determination Castlereagh, it may be noted, said nothing.* 
Meanwhile the prospect of an expedition, however stiff the 
terms that went with it, was enough to keep the Royal Family 
attached to the English Constitution. More than ever it seemed 
the one safe haven in the storm of constitutional speculation 
which then prevailed at Palermo. On the very eve of the opening 
of Parliament the Prince took fright at an anonymous paper 
proposing that Sicily should adopt part of the new Spanish 
Constitution. This he thought ‘the precursor of democracy and 
revolution’; he refused to open Parliament unless Bentinck 
pledged himself to see that the new constitution did not differ 
from the English. Bentinck objected that ‘this would have 
committed me directly and personally in the affairs of the 
Parliament and of the Government, which I have carefully 
avoided’. He did, however, sign a guarantee of his support to 
the legitimate exercise of the Prince’s authority against any 
possible disorder. The statement concluded: 
‘T certainly would not undertake to establish by force and against the 
will of the people even that Constitution which to my conviction were 

<<<PDFPAGE 73>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
$7 
the best. Y.R.H. knows that I have had no part either in the recommen- 
dation or selection of the British Constitution. Y.R.H. has the sole merit 
of deciding upon a Constitution which agreeing in many respects with 
the Constitution of Sicily, and sprung as it were from the same root, 
does promise under salutary modifications to confer the greatest degree 
of happiness and prosperity. ... The period with all its circumstances is 
truly glorious to the Prince and to the People.’ 
This statement, by eliciting which the Prince as usual hoped 
to escape responsibility, later served Bentinck as a pretext for the 
assertion of his own power; it also started a popular legend that 
Britain had in fact guaranteed the Constitution. For the moment 
it reassured the Prince. Parliament met on June 18th, and the 
Speech from the Throne pointed to the English Constitution 
as the fount of wise and moderate legislation.* 
But the honeymoon was brief. Bentinck knew he had taken 
a risk in deciding on an Italian instead of a Spanish expedition. 
Opinion among his military and naval colleagues in the Medi- 
terranean command was divided. All agreed that Italy was in 
principle a much more fruitful battleground than Catalonia; 
and some shared his hopes of independence for the whole of 
Italy. But 
Pellew, 
Maitland 
and 
his second-in-command 
McFarlane warned him that only the certainly of a resounding 
success could justify his disregard of instructions—a certainty 
which his Italian intelligence was too flimsy to establish. Then 
Nugent reported from Spain that the authorities there and at 
home were quite set on the Catalan expedition; they would 
not, he thought, take kindly to Bentinck’s about-turn. Even 
this advice—so Bentinck later said—would not have swayed 
him if the Court had gone with him all the way; but after some 
hesitation they found his terms too much to swallow and re- 
jected them. He accordingly changed his mind once more, and 
decided on June 28th to send the troops to Spain after all. They 
sailed at once under Maitland.* 
Thus the whole raison d’étre for the Crown’s acceptance of 
the English Constitution fell to the ground. As a result the 
Prince threw himself back into the arms of his parents, and 
appeared openly as what he was, ‘the King’s Prime Minister’, 
their differences made up. He began to complain of Bentinck’s 
interference; government business came practically to a stand- 

<<<PDFPAGE 74>>>
58 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
still; he took advice from Circello and other relics of the past 
regime; Parliament met no more after the State opening. The 
Royal Family fell back on the policy which had served them in 
1810, that of playing off the aristocratic against the progressive 
section of the patriotic party. The King let it be known that he 
was ready to revert to the Sicilian Constitution as it had stood 
at the beginning of his reign; that is to say, he would give up 
all the ‘enlightened’ legislation of the Neapolitan Viceroys, 
all the inroads made on the power of the nobility, all the fiscal 
reforms the Crown had attempted, for the sake of averting the 
present danger of revolutionary innovation. He did add that 
if innovation there must be, he would prefer the English 
Constitution to any other; but this was plainly a second-best. 
In order to convert Parliament to his proposals the King set 
about forming a party through the Neapolitan Archbishop of 
Palermo and through Cassaro, whose fears for feudal privilege 
had re-emerged. By juggling with proxies and by persuasion 
both sides in the conflict attempted to make sure of a majority 
when and if Parliament met.* 
This volte-face destroyed what little was left of Bentinck’s 
regard for the Prince, and of his early conciliatory attitude. 
Delay, he decided, was the cause: had he forced a meeting of 
Parliament in January, none of this would have happened: 
‘T have done violence to my own judgment and feelings in endeavour- 
ing to attain the objects of my instructions by the means of conciliation 
only. I regret the sacrifices of great advantages to the common cause 
which have arisen from this undeserved delicacy. I am not ashamed to 
confess that I have been deceived, because when acting for a great and 
magnanimous nation, I should have been sorry that from any illiberal 
construction of the motives of this court I had for a moment abused the 
power committed to my charge. Every act and advice on my part has 
been most sincerely and anxiously intended to promote [the Prince’s] 
own honour and greatness in the eyes of his own subjects and of the 
world. It has been all in vain. And I am now in the decided conviction 
that fear and nothing but fear can direct a mind which many believe to 
be as false and cunning as it is certainly weak.’* 
Bentinck now counterattacked on all fronts. In a Note he 
urged that Parliament should be reassembled. He made the 
Ministers tender their resignations. He suspended the subsidy 

<<<PDFPAGE 75>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
59 
on the ground that the reorganisation of the army, promised 
since January, had still not begun. These, he admitted, were 
negative weapons, ‘but I could do no more and with respect to 
the Ministers, although I could not name those who should be 
appointed, yet I could do as much by objecting to such as might 
be offered’. To Belmonte’s qualms he replied that ‘the more 
embarrassment there was the better’. He warned Cassaro and 
through him the Archbishop that ‘the Constitution must be 
carried—that was my business—It must be done either by the 
Parliament or by force’. The Prince tried to stand firm on the 
army question; but Bentinck returned to the charge, rubbing 
in Sicily’s need for British help; he urged the Prince to follow 
the example of Portugal by abandoning an ambiguous attitude 
which might seem ‘a lurking hatred, too weak to refuse obli- 
gations, too ungenerous to acknowledge them’. The present 
Sicilian army, he said, was a drag on Britain, even a danger; the 
subsidy treaties had not foreseen ‘a case... by which the one 
buys the privilege of defending the other at the price of £400,000 
a year, and is repaid by enmity and insecurity’. Finally, he 
threatened that if Britain resumed 
‘the right which the bad faith of the high Personage who directed the 
preceding administration has given [her] of adopting the most extreme 
measures, a right waived from mere respect and confidence in Your 
Royal Highness’ upright intentions, she will stand acquitted before God 
and man for preferring an open enemy to such a friend, the most active 
hostility to such an alliance’. 
‘Quite a declaration of war’, the Prince thought it; but he had 
to bend before the storm. The Ministers’ resignations were not 
accepted; and a meeting of Parliament was called for 20 July. 
On that date, in the course of an all-night sitting, Parliament 
passed fifteen fundamental articles or ‘Bases’ of the new Consti- 
tution. These were taken mainly from the draft in which 
Balsamo had sought to distil the quintessence of the English 
system while modifying it to suit the ‘debased state’ of Sicily. 
But Parliament made changes in the draft which shewed that 
the chosen model would not escape unscathed. On the contrary, 
it already faced a double challenge from conservative and 
‘democratic’ forces. 

<<<PDFPAGE 76>>>
60 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
The Bases established the separation of powers, with Parlia- 
ment taking to itself, besides the sole power of taxing and 
legislating (subject to the Royal Assent), that of trying and 
punishing Ministers, officials and magistrates. The three houses 
were reduced to two—Peers and Commons—the temporal and 
spiritual peers being merged into one house, and deprived of 
their plural votes; money bills were to originate in the Com- 
mons; and Parliament was to meet annually. No 
Sicilian could 
be deprived of liberty or property without due process of law. 
The articles where Parliament departed from Balsamo’s draft 
were those concerning finances and feudal rights. Article X 
gave the Royal Domain to the nation, in exchange for a Civil 
List, and vested the administration of the nation’s property and 
revenue in Parliament. This was the result of “democratic 
feeling, especially in the Braccio Demaniale, and ran counter to 
the Ministers’ opinions—Castelnuovo in particular objected that 
it would make him, the Minister of Finance, ‘a Comptroller 
only’. Articles XII and XIII abolished all feudal dues, juris- 
dictions, and privileges, and converted all fiefs into alods. But 
an amendment of Cassaro’s was carried by the barons—Bel- 
monte, Castelnuovo, Aci and seven of their friends dissenting — 
which made the surrender of certain privileges, such as local 
tolls and monopolies, subject to compensation by the communes 
benefited. This amendment met with resistance in the Dema- 
niale: Belmonte, however, went down to the lower House; 
overcoming his own distaste for the measure which he had just 
voted against, by his eloquence he persuaded the Demaniale 
members to overcome theirs also and thus maintain a united 
front. In the Ecclesiastico the Archbishop of Palermo could 
muster only a little less than one-third of the members to vote 
against the extinction of their own Estate. The final article of 
the Bases looked forward to the filling out of the Constitution by 
Parliament along the lines of the English model.* 
The Bases of the Constitution, particularly those concerning 
feudal rights, have given rise to a question which is still debated 
—how far did they spring from a genuine surge of national and 
reformist sentiment, how far from the desire of the baronial 
class to maintain, perhaps even to increase, their power in the 
state? The question must be considered later on. The Bases did 

<<<PDFPAGE 77>>>
THE PROTECTORATE AND THE ‘BASES’ 
61 
hold the seeds of those differences over institutional and feudal 
matters which were later to wreck the Constitution, and class 
egoism certainly damped the Barons’ reforming zeal in the 
matter of compensation. All the same we can have little doubt 
that the great majority of parliamentarians sincerely and (at any 
rate) consciously believed they had sacrificed their particular 
interests to the general good of the nation. The sitting of July 
2oth was thus the high-water mark of the patriotic movement, 
and in the eyes of the participants it stood for the rebirth of 
Sicily. Bentinck wrote home exultantly: 
‘So strong an expression of national feeling is scarcely upon record . 
. . 
an unanimity almost without a precedent, and more than ever surprising 
upon an occasion when the ancient privileges and jurisdictions of families 
were to be resigned, when long cherished prejudices were to be sacrificed, 
and when the two more powerful orders of the State were called upon 
to give up their preponderancy, to unite themselves into one Chamber, 
and co-operate to confer upon the third an influence which it had never 
possessed. These things are really honourable to the Sicilians, and the 
exultation and hope which prevail are universal’. 
In a quieter strain, he confided to G.F. Leckie—in a sense the 
first father of British intervention in Sicily—his satisfaction, 
tempered though it was with regret that feudal privileges had 
not been abolished outright: 
‘The business is new to all, but notwithstanding there is a good deal 
of vanity and egoism among the great and that there will be a good deal 
of scrambling for loaves and fishes among all, yet there is a great portion 
of good sense, honour and patriotism, that I hope will keep the ship 
right, even should she be in danger. There is also at hand our independent 
protecting force that I trust will be able effectually to secure this infant 
Constitution from all mishap—and a very gratifying and proud duty 
this is.”* 
This was undoubtedly the culminating point of Bentinck’s 
mission: never afterwards could he feel so unmixed a satisfaction. 
The credit for this golden moment he gave to his Sicilian 
friends, to Belmonte in particular. But it was he who received 
the congratulations of his British friends—of Ambherst, for 
instance, who wrote that Bentinck had accomplished, without 

<<<PDFPAGE 78>>>
62 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the usual attendants of bloodshed and confusion, one of the 
most complete and important revolutions recorded in the history 
of our eventful times. There now opens indeed a fair prospect 
of happiness, as well as of political power, to the inhabitants of 
the rich and beautiful island of Sicily.’ 

<<<PDFPAGE 79>>>
GHA PIE 
RAITT 
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
‘A work of Herculean labour....It 
is the triumph of humanity 
and Reason over darkness, cruelty and oppression and stamps you the 
Benefactor of Mankind... you will enjoy the prayers of millions who 
will ultimately derive freedom and happiness from this distant example... . 
Thus Pellew, on hearing of the Bases of the Sicilian Consti- 
tution. That this was not the mere effusion of a politically naive 
sea-dog is shewn by the chorus of praise and encouragement 
which reached Bentinck from British observers, both after his 
first victory over the Court in January and after the voting of 
the Bases on July 20th. It was perhaps not surprising that the 
generals and admirals should expect Sicily to reap great and 
sudden benefits from the change—like nearly everyone else they 
believed in her potential. prosperity, which (they thought) only 
misrule had stifled, and only the Queen’s mischief still imperiled. 
Nor was it strange that they, like Bentinck, should see in 
‘regenerated’ Sicily a great accession of strength to the British 
cause—'the world may be saved by it’, exclaimed Pellew. No 
doubt these illusions were the outcome of a classical education. 
But it was noteworthy that the Prince Regent himself, according 
to one report, praised Bentinck’s conduct highly in private; it 
was, one supposes, unusual for the Under-Secretary of State to 
tell the envoy who had just revolutionised the country to which 
he was accredited: ‘I congratulate you du fond de mon cceur’. 
This enthusiasm for Bentinck’s achievement was not confined 
to one party; it sprang from the most disparate motives: from 
the Benthamism ofa pompous East India official who wrote that 
Bentinck had done ‘that for a sea-girt isle in the Mediterranean, 
which one of your ancestors assisted in doing for the island of 
your birth’; or from the forthright John Bull attitude of the 
Bishop of Limerick, who trusted that ‘that miserable country 
will now be... made sensible of its good fortune in being 
placed under the control and protection of the British Govern- 
ment’. 
What doubts and suspicions there were likewise cut across 
party lines. A Government supporter like Lord Sheffield disliked 

<<<PDFPAGE 80>>>
64 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
‘crusading with our Constitution’ no less than did Sir Robert 
Wilson, Grey’s ambassador-at-large, for whom Sicily was an 
example of ‘the march of usurpation in English policy’. A 
Cabinet Minister—Harrowby—thought Bentinck “Whig-mad’ 
on the evidence of the new Constitution, which of course he 
took to be Bentinck’s own work; while another Benthamite 
—Blaquiere—complained of the ‘overstrained moderation’ of 
Bentinck’s measures. Indeed, those who knew something of 
Sicily and agreed most 
closely with Bentinck—Ambherst, 
Maitland, Pellew—had had their doubts at first; they had seen 
some of the difficulties of the situation, and particularly the 
danger that Sicily might lapse into feudal reaction: ‘these proud 
barons’, they agreed, must be induced to ‘lay aside selfishness; 
and take the people (not the mob) for something’. But they, as 
well as Bentinck, were quite satisfied on this score by the reso- 
lutions of July 20th. Not so Leckie: he complained bitterly that 
Bentinck had been deceived; the ‘English’ Constitution was 
‘one of the greatest fallacies ever exhibited’; it would lead to an 
‘unbalanced oligarchy’ more oppressive to the mass of the 
people than the former despotic monarchy. Bentinck was sensi- 
tive to this charge, which French propaganda in Italy fastened 
on from the first, and he had it refuted in the official British 
paper: significantly, the article was written, not by a Sicilian, 
but by a professed agent of the Italian patriots, and described 
the Constitution as the symbol of a risorgimento, a raising up of 
the whole people to a better condition of life.* 
To Bentinck these tokens of British commendation or blame 
mattered less than the official opinion of his Government. From 
Harrowby’s outburst, already mentioned, and from Liverpool’s 
later strictures, it is plain that those Ministers who considered 
the matter at all had no liking for the new constitution: they 
were ready if need be to explain it away as the by-blow of 
Bentinck’s Whiggish enthusiasm. Castlereagh was more cau- 
tious: he had, in May, made constitutional reform one of 
Britain’s official aims in Sicily; and he told Bentinck in Sep- 
tember that 
‘under the corrections and alterations which some of the articles are 
susceptible of and appear to require, there is a fair prospect of Sicily 
holding out to Italy and to Naples the safe and honourable example of 

<<<PDFPAGE 81>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
65 
a reformation without violence, and a regenerated constitution, under 
the sanction and with the aid of the legitimate sovereign’. 
But there remained unresolved a lasting, and, one may say, 
a fatal ambiguity in Castlereagh’s thinking. A statement of his 
in a Commons debate of April 1813 illustrates this. Whitbread 
had asked him whether the new measures taken in Sicily at 
Britain’s suggestion were likely to give the people those political 
advantages which had been hoped for. ‘It was a very great 
question’, Castlereagh replied, ‘which had been put to him’; the 
constitution was still incomplete, but 
‘he was sanguine enough to hope great advantages from it. He trusted 
it would prove beneficial to the Sicilians and their posterity, and secure 
their attachment to this country... this country had never attempted 
to exercise such a controlling power in Sicily as would be inconsistent 
with the independence of its government. It had been the endeavour of 
Ministers to unite the feelings of the people, and embark their whole means 
of defence in the common cause.’ 
Thus the Sicilian constitution could be seen in three different 
ways: as the outcome of deliberate British policy; as the hobby- 
horse of a Whiggish proconsul; or as the spontaneous achieve- 
ment of the Sicilian people, with which Britain and her Minister 
had nothing to do. The trouble was that Castlereagh held all 
three views simultaneously, kept them all, as it were, in reserve; 
or if, as seems likely, this statement implies a greater and more 
calculated attention than he could give to Sicilian policy, one 
may say that he never thought out until too late which view 
the British Government should adopt. True, Sicily was far away 
and he had to leave Bentinck great latitude. But flexibility is 
one thing, letting matters take their course is another. That the 
Foreign Secretary should keep in mind, and prepare for, every 
eventuality was not the same thing as that he should scarcely 
realise or even inquire what his own policy was leading to.* 
Meanwhile the Bases were only a beginning: the Constitution 
had yet to be worked out in detail; and the attitude of the Royal 
Family was 
still uncertain. They had been defeated in the 
attempt to avert a new constitution; they must now be made to 
acquiesce in their own defeat. This acquiescence Bentinck and 

<<<PDFPAGE 82>>>
66 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the Ministers decided to obtain at once. Even Cassaro was with 
them in this: Bentinck, he realised, having guaranteed that 
Parliament should not disturb the peace, was now entitled to 
demand that the Crown should not drive Parliament into violent 
opposition. In fact Parliament already looked like getting out 
of hand: only a week after the voting of the Bases, Bentinck 
had, at Belmonte’s request, to go to the Demaniale lobby and 
urge moderation on the more radical members, who shewed 
signs of wanting to set themselves up as a Long Parliament of 
Sicily. Here Belmonte’s own brother, Ventimiglia, was one of 
the leaders and another member—Sperlinga—threatened, if the 
Royal Assent was withheld, to move for a parliamentary com- 
mittee to investigate the abuses of the previous regime. Bentinck 
and his friends, however, decided—partly as a sop to Cassaro, 
partly as a safeguard for the future—that the King, not the 
Prince, should give the Royal Assent to the Bases.* 
The King, however, proved stubborn. Possibly the current 
report was true that he and the Queen had countenanced the 
English Constitution in the first place only because they had 
never expected Parliament to adopt it—the clergy would never 
agree to abolish their own Estate. At any rate the King, when 
Cassaro first approached him, began by threatening to dissolve 
Parliament and refusing to sanction any of the articles. Cassaro 
pointed out that Parliament had kept fairly closely to the 
English Constitution: “but who wanted, he cried, the English 
Constitution?’ The Queen agreed that they did not. On August 
1st Bentinck himself saw the King for the first time since 
January. The King still said he would never sanction the Bases, 
‘mai mai mai’; he obviously thought the Parliament null and 
void, reform abhorrent, and Bentinck’s acts unauthorised. He 
shifted his ground, however: he would sanction the English 
Constitution “but this was neither carne nor pesce neither 
English nor Sicilian... he had not slept for 15 nights... his 
conversation was very much mixed with religion’. He tried to 
bargain with Bentinck—let Britain, he said, guarantee the safety 
of the whole Royal Family (meaning the Queen), as well as 
the Civil List—and ‘no man royal or common ever played the 
beggar better’. But he found Bentinck, as he put it, ‘like an 
inanimate statue of the hardest marble’, and the interview ended 

<<<PDFPAGE 83>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
67 
inconclusively. The King, however, was now isolated. On one
