side was the Queen, plagued as ever with visions of Maric- 
Antoinette’s scaffold: 
“YOU ARE LOST FINISHED 
DISHONOURED,’, she fairly screamed in one of the notes 
that passed between them at La Ficuzza (underlining the last 
word nine times), ‘if you accept this [instead of putting] an 
end to these infamous deeds of a government, of Baboons, and 
Scoundrels’: 
‘the Tears, the blood, the harm to subjects entrusted to you by God, 
you want dishonour, you may have it, but to ruin Subjects, family, all 
and end dishonoured after 60 years, while you had the people, and the 
troops, on your side, I repeat to you, conquer this criminal poltroonery, 
awake from criminal lethargy, go tomorrow to Palermo, take up your 
duty again, or if not, expect the contempt, curse of a sacrificed people, 
of your ruined family, so much my duty forces me to tell you, praying 
God that He may enlighten you, your desperate afflicted most unhappy 
wife.’* 
We should not underrate the effect of such outbursts on a 
man who liked a quiet life. But on the other side Cassaro urged 
the King to give way, and so did the wretched and flustered 
Prince, whom Bentinck’s thunderings on the army question had 
deprived of any will to resist. Even so the King stood his ground 
as long as he could; he urged his son to dissolve Parliament and 
summon another to choose between the old Constitution and 
the English. In the end all he would consent to was to scribble 
a note authorising the Prince to sanction such articles as ‘con- 
formed absolutely to the English Constitution’.* 
There was reason to fear that this was one of the King’s usual 
devices to avoid committing himself; but it is doubtful whether 
any oath or signature would have bound him. What did matter 
was that from then on he invariably insisted that the new 
constitution must not depart by one iota from the English. He 
probably had no very clear notion of what the English system 
was like—he later decided to send to England for an authori- 
tative version—but it was at any rate known or ascertainable; 
it would set bounds on innovation; and it was something to 
which Bentinck could not easily object. The Prince’s assent to 
the Bases was accordingly published on August roth. By agree- 

<<<PDFPAGE 84>>>
68 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
ment with Castelnuovo and Bentinck—who now attended an 
official meeting of the Council for the first time—the Prince 
vetoed the’ article concerning the Civil List and the adminis- 
tration by Parliament of the national revenue, and reserved 
judgement on the proposed compensation for feudal rights—this 
last in the hope that the Barons might later be induced to 
surrender their privileges outright.” 
But the difficulties over the Royal Assent marked only the 
first act in the conflict that summer between Bentinck and the 
King. The second centred round the army question. The Sicilian 
Army was still not reorganised, and therefore the subsidy was 
still suspended. Bentinck decided after eight months’ delay that 
the matter must be brought to a head. He had, on July 22nd, 
suggested a new and more binding Anglo-Sicilian treaty of 
alliance; he now demanded several specific measures. A Sicilian 
corps in British pay must be formed to serve outside Sicily—first 
in Spain, later if possible in Italy; then the Captain-General 
must be given greater powers, designed to get round Aci’s 
meddling; and finally the King’s Neapolitan Guards must be 
reduced and partly replaced by Sicilians. This last measure was 
aimed at the Queen, who supposedly pinned her hopes on the 
Guards’ support if the King should attempt to resume power. 
The negotiations over the proposed treaty dragged on through- 
out August and into September. Meanwhile the army could not 
be paid, and the government were left at one time with only 
three days’ bread and forage in hand. The Prince kept up his 
threats of resignation and his desperate appeals to his parents, 
but the King found his letters ‘impertinent’ and ‘incomprehensi- 
ble’, while the Queen denounced him as ‘a traitor, a revolution- 
ary!’ When the Prince tried to convince them, on the strength 
of fresh despatches from Castelcicala, that Bentinck had the full 
backing of the British Government, ‘the answer sent back was 
that Castelcicala was either a fool or a traitor. The Hereditary 
Prince cried out que diable puis-je faire’. The King wanted to 
deal separately with Bentinck through Cassaro and Caccamo; 
once again he tried to bargain for a guarantee of the pure 
English Constitution and of the Civil List. He fought especially 
hard to keep his Guards intact; but once again he could not 
resist indefinitely, and once again he left the Prince the responsi- 

<<<PDFPAGE 85>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
69 
bility of signing, on September 12th, a new treaty. This embod- 
ied: all of Bentinck’s demands, together with a British guarantee 
of the possession of Sicily—though not, as the Prince had 
wished, of Naples. The King might, and did, assert that the 
treaty was invalid without his own assent; he tried to undermine 
it through Castelcicala, who was much more his personal am- 
bassador in London than the Prince’s, let alone the government’s; 
but the treaty could not be undone.* 
Bentinck now had in fact complete control over the Sicilian 
army; in a moment of optimism he foresaw that as soon as 
Parliament finished its work of constitution-making all Sicilian 
problems would be solved, and he would be able to join his 
‘distant flock’—‘the best and dearest part of my army’—in 
Catalonia. To his Government he wrote that the new treaty 
‘may be said to have been made not only between the two 
sovereigns, but between the two nations, sealed by the unani- 
mous gratitude of the Sicilians, and indissolubly fixed in the 
feelings of political interest, of liberty and of independence 
common to both’. To his friend Bunbury he had written a few 
days earlier: “We are now masters and kings if you please.” 
Yet there was still one more act to come, and one more 
problem to be settled: the Queen’s interference. Ever. since 
January Bentinck had had to complain of delay and obstruction 
at almost every point: the new Ministry, the summoning and 
later the meeting of Parliament, the reorganisation of the army, 
the sanctioning of the Bases, the new treaty. The ostensible 
cause was the King’s stubbornness: as he told the Prince: 
‘His Majesty, who had before renounced all business, now insisted 
upon seeing every paper, and directed every measure till at length the 
Government exhibited the singular spectacle of an ostensible head without 
any power, unable to decide upon any question, without the previous 
permission of a superior; and that superior at a distance from the capital, 
in no communication with the leading persons of the country, unac- 
quainted with the temper and circumstances of the nation, and viewing 
them through the false medium of the passions and prejudices of the 
single person in whom he reposed confidence.’ 
The Queen, as usual, seemed the real source of the trouble. 
Everyone said so: the Prince repeated that ‘if his mother was 
gone the King would be easily reconciled’; the King himself 

<<<PDFPAGE 86>>>
7O 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
*. 
had hinted something of the kind in the interview of August Ist; 
Cassaro and Caccamo kept saying that he really wished to send 
her away, but could not withstand her influence—‘if the King 
gave an opinion which the Queen disapproved’—Cassaro re- 
ported during the treaty negotiations—‘she gave him a nod and 
said that it went for a non-dit’. Cassaro also said the Queen’s 
emissaries were intriguing for fictitious protests against the new 
Constitution to be sent to Palermo from the provinces; a reputed 
agent of hers had recently thrown a bomb into the Parliament 
building; and rumour, as ever, had her plotting for the King’s 
return to power.” 
Bentinck therefore felt he had ample grounds for demanding 
her utter removal from the political scene, taking up the question 
where he had left off in April. Occasion served him: he had 
received at the end of August Castlereagh’s instruction giving 
him complete discretion in matters concerning the Queen; and 
she herself had made a fatal slip during the interview of August 
tst—when she and Bentinck had met face to face for the last 
time—by saying, in her distraction, that she meant to go to 
Vienna, and by asking for a frigate to take her away. In subse- 
quent weeks she announced and cancelled her departure several 
times. Bentinck was of course all for it; but ‘desirable as this 
event might be’, he felt “very great reluctance to enforce it—at 
the Queen’s time of life, in the state of her health, and at this 
advanced season such an act might appear cruel’. In the letter to 
the King with which he opened his attack on September 13th, 
he accordingly referred only to ‘positive orders’ from his govern- 
ment which made it imperative that the Queen should remove 
to a greater distance from Palermo—that is, by implication, 
that she should part from her husband. It was the resistance this 
demand met with that raised Bentinck’s terms to include, first 
a verbal, then a written promise by the King of her departure 
from Sicily altogether.* 
For this act ran its course like the other two—the limit of the 
King’s powers of resistance seems to have been a month or six 
weeks; this time he did not give in until October 25th. The 
tedious and familiar events recurred—the letters flying back 
and forth; the proofs tendered and refused; the Prince’s en- 
treaties; the King’s stubbornness; the Queen’s fury; the am- 

<<<PDFPAGE 87>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISR’ 
71 
biguous shuttling to and fro of Cassaro and Caccamo, the 
Rosencrantz and Guildenstern of Sicily. As before, the Queen’s 
debts complicated the issue. Who was to pay them if she left 
Sicily ? 
When, somewhat later, Bentinck suggested that the King 
might, the Prince made a face “expressive of the opinion that he 
would not give her a farthing’. Bentinck himself had no au- 
thority to pay them, and in any case he thought power, not 
money, was the Queen’s object. Another difficulty was the way 
the King and Queen fastened on Castelcicala’s reports from 
London—and in particular on what Castlereagh and the Prince 
Regent had said about the Queen. Castlereagh had told the 
Sicilian Ambassador, and was still telling him at that time, that 
the Queen ought to go away—perhaps not out of Sicily alto- 
gether, though he could promise nothing; but he was sure 
Bentinck would ‘act with all due delicacy and forbearance’; 
the notion that force might be used against her was repugnant. 
The Regent had dropped some well-meaning remark to the 
effect that the Queen might come into Palermo now and again 
to see her family. The King and Queen took all this to mean 
that Bentinck had no powers to use force in the last resort: his 
threats and demands could therefore be discounted.* Faced with 
this opposition, Bentinck did contemplate the use of force: 
‘...it was impossible for me to recede. My influence and 
authority depended upon the result’, On October 8th he con- 
sidered sending troops to the neighbourhood of La Ficuzza and 
facing the King with the choice of abdicating or giving in; he 
even got Belmonte to draw up a form of abdication. If need 
be he was ready to expel the Queen bodily from the island, 
should the King refuse either alternative. After a further flurry 
of letters and talks Bentinck rode up to La Ficuzza on the rsth, 
with the form of abdication in his pocket; but the King would 
not see him, for fear of an attack of bile, and Bentinck had to 
deal with Father Caccamo. 
‘The question must be decided,’ he told him; ‘I had been burlato for 
the last six months by the Queen’s continual declaration that she would 
go away... I had always known she would not and I told him now 
that until I came at the head of a British force she never would.’ 
He therefore gave the King an ultimatum: if he did not hear

from him by the 1oth, ‘I should consider myself to be at liberty 
to do what I pleased’. 
. 
The King gave way: Circello was called out of retirement; he 
and Cassaro together persuaded the Queen to go to Santa 
Margherita, in the southwestern part of Sicily, for the winter, 
and to announce her intention of going to Vienna in the spring. 
Negotiations went on for ten more days over the wording of 
the written promise which Bentinck now demanded from the 
King: Bentinck wanted the word ‘assurance’ to be included, 
while the King wished him to be merely ‘informed’ of the 
Queen’s decision. Bentinck won in the end; even then the 
‘assurance’ was signed, not by the King, but—according to true 
Bourbon practice—by Cassaro and Circello in his name.* 
Bentinck was exasperated by this continuous succession of crises: 
‘T have found myself in very great embarrassment about the Queen,’ 
he told Bathurst. ‘I had quite made up my mind to take her away by 
force, fully convinced that, if the British Government would not support 
me, it was better for this country and for our own that I should rather be 
sacrificed than she allowed to remain.... They cannot resist decided 
language, and it is a pity that what has been so easily and so justly done 
should have been left undone for six years. The Hereditary Prince said 
to Belmonte the other day, il n’a qu’d montrer ses dents et ils (the King and 
Queen) cederont tout de suite. I have done my best to keep up all the forms 
and have sacrificed to the desired appearance of conciliation real and 
substantial advantages. If I had been empowered at first to have said 
‘this must be’, I could have arranged in one month what has occupied 
a year, what has deprived Spain of the aid of many thousand men, and 
what has given to Sicily a much worse and more impracticable Consti- 
tution than she would otherwise have had. . . . I trust that the Government 
will not relax in anything, even the least trifle, respecting the final 
arrangement that I may make with the Queen. The way is not smooth at 
all... . There will always be abundant means with which she can put in 
practice her wicked and artful designs. She is I think now completely 
beat, if 1 
am supported.’ 
At last Bentinck could look forward to leaving for Catalonia, 
where his staff officers were steadily clamouring for him to 
come and take charge. Now that the Queen was ‘beat’ he might 
be able before leaving to settle the whole internal Sicilian 
situation and even (he began to think) to reconcile the King 
to the Constitution.” 

<<<PDFPAGE 89>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
73 
Parliament and the Constitution had, indeed, been constant 
sources of concern to Bentinck throughout this series of diplo- 
matic crises. We have seen that in his opinion delay had produced 
a constitution much more impracticable than it need have been. 
In the same letter to Bathurst he had to complain, of the very 
Parliament to which three months earlier he had given such 
high praise, that there was ‘but a very moderate proportion of 
public spirit among them’. Experience had taught him that a 
Constitution octroyée would have done better after all: ‘at first all 
would have received with thanks whatever had been proposed, 
but by delay cabal and intrigue has got ahead and the Parliament 
has been entirely for a long time past out of all control’.* How 
had this come about? 
The Parliamentary session of 1812, during which the Consti- 
tution was worked out, lasted from July 20th to 
November 4th; 
it revealed dissensions among the Patriots which, by choosing 
to adopt the English Constitution, they had only glossed over. 
Dividing lines were not always clear. There was, on institutional 
questions, 
the old split between, 
on 
the one 
hand, the 
conservative barons and their friends, led by Cassaro and the 
Archbishop of Palermo, and attached to baronial, ecclesiastical, 
sometimes to royal privileges; and on the other hand the strictly 
‘constitutional’ majority led by Belmonte and Castelnuovo. But 
these “constitutionalists’ were not always united; the new radical 
group which had emerged in the May elections to the Dema- 
niale, the Ventimiglias and Sperlingas, sometimes led them into 
measures which the Ministers disapproved, or else broke away 
altogether. More important, on the feudal question there began 
to appear a split between Belmonte and Castelnuovo, which was 
to grow in subsequent months and eventually to wreck the 
movement altogether. 
The split originated partly in personal jealousies among the 
lesser members, and partly in differences of opinion and tempera- 
ment between the two leaders themselves. Belmonte was bril- 
liant and fertile in expedients, but—as Bentinck gradually came 
to see—he was not the man to be Prime Minister except in his 
own fancy. He had little notion of Ministerial leadership in 
Parliament: ‘when he passed the doors of that House’, he told 
the Barons, ‘he forgot that he was the Minister of the Prince. 

<<<PDFPAGE 90>>>
74 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
He came there as a Sicilian Baron and his duty was to his coun- 
try.’ This attitude—aristocratic at bottom, however veneered 
over with liberal ideas—was one cause of friction with the more 
systematic Castelnuovo. Early in the session Orleans complained 
that, partly through ill-health, partly through his princely neg- 
lect of details, Belmonte was losing his grip on his followers; 
after the first few weeks he attended Parliament less and less 
often, until towards the end he scarcely turned up at all. He 
was just as nonchalant in matters of day-to-day administration: 
‘business was not his fort’, Orleans complained; ‘he would 
neither write nor read. His delight was in lounging and conver- 
sation.’ Besides, he was as usual quite unable to impose unity on 
his colleagues, and all too ready to fall back on Bentinck for 
support. Bentinck and Orleans would meet and shake their heads 
together over their friend’s shortcomings: 
his vanity, they 
agreed, blinded him to the dangers of the situation; though he 
was still supremely confident of his own ability to manage 
everyone and everything, Prince and Parliament, his followers 
were practically out of hand and ‘nobody [was] so humbugged 
by the Hereditary Prince as Belmonte himself’. 
Then it was that Bentinck began to prize Belmonte less as a 
politician, and Castelnuovo more. Castelnuovo had the virtues 
of Belmonte’s defects; but he had adopted ‘the unalterable 
system of not giving in to anyone or on any issue when he 
believed truth and reason to lie on his side’. This was shewn in 
his management of the finances: moved (as the Prince put it) 
by ‘a morality too philosophique’, he refused at one time to 
pay out the army credits, on the ground that the State’s debts 
were not yet all discharged: until that was done, ‘there were no 
credits’. On that occasion and on others he tried to resign and 
had to be dissuaded by his friends. Bentinck thought him 
‘wrong-headed’ in this: still ‘it would be a great pity to lose 
him... a man of the same character for integrity and firmness 
was not to be found—I regretted in some respects his inflexibility 
—If I could remake him, I would alter him, but as he was he 
must be taken and he was the man of all others calculated to 
establish the public credit.’* 
As the split between Castelnuovo and Belmonte became more 
obvious, Bentinck took to lecturing them and the other Ministers 

<<<PDFPAGE 91>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
75 
on the need for solidarity. They ought to realise that ‘one 
department was inseparably connected with another’. The 
Government, he complained, ‘has in fact no power and energy 
—they are afraid of the people—the Parliament is the same—they 
all want character’. Castelnuovo was too austere to be popular, 
while Belmonte “did not see the necessity of collecting round 
him a strong party—the consequence was that the Parliament 
was under no guidance and were doing what they pleased’. In 
fact the whole political system was far too fluid: ‘there must be 
two parties—those who had the places—and those who had not 
and wanted them...’. But if the idea of a strict two-party 
system was new enough in England, it was quite unthinkable in 
Sicily: Bentinck might grumble, but he could not alter the 
existing confusion.* 
In the proceedings of Parliament Bentinck seldom interfered 
directly. He did once or twice, at Belmonte’s invitation, lecture 
the more intemperate members—they of course protested that 
‘they wished to do nothing disagreeable’ to him, but little came 
of it. One member of the Demaniale was a paid British agent, 
and as ‘he was supposed to speak my sentiments’ Bentinck 
arranged that they should meet to concert his attitude to specific 
measures. But on the whole Bentinck confined himself to giving 
advice in conversation with members and Ministers; in fact the 
constitutional leaders complained that he was too reticent and 
did not interfere enough.” Only at the end of the session did he 
throw his weight into the scales: and then on a matter which, 
typically, stirred Belmonte and Orleans all the more because it 
touched their interests rather than their principles. 
At the end of October the Demaniale passed the new Civil 
List: after the usual fashion of Civil Lists it provided separately 
for each adult member of the Royal Family. But when the bill 
went to the two upper Estates some members threatened to 
amend it so as to give the whole of the Civil List to the King 
and let him look after the distribution. Cassaro and the Arch- 
bishop of Palermo, apparently, were behind this move, together 
with a boon companion of the King’s, the Prince of Pantelleria; 
and their inspiration could be traced easily enough to La 
Ficuzza. All this quickly threw Orleans into high alarm (then 
as later nothing moved Louis-Philippe like money); the Prince, 

<<<PDFPAGE 92>>>
76 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
too, faced with the prospect of being kept in leading-strings by 
his parents, became ‘very reasonable’ and talked of proroguing 
Parliament ‘on some pretext before the fatal amendment could 
be carried; his sister Marie-Amélie, for her part, was ‘very much 
montée... sure if left to the King and Queen they would get 
nothing’. Of course they all looked to Bentinck for help; and 
Belmonte automatically complained that Bentinck had not 
interfered even though he himself, by neglecting Parliament and 
allowing dissension to grow between his followers and Castel- 
nuovo’s, had given Cassaro and the Archbishop their chance. 
Nor was this all: not interest merely but principle also agitated 
Parliament. The Demaniale claimed—mistakenly, for the Eng- 
lish Constitution was not yet in force—that the other two 
Estates had no right to amend money bills, and it sent the 
Barons a message to say so; at which the Barons were furious 
and drew up a counter-remonstrance. Bentinck, visited by one 
after another of these men with a grievance, was annoyed: ‘the 
fault’, he told Belmonte, “had been that the Ministers had not 
been united. They opposed each other in the House—I could 
only support them as an administration, it being their business 
to lead the House.’ Still, if they could gain time ‘I was sure I 
could obtain a majority’. This he set about doing by means 
both informal and official, He argued with Cassaro, who 
hastened to put the blame on Pantelleria but still seemed afraid 
to offend the King; he tackled Butera, the wayward but influ- 
ential first baron of Sicily; on Belmonte’s advice he wrote a 
Note opposing the amendment, which the Prince could lay 
before Parliament (the grounds for this unwonted interference 
being that changes in the Civil List would affect the Army 
supply). Meanwhile Lady William Bentinck had canvassed 
Princess Cassaro on Marie-Amélie’s behalf. Forty-eighthours after 
the original alarm Parliament met in the evening and sat until 
3 p.m. on the following day; Butera made a long speech against 
the amendment in the Militare; the Civil List was finally carried 
according to the original draft; and so the situation was saved.* 
The chief matters for dispute in the three months’ debate on 
the new Constitution were: the powers of the legislative and 
executive branches; the freedom of the press; judicial and ad- 
ministrative reform; and, most important, whether in the back- 

<<<PDFPAGE 93>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
77 
ground or in the open, the abolition of feudal rights, in particular 
the question of entail. The one major question on which all 
were united was, as before, the independence of Sicily.* 
English Constitution or no, it became clear early in the session 
that Parliament meant to depart from it—and from Balsamo’s 
draft—over the executive and legislative powers. True, Anglo- 
mania was as strong as ever: members paid lip-service to the 
English model and used it as a justification, claiming that unless 
the executive was hemmed in on every side it would reduce 
Sicily to slavery ‘like England in the time of Henry VIII’.* But 
their motives were plain fear of the Crown; in some quarters, 
a new interest in French and Spanish constitutional doctrine; 
in others a desire to preserve the financial and administrative 
control which the Barons had exercised through the old 
Deputation. For these reasons Parliament adopted once again 
the article (vetoed out of the Bases by the Prince) which vested 
the Crown lands and revenues in the nation. This time their 
administration was left, on Castelnuovo’s advice, to the execu- 
tive; but the spirit if not the letter of parliamentary dictatorship 
prevailed, so that when the time came to vote supply, Castel- 
nuovo as a matter of course did no more than present his ac- 
counts, leaving it to Parliament to distribute taxation. In the 
same way Parliament, while it adopted the structure and pro- 
cedure of Westminster wholesale, interpreted the Commons’ 
right of arrest, trial and impeachment rather in the spirit of 
17th century than of early 19th century English practice; it 
tended to follow the Spanish model in making nearly all 
Government servants ineligible; and the radicals would have 
made Parliament appoint to all offices. In the end Ministers 
were not, as in Spain, excluded; but once again the spirit of the 
new constitution was such that they had scarcely more initiative 
or authority than any other member. 
Still departing from 
Balsamo’s draft, and against the strong advice of Castelnuovo 
and Belmonte, Parliament lowered the property qualification 
for electors and candidates, and freed the municipal councils 
almost entirely from central control. None the less the Crown 
was given a real power of veto; so that formally the executive 
was no weaker than in England. What was missing was any 
notion of Cabinet government.” 

<<<PDFPAGE 94>>>
78 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
All this made Bentinck uneasy. He had no wish to see Parlia- 
ment enthroned as the arbiter of the new constitution. In fact 
he shewed ‘a moderate spirit rather at variance with the flam- 
boyant tone of some of his despatches. When, for instance, Aci 
argued with him that the Lockian doctrine of legitimate resist- 
ance to royal authority ought to be included in the Constitution, 
he replied that ‘there was no necessity to sanction those acts 
which were acts of revolution, perhaps necessary, by law’. He 
told Anjou—one of the five Barons arrested in 1811, now a 
prominent radical—that present fear of the Crown ought not 
to influence Parliament: 
‘They were now legislating for Posterity ... though there was now 
an interest different from that of their Nation, that must cease here- 
after ...a broad line must be drawn between Executive and Parlia- 
ment ...the Executive would not have the responsibility they ought 
and the Houses of Parliament would be a scene of perpetual intrigue.’ 
But ‘I did not convince him’.* 
Bentinck’s attitude was much the same on the other two 
institutional questions which agitated Parliament, the Press law 
and the reform of the judicial and administrative system. He had 
originally approved, because of ‘the debased state of this society’, 
the restrictions placed on the freedom of the Press in Balsamo’s 
draft. But when the radical group in the Demaniale voted to 
make the Press quite free, and when, after a conflict with the 
Ecclesiastico, 
a compromise was reached freeing all but religious 
publications, he did not object: not because he approved the 
principle—he apparently did not—but because it seemed as well 
to let Parliament have its way unchecked and uncriticised in 
matters not of the first import." 
As for judicial and administrative reform, it was the occasion 
for the first serious disagreement between the constitutional 
leaders. Castelnuovo and Balsamo had devised a plan to divide 
Sicily into 23 districts, each with its own court, sheriff and other 
officials. The districts were to be drawn less arbitrarily than the 
French Departments; all the same, Castelnuovo, so far as he 
meant to impose uniformity on a country ridden with multiple 
jurisdictions, seemed to accept some of the administrative prac- 
tices of the new France even while he rejected its political 
doctrine. He also claimed—though dubiously—that his plan 

<<<PDFPAGE 95>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
79 
would strengthen the judiciary against the interference of power- 
ful individuals. Inevitably the plan ran into opposition: from 
Palermo, whose many lawyers—strong in Parliament—saw 
their livelihood threatened by the setting up of new courts 
elsewhere; from a number of other towns, which kept up the 
enduring spirit of Italian municipal rivalry and feared domi- 
nation by their neighbours. Belmonte took sides with the op- 
ponents of the plan; and Bentinck too argued against Castel- 
nuovo that it would prove cumbersome, expensive and hard 
on the poorer districts—they had better have half the magistrates 
and pay them well. In the end Parliament passed the plan in 
part; but the whole question was left unsettled, as was that of 
the new criminal and civil codes; in fact judicial reform was 
never completed or properly tackled until the Restoration 
period, when the Constitution itself had been destroyed.* 
The rock on which the Patriotic movement split beyond 
mending was the feudal question. The abolition of feudalism 
on July 2oth has long been a matter for debate. Some have held 
that it was largely a gesture and did not effectually cut down 
—was not in fact meant to cut down—the social and economic 
power of the Barons. In this view the whole movement of 
opinion among the Sicilian parliamentary classes in these years, 
and its culminating point the 1812 Constitution, came to no 
more than an attempt by the Barons to defend their position 
against the inroads of the central government, even to strengthen 
it. By converting feudal tenure into personal property—so the 
argument runs—they gave up more burdens than they did 
benefits; they exacted compensation for the loss of local privi- 
leges like tolls and monopolies; and they saw to it that the 
nobility as a class and a power in Parliament was kept intact.* 
But this seems too simple. The night of July 20th was a time of 
high excitement; Castelnuovo’s followers, and even for a time 
Belmonte’s, were swayed by the thought of the Enlightenment; 
in latter years, too, their cause had seemed to develop into a 
national one. So far as we can judge their motives many of the 
barons did at first believe they were abdicating their privileged 
position in the State. None the less there was already a conflict 
between conservatives like Cassaro and reformers like Castel- 
nuovo. As the initial fervour faded away the conflict spread; an 

<<<PDFPAGE 96>>>
80 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
* 
increasing number of barons began to take fright at what they 
had done; gradually they slid back into the conservative camp. 
Parliament, under Castelnuovo’s influence, did confirm the 
abolition of feudal privileges, this time without compensation. 
But the opposite tendency manifested itself in an article abolish- 
ing common rights on former feudal lands—a step akin in its 
effects to the contemporary English enclosures. The clash came 
when Castelnuovo’s followers proposed the abolition of entails 
(fidecommessi). Members at once recognised how important 
was this proposal. They might and did quote Blackstone at one 
another in debating it; but they seem to have been clear in their 
minds that what was at stake was the survival of the barons as 
the exclusive ruling class. On one side Castelnuovo and Balsamo 
foresaw that abolition would lead to an increase in small pro- 
perty and to the rise of a new class of middling landholders, like 
the English gentry the pillars of the Constitution: this meant 
the partial break-up of great estates. Cassaro, on the other side, 
was extremely worried: he hoped Bentinck “would not permit 
the nobility to be destroyed.’ All the same the issue was not 
quite so clear-cut. Many barons, burdened with debts and 
mortgages, might have welcomed a chance to alienate their 
lands: but they were ‘moved less by private interest . . . than by 
vanity and class considerations or prejudices’. In the end the 
majority of the Barons voted against abolition “for fear of seem- 
ing enemies to their own order and degenerate sons of their 
illustrious ancestors’—had the vote been secret, thought Castel- 
nuovo, it would have gone the other way. As the other two 
Estates voted for abolition, the question stood unresolved.* 
But politically the conflict had already had far-reaching effects. 
The split now ran not only between Castelnuovo and Cassaro, 
but between Castelnuovo and Belmonte. For Belmonte, whether 
from a desire to conciliate the Barons, or from his own aristo- 
cratic leanings, voted against abolition, as did his followers. The 
full consequences were not felt at once; but the question—which 
was, indeed, fundamental—remained a source of dissension. In 
the next few months it was to split the constitutional majority 
into “Belmontisti’ and ‘Villermosisti’ 
(so called after Castel- 
nuovo’s other title, Villermosa), and gradually to estrange the 
two leaders.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 97>>>
THE CONSTITUTION AND THE ‘PROMISE’ 
8I 
Bentinck took little part in this stage of the debate. His 
sympathies were with Castelnuovo, for he too thought the 
barons were unpopular and their privileges a burden to the 
country. It is not clear whether he already despaired of their 
political capacity—as he was later to do—and therefore, like 
Castelnuovo, wished the middle classes to come into a greater 
share of political power. But it was bad enough that the dispute 
over the feudal question should have still further split the 
Ministry.” 
‘The Barons wanted an aristocracy, and...all wanted to 
avoid paying’, said the Prince about this time. He was right, at 
any rate, about the Sicilians’ willingness to pay: only at the very 
end of the session did Parliament bring itself to vote even an 
unsatisfactory budget. The economic situation was no better 
than before: the grain shortage continued, leading here and 
there to riots;* and Castelnuovo’s patriarchal notion of political 
economy only matched the antiquated financial structure of 
Sicily—a state with no modern system of banking or tax- 
collecting and no funded debt, quite unable to bear the strain 
of a long war even with a British subsidy. To these difficulties 
were added the reluctance of Parliament, first to vote supply at 
all for fear of a dissolution, then to vote adequate supply; and 
Castelnuovo’s own reluctance—whether from delicacy or for 
fear of unpopularity—to put pressure on Parliament. The upshot 
was that the deficit remained almost as large as before.” 
This was a serious disappointment to Bentinck; and so was 
the failure of Parliament to do much for the Sicilian Army 
except keep Neapolitans out of it. “They want the most contra- 
dictory things,’ he complained; ‘they first vote that foreigners 
shall not be employed in the army, they next vote that no 
Sicilian shall be forced to serve....They then desire a large 
army and navy but will not vote any money for it.’ Still, Parlia- 
ment was over by November sth and the Constitution made. 
After several stormy months, Sicily lay for a few weeks in the 
doldrums. 
Early in December Bentinck foresaw that in about a fortnight 
the Royal Assent to the Constitution would be published. The 
next step would take him to Spain.” 

<<<PDFPAGE 98>>>
CHAPTER IV 
' 
THE KING’S RETURN: 
THE QUEEN SIDEPAR TURE 
Unhappily for Bentinck what happened in the winter of 
1812-13 was not his own departure for Spain; it was the King’s 
return to Palermo and his attempt to resume power. The 
resulting crisis lasted from December to April: if it proved to be 
the longest and fiercest yet, that was because it brought to a 
head not only Bentinck’s contention with the Royal Family 
but also the conflicts that had for some time been growing up 
among the patriotic Ministers and their followers. The ostensible 
question was whether the King or the Prince should rule; the 
real question, whether the new ‘system’, launched with British 
help and now embodied in the Constitution, should stand. 
The King’s decision to delegate his powers in January 1812 
had been an accident: Bentinck, taken by surprise, neither able 
nor willing to press the King to the point of abdication, had 
accepted it. Since then nothing had, on the face of it, prevented 
the King from putting an end to the Vicariate at any moment. 
He had not done so; all the same Bentinck had been unable to 
preserve the new ‘system’ from continual trouble and delay, 
due in part to the King’s obstruction. 
Now, at the beginning of November 1812, Bentinck held in 
his hand what looked like several strong cards. He had just 
exacted the ‘promise’ of the Queen’s departure; Parliament had 
risen, and the Constitution, which it had at length drawn up, 
seemed likely to be no longer a matter for dispute. Yet Bentinck 
was aware that the King’s means of interference were not 
exhausted; he wished before leaving for Spain to improve on 
his latest victory by reconciling the King once and for all to 
the existing state of affairs. No sooner, indeed, had he exacted 
the ‘promise’ than he hinted to Caccamo that Ferdinand ‘the 
Queen gone would make an excellent King. He had a good 
understanding and an honest heart.’ Why should he not leave 
his self-imposed retreat at La Ficuzza to come and shoot at La 
Favorita, the royal villa near Palermo? ‘I would carry the King’s 
gun for him.”* 

<<<PDFPAGE 99>>>
THE KING S$ RETURN: THE QUEEN’S DEPARTURE 
83 
The King’s own intentions, however, were not clear. There 
was certainly talk of his returning to power; the Queen con- 
tinued to agitate for it from her own retreat at Santa Margherita; 
and Bentinck foresaw that she would do all she could to evade 
the ‘promise’. But when the Archbishop suggested to the King 
that he should at any rate return to Palermo, he ‘put his hand 
upon the Archbishop’s mouth and said Tace Monsignor... . 
What I am doing is right and one day you will be of the same 
opinion.”* No less difficult was it to make out the King’s 
attitude from the behaviour of the Prince. The various crises 
of the summer and autumn, throughout which the Prince had 
been the helpless advocate of compliance, had left a coolness 
between father and son. The Prince now put on a tentative 
show of independence; when Orleans advised him to be l’homme 
des Anglais ‘si vous voulez rester sur votre throne’ he ‘made a 
face and said—Je le veux bien’. The King complained that he 
was kept in the dark; he threatened to wash his hands of the 
whole business: the ratification of the new treaty, the sanctioning 
of the Constitution—his son might attend to them alone and 
take the consequences. Yet the Prince was more dilatory than 
ever: the Constitution awaited his assent, but Bentinck—now 
an ad hoc member of the Council—was shortly to complain that 
in three months there had been three Councils on the subject. 
All the Ministers complained of the Prince’s distrust and mania 
for detail, which slowed down the course of business almost to 
a standstill. He conferred with subordinate officials without 
telling their Ministers; he sent for paper after paper until the 
departments ran out of despatch-bags, and he kept the papers 
for weeks without doing anything about them. The Ministers 
had found, on taking office, that many of their subordinates 
were hostile to the new ‘system’; but they could not induce the 
Prince to replace them: as usual, he promised much but per- 
formed little. And over one important appointment the Prince, 
as will be seen, seemed determined to thwart Bentinck and the 
constitutional leaders.* 
The only explanation Bentinck could find for these difficulties 
was that the King was still interfering and still unreconciled. 
As Orleans put it, 

<<<PDFPAGE 100>>>
84 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
‘The King was at present as it were retired into his citadel from whence 
he was ready to make a sortie whenever he pleased, waiting for the turn 
of events and encouraging by his hostile position all those who were 
unfavourable to the Constitution.’ 
How was this state of affairs to be remedied? Orleans and 
Belmonte suggested on December 17th that Bentinck should 
present the King with these alternatives: he should either resume 
power, or else give his “decided countenance’ to the Prince’s 
administration. Bentinck doubted whether the King’s ‘counte- 
nance’ would in fact turn the Prince into a satisfactory ruler; 
but it seems he was already toying with the notion of bringing 
back the King on terms that would put an end to delay and 
dissension.* 
For what moved Orleans and Bentinck to make their sugges- 
tion was the split within the Government. This was no news, 
but it had lately gone so far that the Government could scarcely 
carry on as it stood. Bentinck was losing patience: ‘although 
Belmonte was always complaining of the backwardness of the 
English ...the Ministers must be the principals... the British 
Minister could only be an auxiliary’. The Ministers, he insisted, 
must work together at all costs; if they did not, England could 
do no more, short of assuming the government; as it was ‘we 
appeared to have everything our own way and to have all the 
power in our hands while in fact we had nothing at all—We had 
our own Ministers, but they were not united together did not 
know how to manage business and had no power over the 
Prince’ .* 
Aci and Bentinck had been at odds for months; but since the 
end of Parliament Aci had come into conflict with Castelnuovo 
as well, over the Army credits which the ‘too philosophique’ 
Castelnuovo refused to pay out. This quarrel became further 
embittered in December when Castelnuovo proposed. various 
appointments designed to give him effectual control over his 
own department, among them that of Balsamo as Under- 
Secretary (Direttore). The Prince objected to Balsamo as a 
‘republican’; when Castelnuovo threatened to resign Aci urged 
the Prince to seize the opportunity of getting rid of his inflexible 
and ‘democratic’ Finance Minister. Bentinck came to the rescue 
with the usual expedient of suspending a loan—Castelnuovo’s 

<<<PDFPAGE 101>>>
THE KING'S RETURN: THE QUEEN'S DEPARTURE 
85 
hold on the public purse-strings, he said, was a security essential 
to the British Government. But the Prince shewed unexpected 
powers of resistance: was one thing after another, he cried, to 
be extorted from him by Bentinck’s ‘couteau 4 la gorge’? At 
the New Year the matter was still unsettled. Bentinck had, 
however, decided that Aci must go: ‘His opposition to me I 
forgive—but his attempt to get rid of Villarmosa to put in his 
place a creature of his own I shall never pardon.’ He guessed 
that Aci was ‘achetable’: he did not know that for the last five 
months Aci had been conducting a secret correspondence with 
the King.* 
If personal jealousy and spite accounted for Aci’s defection, 
Cassaro’s was a symptom of deeper differences among the 
patriots. He, too, opposed Balsamo’s appointment, but he did 
so because in the Parliamentary debates Balsamo had supported 
the abolition of entail. When at the end of December the Prince 
seemed on the point of giving way to Bentinck and Castelnuovo 
over the appointment, Cassaro tried to resign, but was dissuaded 
by the Prince and by Bentinck himself. Bentinck was at pains to 
puzzle him out: ‘his prejudices against the new system I had 
seen with concern, but I respected his integrity and always 
recollected with pleasure his uprightness and firmness under the 
administration of the Queen’. Cassaro was, he thought at one 
moment, ‘an honest man in his way . . . he liked the old system 
well administered’. Yet his behaviour was disquieting: he had 
begun to complain that the Prince was a hopeless failure as a 
ruler, while he hinted that ‘the King was now as desirous of 
governing as he had been all his life averse to it’. Bentinck began 
to reconsider his estimate: Cassaro was (he decided at the end 
of December) ‘a very ambitious man... in the last adminis- 
tration he could not bear Ascoli—now Belmonte. He is violent 
and cannot command his temper. . . 
. He sees no chance of being 
Prime Minister.”* 
Here Bentinck put his finger on a part of Cassaro’s motives: 
the fact was—and Bentinck took a surprisingly long time to 
find it out—that Cassaro was the chief engineer of the King’s 
attempt to resume power. He may well have been moved by 
jealousy and thwarted ambition, as Bentinck and the Prince 
believed; and he managed for a while to throw a smoke-screen 

<<<PDFPAGE 102>>>
86 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
about his intentions. But although constitutional issues seldom 
came to the surface in the ensuing crisis, they were there all the 
same and they mattered at least as much as personal motives. 
Cassaro’s action meant at bottom that after much ambiguous 
behaviour—and perhaps real hesitation—he was seceding from 
the Patriots outright and going over to the King in opposition 
to the constitutional leaders, Belmonte and Castelnuovo. Other 
great noblemen—people like the Prince of Trabia and the former 
Lord Mayor of Palermo, San Marco—also moved closer to the 
King about this time. Some of them had, like Cassaro, defended 
baronial liberties in the past; we must suppose that in their minds 
the abolition of feudalism (and its more effective corollary, the 
abolition of entail which they feared would “democratise Sicily’) 
now outweighed the political advantages of the new Constitu- 
tion: the King seemed their last refuge.” Ferdinand, of course, 
had his own reasons for wishing to forestall the sanctioning of 
the Constitution: the loss of the Royal Domain in exchange for 
a Civil List, and—most important—the separation of Sicily 
from Naples, were measures he could never stomach. 
Such was the confused and perplexing situation when on 
January 4th the Queen produced a coup de thédtre by returning 
to the King’s side at La Ficuzza. She acted most likely on her 
own impulse—or as she put it by the will of God—though 
Bentinck disbelieved it. Cassaro at any rate had tried to dissuade 
her; he deplored her coming as likely to complicate matters 
unnecessarily by antagonising Bentinck from the start. As for 
the King, his wife’s move put him (according to Caccamo) 
‘nel inferno’: ‘Iam in the situation of Tantalus’, he said, ‘I know 
what I ought to do, I wish to do it, but I cannot do it.’ What 
in fact ensued—and Bentinck was not at once aware of it—was 
a backstage tug-of-war between the Queen and Cassaro, with 
each of them trying to make the King resume on his or her 
terms: Cassaro telling the King that ‘tutti i buoni’ desired his 
return, but that he ought first to send the Queen away again; 
she hoping by the King’s return to undo the ‘promise’, forestall 
her own expulsion and regain her influence. The King’s inability 
to make up his mind between these alternatives prolonged the 
crisis.* 
About this intestine conflict at La Ficuzza Bentinck was at 

<<<PDFPAGE 103>>>
THE KING'S RETURN: THE QUEEN’S DEPARTURE 
87 
first in the dark; and Cassaro, who as usual acted as go-between, 
managed to keep him there for some time. Bentinck was faced 
with two happenings, which seemed related but need not be 
equally objectionable: the breach of the ‘promise’, and the King’s 
desire to resume power. About the former there could be no 
doubt: when the King tried to explain it away—he had merely 
stated the Queen’s intention of going, and if she now changed 
her mind he could not help it—Bentinck lost no time in shewing 
‘very great anger . . . for the indecency and meanness of this at- 
tempt to declare nugatory a solemn assurance of the King 
certified by persons of such respectability as Prince Cassaro and 
Marquis Circello’.* But the King’s wish to return to power was 
another matter: Bentinck might use it as a lever to obtain the 
Queen’s removal and the fulfilment of the ‘promise’: and this 
he tried to do during the next six weeks. Besides (Bentinck began 
to think) the King’s return, if properly managed, might even be 
useful in itself as a settlement of the whole Sicilian situation. 
In the first place, the King was popular, as Bentinck tried to 
convince Orleans: 
‘I said there were four points upon which there was a very general 
sentiment in this country: 
1. Popularity of the King 
2. Hatred to the Neapolitans 
3. Do. — — Queen 
4. Dislike of the Barons. 
He did not believe the first and last. I asked him how it could be other- 
wise with the general oppression exercised by the latter and their refusal 
at all times to contribute to the public burthens. He was not convinced. 
He said it had always been the policy of the Court to destroy the Barons 
and their privileges thinking by that means more easily to govern—this 
had been the object of Caracciolo’s administration.” 
The Prince had, on the contrary, so lost whatever popularity 
he had had that the King’s return would (Bentinck thought) 
be greeted with joy. True, the Prince had latterly seemed to 
acquiesce in the truism that (as Orleans kept telling him) “when 
a sovereign for his protection received from a foreign power 
a large subsidy and a large force, that sovereign could not be 
called independent’; he appeared genuinely to desire the Queen's 
departure; and whether he liked the Constitution or not, he 

<<<PDFPAGE 104>>>
88 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
had ‘neither ability nor courage enough to attempt its destruc- 
tion’. But this very weakness was a disadvantage so long as the 
Prince remained open to his parents’ pressure. The King, on 
the other hand, would be his own master, provided his wife 
_was absent; and although Bentinck thought it would never suit 
him to resume as ‘he had been a despot all his life had never 
attended to business and could never give up his old habits and 
prejudices’, yet this legendary idleness (in which Bentinck 
plausibly but mistakenly believed) might mean that, if the King 
did resume, he would subside into a King Log and let the consti- 
tutional machine run without hindrance. Above all, Bentinck 
still thought him ‘the innocent instrument of the Queen’s will’; 
if he could be got to Palermo alone, the ‘fatal charm’ might be 
broken, and the King reconciled to the new ‘system’.* 
It took six weeks’ negotiations to undeceive Bentinck. At 
first he was prepared to act moderately; he objected to Bel- 
monte’s and Orleans’ policy of ‘frightening’ the King and his 
associates; he refused to be drawn (as the Queen hoped he 
would be) into making it a condition sine qua non that she and 
her husband should live apart. As he later pointed out to Cassaro, 
‘I must be always prepared for my defence...I was liable to be 
attacked in England... I was threatened with the publication of every- 
thing in Russia and Germany, and therefore I could let nothing pass 
without its refutation.”* 
He began with a Note (15 January) to Cassaro and Circello, 
recalling the King’s ‘parole sacrée’ and requesting that the Queen 
should go back to Santa Margherita. He had already privately 
suggested to the King, through Caccamo, three possible courses: 
he might live with the Queen at a considerable distance from 
Palermo; or he might abdicate, in which case the Queen would 
be free to go where she chose; or he might come to Palermo 
and resume power if he wished, provided the Queen fulfilled 
the ‘promise’. There was no reply for a fortnight: confusion 
reigned at La Ficuzza: the Queen refused to budge, and the King 
did his best to dodge the issue.* On the advice of Cassaro (who 
apparently hoped thus to frighten the King into action) Bentinck 
wrote a second and stronger Note (29 January) in which he 
stated that so far the British Government had considered the 

<<<PDFPAGE 105>>>
THE KING’$ RETURN: THE QUEEN’S DEPARTURE 
89 
Queen alone ‘comme ennemie des intéréts de I’ Angleterre, 
comme cause de tous les désastres de la Sicile’; but now the 
King’s breach of promise had placed him in a position hostile to 
the interests of both countries, and might lead to the most 
disastrous consequences for him and his family. Bentinck there- 
fore demanded prompt action. This Note had the effect Cassaro 
desired: the King decided on February 2nd that he would go 
to La Favorita, near Palermo, and the Queen to Castelvetrano 
in south-western Sicily. On the 6th he arrived at La Favorita: 
to Orleans he ‘spoke... with horror of the 14 months he had 
passed [at La Ficuzza] ... he was now in a paradiso’.* 
But the situation was not so rosy as the King thought. Out- 
manoeuvred by Cassaro, the Queen still refused to leave La 
Ficuzza: snow, she said, made the roads impassable; and she 
went on urging her husband to resume at once. Bentinck, on 
the other hand, refused to see the King while the ‘promise’ 
remained unfulfilled. On the roth he got a reply to his Notes 
from Cassaro and Circello: this amounted to a blank denial that 
there had ever been any promise, and a rejection of his charges. 
Orleans and Belmonte urged a strong reply; but Bentinck 
preferred to enter into conversations with Circello and Cassaro. 
The outcome was a mutual withdrawal of Notes, and a fresh 
and more conciliatory reply by Cassaro and Circello, in which 
they promised that the Queen would go to Castelvetrano as 
soon as the snow thawed.* 
Meanwhile the Prince and the Government had been carrying 
on as best they could. Councils on the Royal Assent to the 
Constitution were held in the intervals of the political crisis, and 
the Prince even seemed, for the first time, in a hurry to get the 
business over. He kept aloof from the crisis, but was obviously 
displeased with Cassaro for attempting to play Grand Vizier, 
so much so that he shewed favour to Belmonte and even to 
Castelnuovo. At the same time the conflict between Aci and 
Castelnuovo over the appointment of Balsamo had come to a 
head. Aci was now openly on the King’s side; relations between 
him and Bentinck became more and more strained until, at the 
end of one heated exchange, ‘di Aci continued to talk but I 
took my hat and walked out of the room’; the man was (Ben- 
tinck decided) ‘a miserable dirty fellow’. Aci finally resigned 

<<<PDFPAGE 106>>>
feye) 
BENTINCK AND,SICILY 
at the end of January, with a flourish: ‘surely’, he cried, ‘the 
English Minister was not to govern Sicily ...he was no in- 
triguer...he cared not what priests were made Archbishops 
or Abbots or Archangels...if he had listened to the calls of 
ambition he might have been the first man in Sicily . . . his 
influence over the King might have made him Prime Minister 
years ago.’ After a fortnight’s delay due to the Prince’s customary 
doubts and fears, Ruggiero Settimo, a friend of Castelnuovo’s, 
was appointed in Aci’s place. Thus three out of the four Ministers 
were now staunch constitutionalists; while the fourth, Cassaro, 
continued to hint that he too would presently resign, or else 
become Prime Minister if the King resumed power.” 
For the King was still thinking of resuming. Cassaro brought 
the matter up during the negotiations with Bentinck (13-18 
February) which led to a mutual withdrawal of Notes and a 
momentary détente. Bentinck realised that he could not openly 
object, nor did he as yet particularly wish to. Nevertheless, he 
told Cassaro, he had made himself responsible for the tranquillity 
of Sicily, and he must therefore demand of the King an under- 
taking to sanction the Constitution and to make no great change 
of men or measures for some time. Cassaro agreed, with the 
proviso that the King would sanction the Constitution so far 
as it conformed to the English—in fact he meant to send it to 
England for examination; this made Bentinck uneasy, for it 
implied a long delay. Cassaro also declared that the King could 
not accept the separation of Sicily from Naples, as it was quite 
contrary to the ‘English system’. The representative of the 
United Kingdom could not press this point officially; but as 
a matter of ‘private sentiment’ he strongly urged that it should be 
accepted. Bentinck, however, still doubted whether the King 
would resume, and thought that if he did, he would most likely 
find the work too much for him, and would end by abdicating 
or restoring the Vicariate. 
But these exchanges were only informal; and Bentinck was 
in two minds whether to approach the King direct. He was 
reluctant to leave the King wholly in the hands of Aci and the 
other enemies of the Ministry, who were now clustering about 
him at La Favorita; on the other hand a letter setting out 
Bentinck’s terms for resumption might seem an invitation, 

<<<PDFPAGE 107>>>
THE KING'S RETURN: THE QUEEN'S DEPARTURE 
OI 
especially as ‘I knew he would make no difficulty about the 
terms’. Besides, Bentinck still would not see the King while the 
Queen remained at La Ficuzza. When, however, Bentinck did 
sound Cassaro officially on February 24th, Cassaro replied that 
the King meant to resume at once, whether the Queen went 
away or not; since Bentinck refused to see him, the King would 
have ‘le déplaisir de n’entendre que postérieurement de Votre 
Excellence ce quil aurait désiré savoir avant de reprendre le 
gouvernement .* 
What had happened is still not clear. One conjecture, put 
forward at the time by the Prince, was that the King had 
promised the Queen to resume power when he returned to 
Palermo on February 6th; finding himself still caught between 
the twin intransigences of the Queen and Bentinck, he had 
changed his mind. “The Queen’s answer was rage’, however, 
and now the King had given in to her; ‘the present plan as he 
believed made by di Aci was to resume and to endeavour to 
upset the Constitution by Parliamentary numbers.’ Another 
possible explanation is that the King was induced by Cassaro 
to gamble on Bentinck’s making no effective resistance, and 
was encouraged by some ‘pleasant’ news he received from 
Castelcicala on February 23rd: the contents of Castelcicala’s 
despatch are not known, but he very likely reported Castlereagh 
as saying or implying that Bentinck was not empowered to use 
force.* 
Whatever the reasons for this sudden decision, it shook the 
scales from Bentinck’s eyes. He now saw ‘the impossibility of 
placing even the least reliance on His Majesty’s word or good 
faith’; he quarrelled with Cassaro—in whom he at last detected 
the chief instigator of the King’s move—and told him that ‘if 
the King resumed I was determined to enlever the Queen’: 
‘This was a trial of strength. It was the first effort of a long and con- 
certed plan to destroy the system of liberty, to which in every part of 
its progress every impediment had hitherto been thrown, but in vain.’* 
He refused to deal with Cassaro any longer; negotiations 
would henceforth be carried on through regular channels, that 
is, through Belmonte. To him Bentinck announced that ‘it was 
necessary that I should not be compelled to retreat a step’. 

<<<PDFPAGE 108>>>
Q2 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
Bentinck’s prevailing mood was confidence: ‘in such crises 
as these I had always gained more than I had asked.’ Might he 
not cither prevent the King from resuming, or else carry out 
his threat to expel the Queen by force? Yet on second thoughts 
he decided to continue negotiations. The fact was that Bentinck’s 
position was hazardous. The Sicilians seemed useless—'they 
were all afraid and would do nothing . . 
. they were run already 
to kiss the King’s feet... on the side of the Nation and of 
liberty not a man would stir—we were now completely the 
principals.’ The recent split had disheartened as well as weakened 
his friends; and he was no more certain of support at home. 
Ministers were ‘slippery chaps’ at best; presently he heard from 
Nugent, who had just arrived from London, that 
‘it was very comical to remark the conduct of Ministers in England 
about Sicily. They were very anxious... to see what had been done next 
and who was to get the better—as if the business was entirely left as far 
as regarded them to itself’.* 
Here the King uttered one of his Neapolitan boutades, which 
so impressed Bentinck that he underlined it heavily in his journal 
and (perhaps as a hint) in his despatch to Castlereagh: 
‘But that’s what the English always do,’ the King exclaimed when 
Belmonte tried to convince him that Bentinck was following instructions; 
‘they let their Minister act as he pleases; if he succeeds they approve him; 
if not they break his neck.’* 
There was enough truth in this to make Bentinck keenly alive 
to the dangers of proconsulship in his present circumstances. 
Bentinck’s verbal threat to ‘enlever’ the Queen made the 
King put off his resumption until she should have left La Ficuzza. 
Negotiations went on. In his first Note to Belmonte (26 Febru- 
ary) Bentinck laid down his terms for the King’s return: they 
were those he had put verbally to Cassaro a week earlier, 
but included—besides guarantees of the ‘promise’, of the Consti- 
tution, and of a constitutional Ministry—an undertaking to 
summon Parliament by April. The King’s answer (1 March), 
like Cassaro’s, accepted these terms in principle but insisted on 
the pure English Constitution, and on his constitutional pre- 
rogative to choose his own Ministers. 

<<<PDFPAGE 109>>>
THE KING’S RETURN: THE QUEEN’S DEPARTURE 
93 
‘His Majesty’, Bentinck commented, ‘had no difficulty in giving a 
general assent to future measures which not requiring immediate exe- 
cution might hereafter be evaded. We were now at one of the moments 
the most critical for the successful establishment of the constitution. It 
was upon the point of being sanctioned and carried into execution. I 
was deeply impressed with the King’s ill will and bad faith. To overturn 
the constitution by force he knew to be impossible—but it was easy to 
attack it by sap—to get rid of the Ministers one by one—to bring in by 
means of the Sheriffs, all in Prince Cassaro’s nomination and under his 
influence, to whom the first formation of the lists of the electors is 
entrusted under the constitution, persons attached to the old Court party. 
It became necessary to put the King’s sincerity to the test, to discountenance 
this bold and menacing behaviour—and to obtain sucha Ministry as would 
give confidence to the public, and would not be blindly subservient to 
the King’s projects.”* 
In fact, Bentinck was determined to stop the King, if he could, 
from resuming at all; the Prince he now thought ‘infinitely 
preferable’. 
Bentinck therefore raised his terms: he demanded (3 March) 
that the date of the Queen’s departure for Vienna—announced 
for the spring—should be fixed, and that Cassaro should be 
dismissed.* The King (6 March) evaded the first demand and 
refused the second. To Belmonte he remarked that the English 
‘wanted to get Sicily and that was the object of all their meas- 
ures’; at the same time he thought Bentinck had no powers to 
use force and his bluff could therefore be called: “before the 
troops went to Spain he was obliged to be quiet but now... 
he had the means’. So when Bentinck sent in a still stronger 
remonstrance (8 March) in which he warned the King that his 
hostile attitude might endanger the Alliance, the King did not 
even read it. For on the same day the Queen, her protests 
overborne at last, left La Ficuzza for Castelvetrano; on the 
evening of the 9th the King drove from La Favorita to 
the palace, hastily summoned the Prince and the Ministers, and 
—to the ‘great surprise’ of Bentinck, who had never expected 
him to take so bold a line—announced that his health was 
restored and the Vicariate at an end.” 
The King was, for the moment, exultant. Bentinck, he said, 
might do as he pleased, but he would not be his ‘Maestro di 
Scuola as he had been that of Francesco . . 
. he consigned himself 

<<<PDFPAGE 110>>>
94 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
to the hands of God’. Next day he drove in state amid cheering 
crowds to a Te Deum service at the Cathedral. On the same th 
(10 March) Bentinck got an answer to his last Note, in whic 
the King merely stood his ground. He therefore “determined 
to go personally to the King and see ifI could not frighten him’. 
Bentinck’s arrival at the Palace on the morning of the 11th 
threw the King into a panic, but (as he himself relates) he ‘made 
the Holy Sign of the Cross’ and brought himself to face Bentinck 
for the first time since August. The interview was stormy: 
Bentinck repeated his recent warnings, and stated that the 
British Government would not permit the Constitution to be 
destroyed: “The King took fire at the word Permit. Permit, per- 
mit, permit, he repeated, England has no right to interfere.’ 
When Bentinck began to hint at the past ‘treachery’, not of 
the King but of his government, the King cut the interview 
short: ‘Ni moi, ni mon gouvernement. Je ne peux pas écouter de 
pareil langage. Ecrivez-le, écrivez-le! And he left me.’ No 
sooner was the interview over than the King—‘alterata la bile’— 
had an attack of nerves and took to his bed.* 
Meanwhile Bentinck was counter-attacking on another front. 
The King had planned to stage a second popular demonstration 
in his own favour by attending yet another thanksgiving service 
that evening. The city was in a ferment: 
“Amidst the exclamations of God save the King it was said that cries 
of “Out with the English” had also been mixed. Money was universally 
stated and believed to have been spread among the populace, and all the 
partisans of the Queen were zealously engaged in exciting disorder.’ 
Bentinck therefore warned the Sheriff of Palermo that, since 
he was responsible for the tranquillity of Sicily, he would march 
British troops into the city to ‘protect’ the King. The King 
decided to go on being ill, and called off the service. Meanwhile 
‘the troops had already been in motion and the passage of some 
cannon through the town made a very good effect greater said 
someone than all my Notes for the last year’.* 
The King, though frightened, still would not yield. For his 
sins God had plagued him with this man, he exclaimed—‘ma 
poi questa tribulazione will operate to my salvation. . 
.. Under 
the cross of Jesus Christ was J. Nazarenus Rex Judaeorum—on 

<<<PDFPAGE 111>>>
THE KING'S RETURN: THE QUEEN’S DEPARTURE 
05 
his tomb would be written Ferdinandus Rex delle due Sicilie’. 
His attempt (12 March) at a face-saving compromise—among 
other things he wanted Bentinck to pay the Queen’s debts 
before she left—fell on deaf ears. For Bentinck, despite the 
failure of all but a few Sicilians to rally actively to his side—or 
rather in a sense because of it—was convinced that he ‘could 
not retreat’: he must now hold out for nothing less than com- 
plete surrender.* He was therefore ready to declare the alliance 
at an end if need be, and to treat the King as an enemy. Britain 
(he later explained to Castlereagh) would have been justified 
in doing so at any time by the flagrant ‘treachery’ of the Sicilian 
Government in 1810-11. But 
“Where the one party was so entirely at the mercy of the other, it was 
essential that moderation forbearance and disinterestedness should dis- 
tinguish every part of our conduct. It would have been easy—it would 
have been fortunate for all the parties concerned, had a decisive stroke at 
once... terminated and adjusted this question. The interests of Sicily 
and of England, the happiness and dignity of the King and of the Royal 
Family would have been eventually saved. The necessity and humanity 
of such a proceeding might have been evident to the nearest spectators, 
but to the distant public and to posterity the honour of Great Britain 
might have received a stain.’ 
Persuasion had been tried for a whole year, fruitlessly. 
Now 
that the Constitution, in the success of which ‘our honour, the 
interests of both countries were involved’, was imperilled by 
the ‘faithless and evil advice’ of the King’s counsellors, Bentinck 
felt justified in using force unless the King gave way—as his 
instructions had always empowered him to do in an extremity. 
This decision he embodied in a final Note (14 March): at the 
same time he sent for part of his troops from Spain, and con- 
templated deposing the King by force if necessary: 
‘The principle of the arrangement was to be that we took possession 
of the country and gave up the right to the nation. The Parliament must 
nominate to the vacant throne’ (i.e. the Prince would be ‘elected’ to 
succeed his father, rather than visibly imposed on the country by the 
British).* 
The tension was at its height: Government business had come 
to a standstill; some of the constitutionalist leaders, including 

<<<PDFPAGE 112>>>
96 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
the Orleans family, were preparing to take refuge aboard 
British ships if the King held out to the point of hostilities.* He, 
indeed, did not yield at once: still apparently unconvinced that 
Bentinck meant what he said, he drafted a reply (15 March) 
conceding nearly every point but asking for time and, where 
the Queen was concerned, for money. Belmonte, however, 
refused to sign the draft; a few hours later, at Bentinck’s insti- 
gation, all three constitutionalist Ministers resigned. The King, 
who had already thrown Cassaro to the wolves, was left without 
a single Minister. Almost abandoned in his half-empty palace 
by his relatives and courtiers, he began to lose heart. Orleans, 
whom he sent for on the 16th, found him distracted: Bentinck 
might blow him up with grapeshot, he cried—he had a pain 
in his breast and in his side—me ne vado—I am dying—Oh, 
how I wish to be quiet’. He had committed a bestialita in 
resuming; for most of his misfortunes he blamed the Queen. 
Yet, in spite of his fears, he still would not commit himself to 
making her go away, especially so long as Britain refused to 
pay for her; and he categorically refused to consider abdicating. 
Finally, faced with a 24-hour ultimatum from Bentinck, the 
King did as he had done in January 1812: he announced verbally, 
on the evening of the 17th, that he meant to restore the Vicariate 
and not to resume in future without the consent of the British 
Government. He then decamped to La Favorita.* 
At this point Bentinck could perhaps have pressed the King 
as far as abdication, and indeed Orleans urged him to do so; 
but he had no authority for such a step. Instead he went on de- 
manding the Queen’s departure; and he insisted that the Vicar’s 
new powers should specifically include all those matters—such 
as Parliament, the Constitution, foreign affairs, and appoint- 
ments—in which the King had kept interfering since his first 
delegation of powers in January 1812. The Prince agreed with 
Bentinck that sooner than accept such another delegation in 
general terms—absolute in theory, at the mercy of the King’s 
whim in fact—it would be better to let the King resume again; 
though (Bentinck added privately) ‘if he resumed, it should 
only be with the condition that he should be bound hand and 
foot’. Finally, Bentinck demanded the dismissal from their Court 
functions of three of the King’s partisans, among them Cassaro: 

<<<PDFPAGE 113>>>
THE KING'S RETURN: THE QUEEN’S DEPARTURE 
97 
this he did at the insistence of Belmonte, who wanted revenge. 
It was unnecessary and rather weak of Bentinck, who would 
himself have preferred forbearance.* 
The King’s remarkable powers of resistance dragged out the 
crisis for ten more days. At first he would not discuss the new 
delegation of powers at all; then he took his stand on the vague 
powers of January 1812; then (21 March), goaded by another 
ultimatum from Bentinck, he chose to delegate ‘in accordance 
with the English Constitution’, which meant nothing. Mean- 
while Bentinck had taken alarm at rumours that the King meant 
to slip away and join the Queen and her guard of Calabrian 
brigands at Castelvetrano: ‘I never should catch him again and 
he might involve us in a civil war.’ Bentinck therefore posted, on 
the night of March 21st, a troop of dragoons outside the gates 
of La Favorita: 
a number of members of Parliament spent the 
night there too to await the outcome, and ‘many of the common 
people flocked thither as to a spectacle, buying nougat and other 
trifles that were being sold there, as they commonly are at 
religious processions and similar events’.* In the morning Ben- 
tinck himself rode up, withdrew the troops, and met the King, 
who had been sitting quietly in the front garden since dawn. 
The King greeted him with an announcement that as a token of 
his loyalty he meant to go to Malta, among the English. 
Bentinck, somewhat taken aback, decided not to require—as he 
had intended to do—the King’s signature then and there to the 
full delegation of powers: if he did so he might appear to be 
driving the King into exile. Instead he asked for a verbal assent, 
which the King gave at once. There was a kind of reconciliation: 
Bentinck told the King ‘I was not that méchant homme that I 
had been represented to him—he took me by the hand’. 
In fact the King had momentarily outwitted Bentinck: he 
did not, of course, go to Malta, but he did go back on his word. 
It took another week’s hectic coming and going to secure the 
full delegation. By now the proceedings were taking a grotesque 
turn: witness a proposal by the King’s chief counsellor at this 
time, the Duke of Sangro, that the King should be allowed to 
resume on condition that he made Bentinck his Great Chancellor 
for life. Bentinck’s patience was exhausted: 

<<<PDFPAGE 114>>>
98 
BENTINCK AND,SICILY 
‘As for the King and Queen, there was no argument but force that had 
any weight with them’, he told Sangro. ‘Shew it and they were down 
to the ground—withdraw it and the next moment they were on their 
high horse.’* 
He therefore despatched a body of troops to the neighbour- 
hood of Castelvetrano—where, rumour had it, the Queen was 
hoping to raise the local population—with orders to make her 
embark, if necessary by force. By yet another ultimatum he 
finally made the King sign, on March 28th, a full delegation of 
powers to the Prince, together with an undertaking not to 
interfere in the government, and not to resume it without 
Britain’s consent. 
On the evening of the same day, by an ironic coincidence, 
despatches arrived at Palermo from Castlereagh and Castelcicala, 
which if they had come sooner would almost certainly have 
forestalled the whole crisis.* For Castlereagh had impressed it 
on Castelcicala that the British Government required the 
Queen’s departure for Vienna; furthermore, ‘the only hope of 
our cooperation’ towards the recovery of Naples‘... rested 
upon the absolute extinction of her Majesty’s authority, and 
upon the establishment of a system in Sicily which by com- 
manding the public confidence might prepare the Neapolitans 
to regard the return of the Royal Family with favour’. To 
speed the Queen on her way the British Government would 
pay her debts and give her a pension (this decision in particular, 
if known sooner, might well have shortened or averted the 
crisis). Castlereagh, in fact, had fully accepted Bentinck’s view 
that the Queen was the chief obstacle in the way of a Sicilian 
settlement; he regretted that Bentinck had been unable as yet 
to join his troops in Spain, but commended his ‘zeal, prudence 
and firmness... under the many difficulties with which you 
have had to contend’; and the same packet brought Bentinck 
the K.C.B. 
In his instructions, however, 
Castlereagh told 
Bentinck not to use force except (as laid down by Wellesley) 
in an emergency and in the face of treachery: 
‘It is of infinite importance in point of impression that the object should 
be accomplished by arrangement and not by force... as it would be 
difficult to make the world at large understand, whilst the whole military 
force of the island is in Your Lordship’s hands, that there existed an

99 
adequate necessity for forcibly removing the wife of the Sovereign, 
more especially if no tumult existed, from her family and dominions.’* 
Bentinck had been aware throughout the crisis of the risks 
he was taking; he had already decided to send Lamb, his 
Secretary, home to explain the complex Sicilian situation to 
Castlereagh personally. He now doubly regretted that Castle- 
reagh’s instructions had not come sooner; for if he had not 
exactly used force, he could not very well quibble over the 
distinction between the threat and the deed. 
‘T feel that I have taken upon myself the most serious responsibility,’ 
he wrote. ‘H.R.H.’s gracious approbation can alone determine whether 
I have really consulted, as was my sincere desire, the real honour of 
Great Britain, the happiness, the liberty and the independence of Sicily. 
I anxiously await this approbation for my own comfort, but I still more 
ardently desire it for the sake of this great population who will view in 
it the irreversible confirmation of the great charter of their rights and 
liberties.”* 
Castlereagh’s reply was approval tinged with regret. In a 
private letter he hinted that 
‘in giving to our adherents the support they are entitled to claim at 
our hands, and in affording the new system the aid and countenance 
which the safety of the island requires and without which it would in 
its infancy perish, we must try so to arrange as to obviate the necessity 
of frequent acts of undisguised power’. 
Bentinck should if possible arrange for the King and Queen 
to become entirely dependent on Britain for their income, and 
so acquire a means of coercion over them less flagrant than that 
which he had been led to use.* 
It is somewhat odd that Bentinck should have used in his 
despatch the glowing phrase ‘this great population’ of those 
whom, in a private letter to Castlereagh dated three days later, 
he called ‘this very timid people’. Throughout the crisis he had 
complained that the Sicilians were not supporting him properly; 
the slightest appearance of faltering on his part (he was aware) 
would have had the worst possible effect,

‘here where cowardice was carried to such an extraordinary pitch... 
they never looked forward in this country. They only considered the 
present danger but... the greatest possible evil... they never contem- 
plated at all, and that was the possible disavowal by the British Govern-
