No doubt Bentinck wrote his despatches with an eye to 
publication if the Queen should decide to bring out her side of 
the story. All the same, one wonders whether he still could not 
help judging the Sicilians by a double standard—on a particular 
occasion they might prove cowards, but in the abstract they 
remained ‘this great population’. 
At all events Bentinck’s victory now seemed complete. After 
the re-establishment of the Vicariate on March 28th, and the 
simultaneous arrival of Castelcicala’s despatch announcing that 
Bentinck was fully supported by his Government, the powers 
of resistance of the King and Queen were broken. Bentinck 
could prepare to leave for Spain at last. 
The King confined himself henceforth to wrangling with his 
son over the Civil List; he refused to have anything to do with 
the settlement of his wife’s debts—‘affatto, affatto, he would 
not read a word’. In May he even had a friendly conversation 
with Bentinck, in the course of which he complained bitterly 
of the Prince—though ‘he loved him and forgave him and 
prayed God every morning illuminarlo’. Of the Sicilians ‘he 
said, to speak plainly, we are living in the midst of cannibals. 
He knew them well . . . the changes which had been made must 
end in bloodshed and anarchy’. He therefore asked for British 
protection in case of need. Bentinck agreed to it, but added 
that he too knew the Sicilians: ‘they made a great outcry... 
oe barked very loud but had no teeth—they did not dare 
ite 
The Queen made no more serious resistance, though illness 
and wrangles over her debts delayed her departure for over two 
months, and the ever-present threat of force—not force itself— 
was still needed to make her go. She sailed from Mazara on 
June 14th. Rather than ask for a French laissez-passer through 
Trieste or Fiume, she travelled to Vienna via Constantinople and 
Odessa. Delayed by winds and quarantines, she reached Vienna 
only in February 1814. Seven months later she died of a stroke.”

IOI 
On May 27th, three weeks before the Queen’s departure, 
Bentinck sailed to Spain, though Castlereagh had instructed 
him to remain in Sicily on account of the crisis: 
“When I reflect that the crisis to which this order has reference is past 
—that the causes of all our past embarrassments have absolutely termi- 
nated—that the Queen’s influence exists no more—that the King’s hos- 
tility is neutralised—that full and unlimited powers have been delegated 
to the Vicar General—that his administration is composed without 
exception of popular characters—that this administration appears to enjoy 
the Prince’s confidence, I am warranted in saying that the system against 
which we have been contending for the last six years, and in support of 
which the last struggle has been ineffectually made, is completely de- 
molished.’ 
The Constitution, too, was on the point of being sanctioned 
at last. It was a confident parting flourish. But then it was meant 
for official consumption.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 118>>>
CERUAE 
ERs Vv’ 
THEICONS TEBOHT TON BALDS 
Bentinck, when he sailed to Spain in May 1813, turned his back 
for a time not only on Sicily but also on Italy. It looks as though 
he was not sorry to exchange the heat and dust of Sicilian 
politics for the warlike quiet of Catalonia, where, indeed, his 
army had long stood in sore need of leadership. But Italy was 
another matter: even as he left Bentinck went on pressing for 
a change in British policy that would enable him to raise, free, 
and perhaps unify the greater part of the country. 
The liberation of Italy had been Bentinck’s chief aim at the 
beginning of his mission; Sicily was to have provided a stimulus 
to revolt and a springboard for invasion. But his reluctant 
decision to send most of his troops to Spain in June 1812 had 
scotched the plan; nothing had come of his attempts at coopera- 
tion with Italian patriots;* while the successive crises of the 
summer and autumn had taken up much of his time and had 
turned Sicilian reform into an end rather than a means. Still he 
went on urging his plans for Italian expeditions on the British 
Government, though with little hope of immediate success. For 
Ministers at home were unanimously in favour of his concen- 
trating his efforts on Catalonia; at most Bathurst and Bunbury 
held out vague hopes of action in Italy if and when Spain was 
wholly freed; and Nugent found, on one of his peripatetic visits 
to London in September, that ‘c’efit été inutile de lutter contre le 
torrent’.” The winter and spring of 1813, however, bringing 
news of the retreat from Moscow, gave new life to the Italian 
project, which now appeared at once more urgent and more 
feasible than before. Bentinck renewed his entreaties; moved 
the Prince to support them; sent Lamb home in April to state 
that the Italian patriots seemed ready to rise for ‘the expulsion 
of the French . 
. 
. and the union of all Italians in a single Nation, 
governed by a system of representation’: apart from the un- 
doubted military advantages of an expedition (Lamb was to 
argue) England ought to secure the future balance of power of 
the Continent by giving Italy her protection and so forestalling 
the ‘private ends’ of Austria and Russia.* But except for one or 

<<<PDFPAGE 119>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
103 
two converts with little influence on policy—Bunbury, Miinster, 
Pellew—neither the British Government nor its agents would 
budge;* and Bentinck’s secret negotiations with Murat, held at 
Ponza in June—in the course of which he again insisted on the 
necessity of making all Italy free and independent—came to 
nothing.” He therefore went to Spain convinced that his army 
was being frittered away there to no purpose, and a golden 
chance lost in Italy. This conviction he frequently expressed, 
and so added another difficulty to his already rather uneasy 
relations with Wellington; he also laid up another grievance in 
Ministers’ minds against the day when they should decide, 
in King Ferdinand’s words, to ‘break his neck’.* 
If Italy was a reminder of what might be, Sicily was a commit- 
ment both tangible and disquieting. Bentinck, as we have seen, 
assured his Government that there all was for the best. Perhaps 
that was what he himself wished to believe. Yet even before his 
departure he had been aware of possible danger ahead. 
The Constitution was sanctioned. But the Councils held for 
the purpose from December onward—part of the time under the 
shadow of the King’s attempt to overthrow the ‘system’—had 
perpetuated old dissensions and given rise to new ones. At first 
the Council had been divided, over such questions as the freedom 
of the press, between the conservative Cassaro, with whom the 
Prince generally sympathised, and the constitutionalists Bel- 
monte and Castelnuovo, often though not always supported by 
Bentinck. The Prince usually gave way to the latter: most of 
the institutional articles were sanctioned without serious changes. 
Bentinck was sometimes deputed to find out the ‘English prac- 
tice’; far from dictating to the Council, he was often to be found 
on the side ofcompromise; and he failed to carry the Council with 
him when, for instance, he attempted to secure a clearer sepa- 
ration of powers and a stronger Executive. 
The first serious trouble came over the confiscation of the 
Royal Domain in exchange for a Civil List, and the separation 
of Sicily from Naples. The Prince wished to veto both measures: 
he knew his father was irreducibly opposed to them, and he 
disliked them himself—as for separation, he scarcely concealed 
his belief that Sicily was a natural dependency of Naples, and 
the Sicilians ‘good for nothing’. He yielded on the Civil List, 

<<<PDFPAGE 120>>>
104 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
however, after thrusting responsibility for it on the Councillors. 
It took longer to make him accept separation. Bentinck admitted 
that England ‘did not care about it’—in fact it might be against 
her interests, since while the two countries were united she 
could always put pressure on Naples through Sicily. But the 
Sicilians, he pointed out, did seriously resent their subjection 
to Naples, and they did want independence; the two kingdoms 
had different interests—‘Naples... must always follow the great- 
est political powers of the Continent; Sicily the greatest maritime 
power —and that maritime power would certainly not help to 
thrust them together again by force. Sicily, independent, re- 
formed, properly developed, would be strong enough to stand 
on her own feet and have ‘allies, not masters.’ Besides, she 
might one day take to reunion of her own free will, especially 
if her free constitution was extended to Naples. The Prince at 
length gave way to the advice of the Council—this time unani- 
mous; but he did so with obvious distaste, and the setting up 
of separate dynasties in the two kingdoms was put off until a 
general peace.* 
No sooner had this obstacle been removed at the beginning 
of May than fresh trouble arose over the abolition of entail. 
This measure, passed in 1812 by the two lower Estates only, 
had caused the first falling out of Belmonte and Castelnuovo. 
When it had come up in Council the two had again opposed each 
other—with Bentinck supporting Castelnuovo—but at length 
all had agreed on a compromise which safeguarded the rights 
of immediate heirs. Now, however, Castelnuovo reopened the 
whole question by proposing that Parliament should be left free 
to decide it ‘according to the English Constitution’: this really 
meant that he hoped to secure outright abolition later on.* 
Belmonte and Castelnuovo now quarrelled in earnest: the one 
flippant and sarcastic, the other angry and immovable. Bentinck 
tried to make peace: 
‘He should consider himself as Prime Minister,’ he told Belmonte, 
‘and Villermosa as one of his instruments—whom on account of his 
utility he should court as a fine lady. He said he had neither time nor 
health for this operation which would occupy a man’s whole life—I said 
that I knew Villermosa was a very impracticable man but I believed him 
to have no ambition. . . 
. I had seen nothing in him inconsistent with the 

<<<PDFPAGE 121>>>
THE CONSTITUTION EAILS 
105 
most straightforward conduct—Belmonte admitted that he could not 
act without him—that if Villermosa left the Government, he should 
also. 
... I said... I did not think he had always treated Villermosa with 
proper respect.... Why did he refuse to him that great conciliation 
which he shewed to everyone else . . . their disunion would ruin every- 
thing. Belmonte said he knew it... but if they did not quarrel now 
they would do so afterwards.’ 
Belmonte, in fact, was very ready to take offence. He was 
nettled when he heard that Bentinck had called Castelnuovo 
‘the only man in Sicily... had I said this of him (Belmonte) I 
should have said justly’. At length an uneasy peace was patched 
up between the two; and Castelnuovo carried his point. But 
Belmonte’s injured vanity might be expected to make more 
trouble.* 
This dispute between the constitutionalist leaders came at the 
worst possible moment, when elections to the new House of 
Commons were about to be held. Their party, already isolated 
by the defection of Cassaro, Aci, and an increasing number of 
barons on the right, now had to face a new threat on the left 
from a ‘democratic’ movement that appeared to be forming in 
the country. Even with Castelnuovo and Belmonte united the 
Ministry would be none too strong. The two Ministers who 
had replaced Aci and Cassaro were both nobles with little 
following in the Commons. Settimo, a member of the princely 
Fitalia family, was able but young and new to politics; while 
the Prince of Carini, the new Minister of Justice, an honest man 
but so ‘perfectly unfit for the charge’ as to ‘throw ridicule upon 
the Government’, had been appointed merely for lack of a 
better. Bentinck did his best to shore up the Ministry by preaching 
union and ‘encouraging’ Carini.* But the trouble, to his mind, 
lay deeper: as he later told Orleans, 
‘The aristocracy in Sicily hangs upon a thread. I never could convince 
Belmonte that the nobles were universally despised and hated... be it 
ever recollected that my strong advice was to mix the Cabinet of Peers 
and of such respectable commoners as could have given the best support 
to the Peerage and to the Crown.”* 
But the enlightened middle class whom Bentinck and Castel- 
nuovo hoped to see taking a greater share in the government 

<<<PDFPAGE 122>>>
106 
BENTINCK AND. SICILY 
scarcely existed as yet in Sicily; most of the available commoners 
were lawyers and not ‘respectable’ enough as a group.” This 
being so, the Ministers could hardly be anything but aristocrats. 
But their need for unity was all the stronger, especially in the 
face of a coming Parliament which might ‘ride this new liberty 
horse too hard’ as Bentinck put it. Before leaving he therefore 
renewed his exhortations; he took with him as a precaution a 
private letter from Belmonte which gave warning of ‘demo- 
cratic’ perils ahead and might justify a sudden return to Sicily; 
and he urged the Prince to ‘cling fast to the British Consti- 
tution. * 
Bentinck’s fears were all too well grounded. A helpless spec- 
tator separated from the scene by the whole of the Western 
Mediterranean, he had that summer to watch the Constitution 
and its defenders speedily collapse under a double attack by 
democrats and conservatives. This attack grew naturally out of 
the previous course of the Patriotic movement; but it was helped 
to its success by weaknesses in the British and constitutional 
camp, some of them fundamental, some only contingent.* 
The split between Belmonte and Castelnuovo over the feudal 
question had shewn how unready were most of the original 
Patriots for a programme of moderate social change such as 
Castelnuovo advocated. That the split had been papered over 
only temporarily was brought out by the behaviour of the 
constitutional leaders during the elections to the new Parliament. 
Castelnuovo in particular deliberately refrained from using his 
influence to avert the obvious danger of a large number of 
‘democratic’ victories at the polls. Whether he did so, as Balsamo 
maintained, from ‘a greater reserve and delicacy than the character 
of the nation, and very likely the stubborn nature of the human 
heart, should have allowed’; or whether, as the Belmontists 
claimed, he wished to secure a majority for the abolition of 
entail, the result was the same. The Ministers and their followers 
therefore entered the fray not only divided among themselves 
but scarcely certain of a majority in Parliament.* 
Other circumstances hampered the government. The finances, 
never sound, were now in so bad a state as to be beyond the 
usual remedy of short-term British loans and advances: a speedy 
Parliamentary supply would be essential. Castelnuovo’s policy 

<<<PDFPAGE 123>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
IO}7/ 
of economic liberalism had increased food prices and with them 
popular discontent.* But a greater peril, over which the govern- 
ment had no control, was the plague, which had spread from 
the Middle East to Malta and might spread to Sicily. The 
measures taken to avert the peril made things worse politically 
by jeopardising the position of the British as the protectors of 
Sicily. 
The British, it appears, had already incurred some unpopu- 
larity by their display of force at the time of the King’s return 
and the Queen’s expulsion;* but the plague gave rise to much 
stronger grievances. Most of the traffic between Sicily and the 
rest of the Mediterranean, including the infected areas, was 
carried on by British ships. Amid the alarm caused by the 
plague, it was easy for the opposition to accuse the British of 
neglecting the quarantine regulations; it was even convenient 
for the government, if their policy was attacked, to reply ‘the 
English have obliged us to do so’. The British generals, for 
their part, were often overbearing and tactless in their protests 
against what they considered the unreasonable attitude of the 
government and the local boards of health.* Neither Lord 
Montgomerie, a British resident to whom Bentinck had en- 
trusted the mission, nor Joseph Smith, his Secretary, had the 
authority or the strength needed to control the situation: both 
were in poor health, one was new to diplomacy, and the other 
to Sicily.* Orleans, writing to Bentinck, complained of the lack 
of ‘une adresse et une circonspection qui n’est pas toujours 
congenial avec le caractére anglais’, and reported ‘the irritation 
produced by the manner in which the English Societies mount 
each other [i.e. se montent] against foreigners when there is no 
superior chief to give the Ton and to take the lead’. In Bentinck’s 
absence the British authorities did not always act in concert: 
besides, said Orleans, 
‘T think, My Lord, that generally they don’t see enough of the Sicilian 
Ministers, of the Hereditary Prince and of the heads of this government . 
. . 
this lack of communications lessens the English influence over the Sicilians, 
and... it renders impossible to adjust beforehand and privately the 
points which must afterwards become the objects of official Notes and 
public discussions.”* 

<<<PDFPAGE 124>>>
108 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
Orleans therefore clamoured for Bentinck’s return. All sum- 
mer long Bentinck was to receive letters by every boat from the 
Prince, the British authorities, and the constitutional leaders, all 
bearing the same refrain. ‘Oh My Lord, ot étes-vous donc? 
Croyez-moi, sans vous, cela n’ira jamais.”* 
There can be little doubt that had Bentinck been there he 
would have imposed greater unity on the British authorities 
and the government and prevented much needless friction. He 
might also have done something to heal the rift within the 
government, which became more apparent as Parliament met 
on July 8th in the intense summer heat and amid an explosion 
of speeches, pamphlets and petitions.* 
Already the forces opposed to the constitutional settlement 
could be discerned. In the Upper House there was now a ma- 
jority of conservative barons, in whose minds fear for their 
interests had damped the early ardour with which they had voted 
to abolish feudal rights. Their fears had lately been increased by 
the behaviour of the new municipal councils set up under the 
Constitution, which had taken to occupying baronial lands and 
escaping for the first time from baronial control. Since the defection 
of Cassaro many of the barons could be classed as King’s Friends 
—though the King himself kept studiously aloof to all appear- 
ances—and therefore as enemies of the ‘system’ they had them- 
selves helped to launch.* In the Commons, on the other hand, a 
large number of members—a potential majority if the Ministers 
failed to hold their supporters together—could be classed as 
‘democrats’ or ‘Jacobins’. The leaders of this party, Rossi and 
Vaccaro, had spent years of exile in revolutionary France; to 
the British and the constitutional leaders they seemed bent on 
setting themselves up as local Robespierres and Marats; and until 
recently Sicilian historiography has echoed that judgement, or 
at best has described them as merely factious and irresponsible. 
An attempt has lately been made to shew the democratic move- 
ment not as a rootless faction, but as the emanation of a new 
and genuine force in the country: the middling landowners of 
Eastern Sicily—of the Messina and Catania regions in particu- 
lar—then as now the most advanced part of the island socially 
and economically.” The democratic movement certainly did 
embody an attempt by Catania and Messina to shake free of the 

<<<PDFPAGE 125>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
10g 
aristocratic predominance of Palermo. Whether it was also trul 
representative of the nascent bourgeoisie may be doubted. Its 
members were mostly lawyers—that is to say, as often happened 
in the later history of Southern Italy, they formed an ‘intellectual 
bourgeoisie’, vociferous but small, largely divorced from its 
economic origins, talking and acting in a void. In 1813 they 
seldom rose above petty factiousness in their tactics, or, in their 
political aims, beyond a noisy but vague desire for equality and 
Parliamentary dictatorship; and in joining with the King’s 
Friends to overthrow the constitutionalists they seem to have 
been moved more by opportunism than by any notion of a 
lasting alliance between the monarchy and bourgeoisie against 
the ruling aristocracy. It may be argued, of course, that the 
constitutional movement was no less insignificant than the 
democratic—a ripple on the surface of that huge lake, the 
economic and social immobility of Sicily. But even a Marxist 
interpreter might admit that the constitutional movement repre- 
sented, at any rate at first, the only class strong enough to carry 
out—because it was also strong enough to frustrate—any meas- 
ure of social and economic change; and that Castelnuovo’s 
aristocratic but mildly ‘progressive’ policy along English lines 
was the utmost that Sicily might conceivably have been prepared 
for at this time. 
The constitutional leaders and the British may well be excused 
for thinking they detected in the democratic party ‘all the 
symptoms of the terrible French Revolution’.* Even Castelnuovo 
could scarcely be expected to see in this band of noisy lawyers 
the middle class on the English model which he had hoped 
might arise in Sicily. If his fears were exaggerated, one must 
remember that the French Terror was still fresh in men’s minds, 
and parliamentary criticism of any sort still new to Sicily: violent 
words therefore had a disproportionate effect. 
The weakness of the Ministry was made plain immediately 
after the opening of the Parliament, when the Commons refused 
to consider voting supply, though the end of the financial year 
was only six weeks off. No doubt they felt that “good govern- 
ment [consisted] in paying nothing’*; but they also feared that 
a dissolution might follow on the heels of a vote of supply. 
They went on to demand wholesale revision of the Constitution 

<<<PDFPAGE 126>>>
I1IO 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
and inquiries into the finances and into contested elections; they 
set up a multitude of committees to which government proposals 
were referred and never heard of again, and they refused to 
submit to any rules of procedure—'the House wants no chains’, 
cried Rossi. The Ministry might have kept some control if their 
followers had been united; but the Belmontists voted more often 
than not with the Democrats against the government; when 
Castelnuovo, Orleans and Montgomerie tried to instil some 
notion of Cabinet solidarity into Belmonte, he replied—perhaps 
with some truth—that he could not control his friends. “The 
Council never meets except in the Prince’s presence’, wrote 
Castelnuovo to Bentinck; ‘the Ministers are therefore often at 
odds over business.... Belmonte... behaves in such a way 
that if I did not know and esteem him I should say he was 
intriguing to make me leave the Ministry.” 
Both he and Belmonte felt that Parliament had fallen into a 
hopeless anarchy; the only remedy they could think of was a 
dissolution and the use of British force. Then, on July 18th and 
t9th, food riots broke out in Palermo and spread to the prisons: 
they were put down by the troops, but the Council (including 
the Prince and Montgomerie) panicked and took to unconsti- 
tutional measures: that is, they prorogued Parliament for a week 
without the proper procedure, and they set up a military com- 
mission to try the rioters. Belmonte now at last realised his 
mistake and made it up with Castelnuovo. But it was too late: 
when Parliament met again on the 27th both Houses were out 
of hand: not only did they persist in refusing supply, but they 
threatened to pass a vote of censure on the government’s 
handling of the riots. 
At this point the Ministry collapsed. Castelnuovo, as Minister 
of Finance, had borne the brunt of the Commons’ attacks: now, 
unable to obtain supply, and unwilling either to dissolve or to 
go on collecting taxes after the approaching end of the financial 
year, he resigned. The other Ministers, failing to dissuade him, 
resigned with him. ‘I foresee this egg is about to break’, the 
Prince told them; ‘I believe you had rather not have it break in 
your hands’.* Indeed they insisted on going out of office at once, 
and both they and their followers stopped attending Parliament 
altogether: so far as they were concerned, the ship of consti- 

<<<PDFPAGE 127>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
III 
tutional government was now quite adrift and beyond their 
help. 
Bentinck, hearing this news before Tarragona, felt ‘necessarily 
hurt at so bad a result to all the exertions I have made, but... 
much more disappointed for the discredit that it gives to the 
cause of freedom’. He had already warned Orleans that, with the 
Ministry hopelessly divided as it had been, ‘a new set of bold 
Patriots’ were likely to spring up and ‘put all that weak aris- 
tocracy to the rout’: if so the Prince and the British would have 
to ‘side with the strongest’. The government had lost control 
of Parliament 
‘by their own folly, their own want of energy alone, and then like the 
carter who prayed to Jupiter when his cart was in the mire, [they] 
complain of the apathy of the British Authorities, if by a timely parade 
of cannon through the Cassaro [the high street of Palermo], or by a 
nocturnal deportation of obnoxious citizens without enquiry or trial, a 
system of terror is not established’.* 
As for Parliament, Bentinck was not too alarmed by its 
alleged ‘Jacobin tendencies’, which he rather attributed to the 
inexperience of 
‘persons brought up in a country where... despotism... has estab- 
lished a rooted distrust and perhaps distaste towards all government, 
where education has been very bad, and instruction very limited, and 
where the freedom of the Press has not impressed upon the minds of the 
people that knowledge and justness of political principles and reasoning 
which discriminate the British Nation’. 
Bentinck therefore advised strongly against any recourse to 
the old despotic system of force, or even to prorogation or 
dissolution. The government, he said, should keep strictly within 
the law: Parliament should be given its head; if it should 
threaten the Constitution itself, the Prince could always use his 
power of veto. Of course Parliament might go on refusing 
supply. In that case 
‘there will and must follow a revolution, as the machine of government 
as established by the constitution will stop. But in the first place time will 
be gained by this process, during which ignorant but well-intentioned 
men may see their error; and they probably will avoid an extremity 
which will threaten their personal safety; for timidity I consider to be a 
peculiar characteristic of the Sicilians. In the second, if anything like 

<<<PDFPAGE 128>>>
Ii2 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
revolution is to take place, it had better proceed from embarrassments 
produced by the conduct of the Parliament. . 
. 
. The conduct of govern- 
ment should be marked with the utmost moderation. If in the end it is 
compelled to extraordinary measures, they should be so obviously just 
and necessary as to admit of no question. The Government has to act for 
the future as well as for the present time, and this forbearance cannot 
lead to active revolution as in France, because the whole military force 
is foreign, and in the country there are no native troops and no military 
spirit.”* 
In Palermo, meanwhile, the Prince had anticipated Bentinck’s 
advice by declining to dissolve and by appointing a new 
Ministry. He did not, indeed, bring in the successful democratic 
opposition—they frightened both him and Montgomerie too 
much. But he managed to find two officials (Marquis Ferreri 
and Duke Averna di Gualtieri), a soldier (Marshal Naselli) and 
an aristocrat (the Duke of Lucchesi Palli)—in other words, a 
parcel of nonentities—who were willing to carry on the govern- 
ment. 
To the minds of the outgoing constitutionalists—Belmonte 
and Orleans especially—the next step was simple: the new 
Ministers were to be mere puppets set up to carry through the 
dissolution they themselves had shirked; then the British would 
‘take the country in trust’, and Belmonte expected to ride back 
to power under that shield. But the British did not fall in with 
this convenient plan. After a few days’ hesitation Montgomerie 
decided, in agreement with the Prince, to give Parliament 
another chance of voting supply.* This move worked, if only 
temporarily: the threat of dissolution did what dissolution itself 
could not have done. Early in August the Commons partly 
overcame what Balsamo liked to call their ‘niggardness for the 
restoration of the desolated finances’*—at least so far as to pass, 
not indeed the whole Budget, but the Civil List and part of the 
army estimates, and to extend the financial year until November 
Ist: 
None the less Parliament was now wholly in the hands of 
the Democrats and King’s Friends, who grew still more dis- 
orderly as the summer wore on. Misunderstandings over the 
quarantine regulations culminated in a Commons motion to 
send an embassy to England to protest against the British gener- 

<<<PDFPAGE 129>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
IT3 
als’ misdeeds; while Montgomerie had again to threaten force 
and dissolution to forestall a motion in the Peers calling on the 
King to resume power. Party hatreds were still further em- 
bittered by the appearance of the first political newspapers ever 
seen in Sicily: amid the resulting uproar the Commons started 
setting up a cry for the Spanish Constitution, making forays 
into the executive and judicial powers, interfering with the 
courts, attempting to try one of their own members, and gener- 
ally giving an imitation—superficial and in little—of the French 
Convention. On September 11th Montgomerie, worn out with 
illness and worry, at last got a letter from Bentinck announcing 
his return: he therefore asked the Prince to prorogue Parliament 
for twenty days and so gain a breathing-space. The Prince 
agreed with some misgiving, but Parliament dispersed quietly 
enough on the 15th.* 
Bentinck had decided at the end of August that he would 
have to go back to Sicily and restore order. It was with con- 
siderable disgust that he gave up his military command, for 
he thought of himself as a soldier first and foremost. In the first 
revulsion of feeling he wrote to Castlereagh that such was 
‘the weakness of the Hereditary Prince, and such... the inca- 
pacity of any set of men who may be placed at the head of the 
Government; such also the silly, the interested and depraved 
character of the people’, that henceforth the British Minister 
would be unable to leave Sicily: he therefore asked to be relieved 
of the mission and so freed to follow his military calling; and 
a few days later he asked for leave in England to look after his 
Parliamentary seat and his private affairs.” This move was a 
mistake, for it later allowed Castlereagh to put an end to 
Bentinck’s mission without actually recalling him. For the 
moment, however, Castlereagh asked him to stay on, and tried 
to soothe his disappointment: ‘I know of no hands in which the 
British influence can at present be satisfactorily placed except in 
Your Lordship’s.. . .’ He felt it unnecessary to send further instruc- 
tions, merely approving ‘the generalline of policy’ which Bentinck 
had recommended to the Prince and Montgomerie. 
A month 
later Castlereagh added that, although Bentinck would be sacri- 
ficing his military career, 

<<<PDFPAGE 130>>>
II4 
BENTINCK AND, SICILY 
‘ 
.. yet Iam persuaded that as the new constitutional system of Sicily 
is in part a creation of your own... you will not be disposed to hazard 
its fate in all time to come by confiding it prematurely to other hands. 
I shall not consider the island safe till we see the Parliament acting cordially 
with the Government, of which as yet there are no visible symptoms’.* 
‘In part a creation of your own’: the words were double- 
edged; for Bentinck was shortly to find that the Sicilian Consti- 
tution had become, in English eyes, a very dubious offspring 
for him to acknowledge. The same generals and admirals who 
had first greeted it with jubilation now inveighed against the 
Sicilians as ‘miserable wretches’, ‘a degraded government and 
corrupt people’, fit only to be dealt with by martial law, like 
the Luddites at home. Bunbury talked of giving up the island 
altogether except for two or three fortresses, and leaving the 
Sicilians to stew in their own juice. Liston, remembering his 
Montesquieu, doubted ‘whether the natives of a hot climate were 
susceptible of the happiness of a free government’.* From 
London Bentinck heard that the 1813 Parliament had set nearly 
everyone against the Constitution, and that he was beginning 
to be blamed for it: 
“On est persuadé qu’en laissant faire aux Siciliens leur Constitution, 
vous n’en n’avez pas eu l’ordre positif du Ministére, mais que vous avez 
agi d’aprés vos propres lumieéres et sur la Carte blanche . 
. 
. que le Ministére 
vous avait donné... il y a méme tel Ministre qui le dit cuvertement. II 
est probable que si la confection de la Constitution avait réussi, les 
Ministres auraient dit quils l’avaient ordonnée. C’est ainsi que va le 
monde.”* 
Bentinck landed at Palermo once again on October 4th. By 
this time his spirits had risen and he was fairly confident of 
setting matters to rights by ‘some bullying and some coaxing’; 
however extravagant the recent behaviour of Parliament, he 
thought, the country at large would still look to the British for 
guidance. Next day he met the Prince and the new Ministers. 
He had come ‘to bring these gentlemen to reason’, he told them: 
‘if Sicily was not true to herself—if instead of pursuing with 
wisdom her own good and the final establishment of the Consti- 
tution her representatives chose to be governed by their own 
passion... despotism itself was preferable and as I had been 

<<<PDFPAGE 131>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
I1l§ 
the first to give it [ie. liberty] so I should be the first to take 
it away if I found it to be incompatible with the happiness of 
the Nation.’ But he wished first of all to negotiate with the Parlia- 
mentary leaders and if possible bring them to their senses—that is, 
induce them to promise, before Parliament met again, that they 
would vote the Budget at once. He therefore asked for a further 
prorogation until the 1sth: the Ministers, anxious to conciliate 
Parliament, objected, but with the Prince’s support Bentinck 
carried his point.* 
Bentinck then set about conferring both with the Parliamen- 
tary leaders and with his defeated friends the constitutionalists. 
His meeting with the latter was melancholy: he dined with 
them and toasted ‘les véritables amis de la Constitution’, but 
they were shame-faced at the mess they had made of the 
government in Bentinck’s absence. There could be no question 
of their being returned to power by British force, as the in- 
corrigible Belmonte still hoped he might be, ‘like the Vizier of 
a Sultan’. About Belmonte Bentinck was especially disillusioned: 
politically he seemed quite hopeless, “dangerous as an opponent, 
still more dangerous as a friend’; both he and Castelnuovo were 
now too unpopular to make possible Ministers. The best thing 
his friends could do, Bentinck told them, would be to form a 
compact and active opposition, 
‘the Peers protesting—the 
Commoners speaking and dividing the House upon all occasions. 
It was necessary that there should be a standard round which 
the good might rally.’ They agreed readily enough, but they 
were plainly demoralised and frightened by the turn their own 
Constitution had taken: their countrymen, they concluded, 
were by no means so civilised as they had imagined. Balsamo 
was already drafting the plan of a revised Constitution which 
would strengthen the executive and judiciary: he proposed a 
partial return to the old system, and the deletion of ‘all abstract 
maxims concerning liberty, equality and the like’; for, he said, 
‘in France the rights of man did more harm than anything else’.* 
Bentinck’s negotiations with the Parliamentary leaders did 
not go as smoothly as he had hoped. He began by telling Rossi 
and Vaccaro that while he meant to stand by the Constitution 
‘as long as possible’, he would not tolerate anarchy: if they 
agreed to vote the Budget, however, he for his part undertook 

<<<PDFPAGE 132>>>
I16 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
that they would not be dissolved, as they feared, immediately 
afterwards. If they wanted freedom they must make some sacri- 
fices for it—‘I would have them independent both of their 
enemies and of their allies and I should not use this language to 
them if Great Britain wanted to keep them in subjection.’ But 
they disbelieved him, and steadily refused to consider voting 
supply before the very end of the session, even though Bentinck 
threatened that ‘for the sake of the Sicilian Nation of which I 
was the advocate...I was determined to dictate the law to 
them and I would do so’. After several inconclusive meetings, 
Bentinck began to suspect that evil influences had been at work— 
perhaps the French, perhaps the King: the events of that summer, 
he perceived, had damaged the prestige both of the British and 
of the new ‘system’ more than he had thought. As often before, 
he regretted his early conciliatory policy: when Rossi remarked, 
perhaps sarcastically, that ‘it was unlucky England had not con- 
tinued them in their slavery for one night longer and given 
them the Constitution ready made’, Bentinck noted it as a 
‘curious and just remark’: had it been possible in the first place 
to work through the King alone (he began to think) his task 
would have been much easier. A further prorogation—which 
Bentinck obtained from the Ministry only by officially taking 
responsibility for it—failed to bring about any agreement, and 
Parliament met again on October 2oth. 
Bentinck now decided to carry the Budget in the Commons 
by force of numbers. He mustered 53 constitutionalists—my 
53'—and drove them back to the House they had abandoned 
in July; he also counted on a further (and sufficient) number of 
votes which the new Ministers assured him they could scrape 
together. But the Ministers—whether from ‘perfidy’ or impo- 
tence—failed to deliver the goods: the motion was lost by 61 
to 53. Bentinck at once went to the Prince and demanded a 
new Ministry and a dissolution. 
He was furious: ‘If I had known as much of the general 
character of the people as I did at present,’ he said, ‘I do not 
think I should ever have undertaken the business. I doubted 
whether these people were made for freemen.’ If the next 
Parliament was no better than this, ‘the sooner the country 
reverted to another form of government the better’.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 133>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
II7 
What Bentinck was demanding was in fact the suspension 
of the Constitution, for the government would shortly have 
to start collecting taxes without Parliamentary authority. Why 
did he take such a step, against his own advice earlier in the 
summer not to do so except in the last resort? 
No doubt Parliament if allowed to go on sitting would have 
kept up its practice of voting taxes a month at a time, and so 
would have been in a fair way to become a Sicilian Long 
Parliament. But the government could have carried on some- 
how, and Bentinck’s control of the army would have prevented 
anything like revolution. No doubt, too, Bentinck felt that on 
the question of the Budget he had committed himself too far 
to retreat. But even he seems to have felt the need of some other 
justification, for he told Castlereagh that what had compelled 
him to act was 
‘all the bad signs that preceded .. 
. the French Revolution. ... Agrarian 
law, universal suffrage and provisions at low prices were preached aloud 
among the people. Contempt was endeavoured to be impressed towards 
the British army. Their small effective force was contrasted with the 
numerical strength of the nation... .’ 
How true or significant this was we cannot easily tell: we 
are ill informed about what was going on in the depths of the 
country, away from the rather empty hubbub of Palermo. But 
it is difficult not to suspect that Bentinck, for all his complaints 
of the Sicilians’ unworthiness, was emotionally committed to 
making the ‘system’ work, to ‘regenerating’ Sicily; and that 
that was why he took on a responsibility greater than his mere 
duty as British Minister warranted. Though his work was now 
‘all to do over again’, he meant to save Sicily from herself by 
using present force for her future good. 
This was made plain when Bentinck turned to the task of 
forming a new Ministry. The Prince liked his present Ministers, 
who were accommodating though weak; but Bentinck would 
not have them, and quickly dropped the pretence of not himself 
suggesting who should replace them: the new Ministers were 
to be constitutionalists, and the Prince openly treated Bentinck 
as the head of the party. But it proved very difficult to find 
anyone willing to serve: Castelnuovo and Belmonte were out 

<<<PDFPAGE 134>>>
118 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
of the running, while all the others dreaded ‘the violence of the 
Parliament and the national proneness to vengeance and blood’. 
Bentinck felt hurt: 
‘I stated to them the hardship of my being deserted by everybody. . . .’ 
It would have been much easier for him to withdraw to Messina and 
allow the Constitution to collapse: ‘if I went through with it as I was 
prepared to do, I should have to undertake a great deal of responsibility 
and I was to do all this in conjunction with men whom I did not know 
and of whose principles I could not be assured.”* 
None the less it took ten days to patch up a Ministry. Of those 
who finally came in Settimo (War) at first would not serve 
without Belmonte; Villafranca 
(Foreign Affairs)—a young 
Belmontist nobleman “more respected for goodness of character 
than for abilities’**—had been one of the five barons arrested in 
1811, and his fearful wife had to be pacified before he would 
serve; Carini (Justice) was no more use than he had ever been; 
and Gaetano Bonanno (Finance), though ‘a sensible bold man’, 
was a lawyer turned Government official. 
Even then there remained the question, by what authority 
were taxes to be collected after November ist, when the Parlia- 
mentary grant lapsed? The Prince wished to issue a proclamation 
in his own name and the Ministers’; but ‘the whole of them 
were alarmed and wished to have nothing to do with it’. The 
proclamation was therefore drawn up in Bentinck’s name alone 
—without even a mention of his functions as Captain-General; 
in it he made himself responsible for the safety and tranquillity 
of the kingdom until Parliament should meet again, and an- 
nounced that he would court-martial ‘disturbers of the public 
peace, murderers, and whatever other enemies of the Consti- 
tution may oppose the policy of the Government’. On October 
30th the proclamation was published; troops marched into the 
interior to ensure order; and Parliament was dissolved. Bentinck 
was now in fact dictator of Sicily.* 
Reflecting on this turn of events, Bentinck consoled himself 
with the thought that ‘in the progress from slavery to liberty, 
more or less disorder must have taken place’: it was better that 
it should have come at a time when a salutary check might 
still bring the Sicilians to their senses. 

<<<PDFPAGE 135>>>
THE CONSTITUTION FAILS 
IIQ 
‘Sicily’, he wrote to Castlereagh, ‘can only be saved from political 
convulsions by our interference. As the Constitution takes effect and as 
the liberty of the Press and their new institutions make an impression 
upon the opinion and character of the people, it may be hoped that 
more correct sentiments of private and public honour will prevail. The 
country must rapidly increase in wealth and strength—all the obstacles 
opposed by the feudal system and a bad government to the prosperity 
of the agriculture and commerce of the country are rapidly removing— 
they are clamorous for all the advantages of freedom but nobody will 
submit to the sacrifices—nobody will pay or serve. This is shortly the 
state of the country. It may become powerful and independent if the 
constitution succeeds and the nation is true to itself—but if not, and in 
the interval of its infancy and weakness, it must be the province of Arcot 
with its Nabob, which if not governed by us, will be by our enemy. 
The management required is to make the interposition of this influence 
the general object of desire both of the Prince and of the People.’* 

<<<PDFPAGE 136>>>
CHAPTER 
tf 
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM 
‘The Vicar kept quiet, while Bentinck alone acted and laid down the 
law, running about from one department to the next, writing instructions 
himself and having them written at his will.”* 
So the King heard at the beginning of November 1813. He 
was not misinformed: Bentinck was now, as Orleans said, ‘the 
first Minister of the country’, and the secretaries of state were 
his ‘clerks’. With them he discussed measures of policy and 
government appointments; while the British generals and agents 
in other parts of Sicily acted as unofficial political representatives, 
recommending local supporters of the Constitution for the 
places and favours which Bentinck could obtain for them. 
Actually, Bentinck’s power was rather that of a monarch than 
of a Prime Minister. Great nobles called him in as arbiter of 
their family disputes; such conservative barons 
as Butera, 
Pantelleria, San Marco, Gargallo—some of whom had been 
unseated by the Patriotic victory of 1812—now sought to in- 
gratiate themselves with him; the Senates of various towns 
apostrophised him as the Moses of Sicily.* Criticism was stifled 
at the outset of his dictatorship by the arrest, trial, and im- 
prisonment of two nobles who had published violent attacks 
on him—one of them, ironically, was Anjou, one of the ‘five 
barons’ whom the Court had arbitrarily arrested in 1811.* 
According to a story current in Palermo, the Prince commented 
on this state of affairs by telling his wife one day ‘“This morning 
I had to wait a long time in Council for His Majesty’—his 
wife, astonished, answered, “What, the King?”—‘No, I was 
waiting for Lord Bentinck” —and laughing he went away’.* 
The country was outwardly quiet. From the officers he had 
sent to garrison the interior Bentinck heard that the great 
majority of the people appeared to feel nothing but gratitude 
for British protection. The Royal Family also seemed perfectly 
docile. The Prince effaced himself, called Bentinck his ‘ange 
tutelaire’, and professed the most commendable feelings; but 
Bentinck, though willing to grant that he was the ablest of his 
family—his chief defect being mere weakness—never quite 

<<<PDFPAGE 137>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
I2I 
trusted him again. The King kept ostentatiously aloof, though 
privately he posed as the only man really attached to the English 
Constitution, which Bentinck’s proclamation had infringed. He 
continued to associate with such conservatives as Cassaro, Trabia 
and the Archbishop of Palermo, and—Bentinck thought—to 
encourage them in their latent hostility to the ‘system’. To a 
hint that he should end this silent opposition the King replied 
blandly that he had not the slightest notion of what was going 
on (‘the greatest imaginable lie’). Bentinck hoped to keep him 
sweet by playing on his hopes of Naples; indeed, as will be seen, 
the swift changes then taking place on the Continent brought 
the two men closer together, and Bentinck went so far as to 
appear at an interview, to the King’s gratification, with his hair 
symbolically powdered. But this did not stop the King (as 
Bentinck was aware) from playing his own hand in his corre- 
spondence with the Sicilian envoys abroad; he was no more 
reconciled to the Constitution than he had ever been; and he 
might be expected to overthrow it as soon as British power 
was withdrawn.* 
The noisy factions of the 1813 Parliament seemed to have 
melted away. The constitutionalists themselves were now little 
more than a set of place-holders kept in power by Bentinck’s 
will. Even so, the dissensions which had begun when they 
formed a real party perpetuated themselves within the narrow 
circle of Ministry and Council. The formation of the new 
government had been a papering over of cracks, so much so 
that care had been taken to give the Belmontists and Villermo- 
sists equal representation. This balance was soon upset, however: 
Bonanno, the new Minister of Finance—who held the key post 
in the government, since as Bentinck’s right-hand man he 
controlled the local officials and dispensed patronage—had come 
in as a presumed Villermosist, but soon went over to Belmonte. 
As a result Castelnuovo and Balsamo began to complain—prob- 
ably with some justice—that he was making an unscrupulous 
use of his powers by wholesale dismissals and promotions of 
officials and by unwarranted interference in local affairs. Bel- 
monte, for his part, had no more cause for satisfaction: Bentinck, 
taught by experience, was determined not to let him be more 
than a figurehead.* These enduring differences did not matter 

<<<PDFPAGE 138>>>
122 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
much so long as Bentinck remained there to impose a semblance 
of unity; but they were enough to suggest that the constitution- 
alist rump would not long survive Bentinck’s departure. 
With Bentinck’s dictatorship established and unchallenged, 
the question might have been asked, what kind of political 
settlement would it lead to? The short answer was that Sicily’s 
pocket revolution, having followed the course of its English 
and French exemplars through constitutional monarchy and 
parliamentary anarchy, was now entering a phase equivalent, 
at any rate in the conception, to the Protectorate or the Consu- 
late. Political liberty had been tried and found wanting; so a 
kind of benevolent despotism was to take its place, with en- 
lightened but authoritarian reform as its aim. 
Here Bentinck’s attitude was ambiguous. The official opinion 
he gave in his despatches home was that the people—except for 
some of the barons, whom the abolition of feudalism had 
alienated—shewed ‘perfect unanimity’ in support of the Consti- 
tution; but that, still suffering as they were from the after-effects of 
misrule, they would be unable for some years to shield it from 
the attacks of a few mischief-makers. So, although ‘our enemies 
are a faction, our support a people’, an interval of British 
tutelage would be necessary, at the end of which Sicily would 
have been made safe for political freedom, and the Constitution 
could be restored to its full and free operation.* Bentinck’s 
personal opinion is not so clear. He seems never to have given 
up the hope that eventually the free Constitution might be made 
to work. But for the present he had few illusions. If the King 
were to abolish the Constitution tomorrow, he remarked, ‘not 
a hand would be lifted against so arbitrary an act’. The best he 
could hope for was that the international situation would keep 
Sicily “free in spite of herself and all her efforts to the contrary’. 
In conversation with members of the former opposition, he 
said that while he remained in Sicily he hoped ‘to hold up to 
Europe a whole nation united in the cause of liberty’, but in 
any case he would carry the thing through ‘bon gré mal gré’: 
‘Tt would have been much better if I had done the work in three 
months... but I did not know the nation as well before as since... 
they never reasoned upon what they saw but upon what they supposed 

<<<PDFPAGE 139>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
123 
—They were led by their imagination and not their reason—-No man 
calculated that 2 and 2 make 4—It was either 5 and 6 or 2 and 3—They 
were poets. * 
Similarly, in conversation with Sperlinga (a refractory noble 
whom he threatened to banish from Palermo), Bentinck antici- 
pated a later statesman by declaring that the Sicilians were to 
be managed only by “Bonsbons in one hand and il bastone in 
the other’. In fact, for practical and immediate purposes Bentinck 
had given up any interest in political liberty and representative 
government. He did not care who the members of the next 
Parliament might be, he said—he would make them do their 
duty in any case. If the barons, as he expected, kept up their 
hostility, ‘I was resolved’ (he told Sperlinga) ‘to abolish the 
order altogether’.* 
A distinctly anti-aristocratic tone, together with an implied 
contempt for the representative institutions he had himself 
helped to set up—these seem to account for Bentinck’s state of 
mind during his brief career as benevolent despot. A friend of 
his put it more plainly still: 
‘Tout le monde se demande, que fera-t-on pour la Sicile? Pour moi 
je demanderois, que mérite-t-elle qu’on fasse pour elle? Rien, si l’on ne 
considérait que la nonchalance et l’immoralité des nobles et des prétres, 
la scélératesse de la seconde classe de la population, celle des gens de loi 
et des agents des nobles tous compris sous le nom de Paglietti. Mais la 
populace des campagnes, les agriculteurs, les artisans de l’intérieur de l’ile 
ont quelques vertus, et ils ont besoin d’étre soulagés du pouvoir trop 
arbitraire du souverain, de la tyrannie des nobles et de la rapacité des 
PagliettiimCe fut la votre objet quand vous laissates faire une Consti- 
tution. Il semble démontré maintenant que pour étre utile 4 toutes les 
classes il ne faut laisser faire 4 aucune, mais leur faire faire par force ce 
qui leur convient.”* 
Drastic and effective reform of the administration was a need 
more essential than representative government: ‘both Parlia- 
ments’, Bentinck thought, ‘had been pursuing the shadow and 
neglecting the substance.’ His mood was far removed from the 
orthodox Whiggism usually attributed to him: in fact Balsamo 
complained that his chief defect was the unsystematic and excess- 
ively pragmatic way he dealt with particular problems. He was 

<<<PDFPAGE 140>>>
124 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
willing, for instance, to throw over the experiment in economic 
liberalism (which had caused much trouble) so far as to acquiesce 
in fixed prices for necessities. In general, too, he behaved less 
and less like a British agent, more and more like a Sicilian ruler: 
when Anglo-Sicilian disputes recurred over the quarantine regu- 
lations, he backed the Sicilian Board of Health; and in his 
despatches home he scarcely mentioned his part in domestic 
politics, merely stating that the island was perfectly quiet.* 
The need for reform was greatest in the financial and judicial 
organisation of the country, which the last two Parliaments had 
done no more than upset without definitely settling on a new 
system. Balsamo’s plan for financial reform, passed by the 1810 
Parliament, had laid a new uniform tax on all land and called 
for a thorough survey as a basis for assessment: the logical 
consequence should have been the end of the barons’ virtual 
exemption from taxation. But in November 1813 the land 
survey—the pet project of reformers, both Neapolitan and 
Sicilian, ever since the 1780’s—had still not been carried out; 
the land tax had had to be collected on the basis of voluntary 
declarations of value (riveli); as a result the barons evaded tax- 
ation more successfully than ever. Small landowners, whom 
Balsamo had meant to benefit, had actually suffered, and so 
had the Exchequer. Now, however, the new Minister Bonanno 
at last set out in earnest to prepare the essential survey. At the 
same time he overhauled the government service: no doubt he 
meant above all to exert political influence in the constituencies, 
but he achieved other things as well: a number of redundant 
offices, relics of the old regime, were swept away, and the 
Ministers gained control of their own officials—which was more 
than Castelnuovo or Belmonte had had. How far Bentinck had 
a hand in these practical reforms is not clear, though he and 
Bonanno were certainly in close touch. That they were not 
begun until Bentinck took over the government is at any rate 
significant. He certainly took an interest in financial reform, and 
he advocated a more equal distribution of landed property.* 
Judicial reform was an equally pressing problem. Bentinck had 
already complained to Castlereagh of ‘the total impotence of 
the laws, venally administered, giving no protection, and there- 
fore imposing no check upon the lively imaginations and warm 

<<<PDFPAGE 141>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
125 
passions of the people’. More murders, he reported, were prob- 
ably committed in Palermo than in the rest of Europe put to- 
gether, Sardinia and Corsica excepted. The new Constitution 
had begun to undo the old system without setting up new codes 
or carrying out Castelnuovo’s plan for the reorganisation of the 
courts. Bentinck took a great interest in this question: it was 
uppermost in his mind when at the end of November he set 
out on a month’s tour of Sicily. He was also concerned, on this 
occasion, with ensuring that the right people were elected to 
the forthcoming Parliament, but (he told the Prince) ‘my great 
object was the administration of justice—to hear what the people 
wanted’; and he returned much impressed with ‘the prepotenza 
[bullying] that generally prevailed—the result I believed of the 
feudal system and of the administration of justice being in the 
hands of the barons’. Early in 1814 he discussed judicial reorgani- 
sation with Bonanno; and throughout this period a committee 
of the Council sat to work out new codes.” But all these attempts 
at reform were cut short by the swift turn of events on the 
Continent; they bore fruit only after the Restoration, when 
Sicilian independence and the British protectorate had both 
come toanend. Abortive though they were, they mattered in that 
they shewed what might have happened in Sicily if the protect- 
orate had gone on. With Bentinck’s dictatorship the wheel had, 
besides, come full circle, for his policy was now very like that 
of the former Neapolitan Viceroys—authoritarian in manner, 
‘enlightened’ and anti-aristocratic in purpose. The difference 
was perhaps not so much that Bentinck still hoped Sicily might 
later become fit for free institutions, as that his policy was 
intended to make and keep her independent; while the Viceroys 
had been very distinctly the agents of Neapolitan centralisation. 
But all this (it quickly became clear), and indeed the future of 
the British protectorate itself, must depend on the fate of the 
Continent. Of this Bentinck was well aware; immersed though 
he was in Sicilian affairs he kept an eye on Italy, whose inde- 
pendence he still hoped for. When he set out on his tour of Sicily 
in late November he had not yet heard of the negotiations 
between Austria and Murat which were to lead to a treaty of 
alliance between those two powers, and then to an Anglo- 
Neapolitan armistice. But he had received instructions from 

<<<PDFPAGE 142>>>
126 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
Bathurst and Castlereagh to give Austria his military and diplo- 
matic cooperation in Italy, if need be to the point of recognising 
—sub spe ratiMurat’s possession of Naples, and accepting the 
principle of compensation for the Bourbons. This Bentinck did 
not like at all. Murat, he thought, by taking part in the battle of 
Leipzig on Napoleon’s side (something Castlereagh did not 
know about when he drew up his instructions) had altered 
matters and made so great a concession inadvisable. None the 
less great and critical changes were plainly about to take place. 
How then did Italy and Sicily stand ?* 
In Italy—Bentinck agreed with his Italian soldier friends at 
Palermo, the Piedmontese Sallier de la Tour and the Friulan 
Catinelli—it was ‘necessary to do something to be beforehand if 
possible with the old sovereigns and to give the people an oppor- 
tunity of asserting their rights, liberty and independence’. True, a 
small-scale landing on the Tuscan coast, which Bentinck ordered 
as an experiment late in November, proved disappointing: the 
troops—ltalian volunteers under Catinelli—met with no re- 
sponse from the local population and had to re-embark. But a 
larger force might yet do better: the setback did not stop 
speculation about the chance of creating an independent Italian 
kingdom; and Bentinck’s private Secretary James Graham was 
not alone in seeing him as a possible ‘Lord Wellington of Italy, 
only with this marked superiority—he would “draw the sword 
to create a free government’.* 
Sicily, however, was another matter. When Bentinck talked 
of raising Italy and forestalling the old sovereigns, he meant 
North and Central Italy; the Two Sicilies he reckoned as quite 
separate politically from the rest of the peninsula, and he seems 
to have assumed like everyone else that if the Allies won the 
war the Bourbons would be restored to Naples.* What would 
then become of Sicily’s independence and constitution? As early 
as November 20th Bentinck warned the constitutionalists that 
England could not be expected to go on garrisoning the island 
after a peace. He already had good reason to guess that the 
British Government would do little to safeguard Sicily from 
Neapolitan reaction. Already Castlereagh had asked Lamb 
—Bentinck’s former Secretary—‘When is all this interference 
to cease?’ Three months later, on hearing that Sicily was quiet 

<<<PDFPAGE 143>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
127 
under martial law, Castlereagh ‘smiled and said: “do you 
know, after all I am of opinion, that is the only kind of govern- 
ment for which they are fit.””’* In the circumstances—Bentinck 
told his Sicilian friends—they ought to induce the Prince to 
nominate a Sicilian Ambassador in London, for Castelcicala was 
the King’s personal agent and actually did his best to discredit 
the Sicilian government; Belmonte—he suggested—might, if he 
undertook the mission, prove ‘the saviour of the country’. But 
Belmonte, overconfident as usual, was convinced that England 
would never abandon Sicily, on which (as he thought) the 
safety of Malta depended; though the matter was discussed 
again and again, Bentinck could make no impression on him, 
and nothing was done.* Clearly, the future of Sicily—of her 
independence, of the free institutions she might eventually be 
trusted with, of the practical reforms that were being launched— 
would depend not on the Sicilians themselves, but on the course 
of British policy. 
It was in these circumstances that Bentinck, during his tour 
of Sicily in November and December 1813, brought to light 
the notorious ‘philosophic dream’ which, if carried into effect, 
would have made of the island ‘after Ireland, the brightest jewel 
in the British crown’. 
Bentinck’s tour through the towns of the East coast and the 
interior was remarkable in that, for the first time since the 
Court’s flight from Naples, someone concerned with the govern- 
ment of Sicily took the trouble to visit it extensively. Bentinck, 
who like the Royal Family had until then scarcely stirred out 
of Palermo and its environs, was much struck: ‘it was impossible 
to know the country without having seen it’, he told the Prince 
on his return. His was a quasi-royal progress; everywhere he was 
received with banquets, balls, triumphal arches, fireworks, and 
allegorical tableaux of England and Sicily embracing. He ‘ha- 
rangued’ the local councils, urging them to see that the bricconi 
of the 1813 Parliament were not re-elected; he interviewed 
place-seekers, discussed local affairs with the leading citizens, 
and returned, as has been seen, convinced of the need to strike at 
aristocratic quite as much as at royal ‘prepotenza’ in local affairs. 
Though the opposition was no doubt merely silent rather than 
reconciled, still Bentinck’s tour was to all appearances a triumph.” 

<<<PDFPAGE 144>>>
128 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
On December 3rd, travelling between Messina and Catania, 
Bentinck thought out, and, in a letter written that evening, put 
to the Prince his ‘réves d’un voyageur’. The dilemma which 
faced them both—he told the Prince—was that while Sicily was 
not yet fit to govern herself alone, still she would not let herself 
be reunited to Naples without bloodshed. He therefore proposed 
that Sicily should be governed by Britain. Such an arrangement, 
he said, would be advantageous to all parties. Sicily would 
prosper; from a burden to Naples she would turn into a friendly 
neighbour, whose dependence on a Power without continental 
ambitions would make impossible any conflict between the two 
kingdoms; and the Royal Family could be compensated either 
with additional territory on the Continent, or—more likely— 
with a British subsidy equivalent to the Sicilian Civil List, and 
with military aid.* 
Bentinck’s proposal was ill-judged; but in itself it was not 
fantastic. Since the beginning of the eighteenth century Sicily 
had passed from Spain to Savoy, from Savoy to Austria, and 
finally from Austria to the Neapolitan Bourbons. It might have 
taken one more change in its stride. The Queen had talked of 
selling it to England or of bartering it against help towards the 
recovery of Naples. The Prince secretly considered Bentinck’s 
proposal of compensation on the mainland.* As for the Sicilians, 
they wanted independence; but they wanted it chiefly because 
they disliked Neapolitan domination and could not forget their 
own former glories; they hardly thought in terms of the modern 
national state, and such a man as Belmonte was quite ready to 
see a permanent British protectorate. Of Italy “beyond the 
Garigliano’ (the frontier between Naples and the Papal States) 
they were, as Michele Amari later said, scarcely aware;* of the 
notion that Sicily might help to make up a united Italy not a 
trace is to be found in this period. So if one looks at Sicily alone, 
rather than at the international situation, Bentinck’s proposed 
settlement was at least conceivable. 
What led Bentinck to propose it? The question has exercised 
a number of historians. Balsamo attributed the ‘dream’ 
to 
‘bizzarria’, Weil to overweening personal ambition, while 
Bianco thought it a mere manoeuvre aimed at the unreconciled 
King’s Friends. None of this is very convincing. Then certain 

<<<PDFPAGE 145>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
129 
Italian writers—among them such an historian as Croce—sus- 
pected that Bentinck’s letter wasa token of some deep-laid British 
design on Sicily.” Bentinck’s own explanation—which, repent- 
ing at leisure, he sent off a few days later in reply to the Prince’s 
agitated enquiries—was this: 
‘The Sogno Filosofico ... was the offspring of my disordered brain 
alone, was brought to light for the first time in the journey from Messina 
to Catania, and has its derivation from no other source whatever.’* 
However that may be, one thing is plain: the British Govern- 
ment knew nothing about the ‘dream’ until well after it had 
been put forward, and even then the Foreign Office (though 
not Castlereagh) first heard of it from Castelcicala. It is also 
clear that the Government’s policy before and after the incident 
was quite opposed to anything like the annexation of Sicily; 
and Bentinck knew this and said so. Since the evidence was 
published Italian historians have accepted it and left it at that.* 
But, as sometimes happens, what one set of documents es- 
tablishes a fresh set of documents calls in question. The notion 
that Bentinck had a brainwave and Castlereagh dismissed it out 
of hand must now be qualified. 
The annexation of Sicily was not a new idea. In 1806-7 
Howick and Grenville had thought of establishing a permanent 
protectorate over the island; and Sir John Moore had put it 
more bluntly still. But neither Canning nor Wellesley nor 
Castlereagh had ever mentioned it or, so far as we can see, 
thought about it. Bentinck himself, until his return from Spain 
in October 1813, always assumed that Sicily was on the way to 
becoming her own mistress. Britain might keep some permanent 
influence over her, largely for reasons of geography, but that 
was all. Since his return, however, Bentinck had come much 
more openly to doubt her ability to secure her own inde- 
pendence. He himself had had to become in effect a dictator 
rather than a mere envoy. It may be that at some point in 
October or November Bentinck began to form the idea of 
making this British rule permanent; and that what he saw on 
his tour precipitated it. He had been used—as he told Castle- 
reagh—freely to discuss with the Prince all large matters of 

<<<PDFPAGE 146>>>
130 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
policy, and the Prince for his part encouraged him to do so. 
Now, all-powerful as he seemed to be, and eager to test the 
Prince’s reaction, Bentinck may have forgotten the difference 
between a passing remark in conversation and a letter, however 
private. Why did he act so hastily? When, two months later, he 
explained his act to Castlereagh, Bentinck recapitulated the 
dangers that had faced Sicily at the time of his letter: the after- 
effects of misrule; the King’s undying hostility to the ‘system’; 
the prospect of a Neapolitan reaction at a peace; and he affirmed 
his belief that 
‘Sicily, in the hands of Great Britain, would become in a very few 
years a source of wealth and strength, and after Ireland, the brightest 
jewel in the British Crown. The capacity of the country exceeds all 
belief. It is only necessary to have seen it fully to believe all the accounts 
transmitted by history of its former greatness. But I affix more importance 
to the character that Great Britain will acquire by the successful establish- 
ment of Sicilian liberty than to the value of Sicily as a British possession. 
I wish Sicily to exist as a perpetual example of the blessings of our 
friendship, in comparison to that of France; and, in the event of a new 
war, which, if Buonaparte lives, must soon recur, I would have Sicily 
become not only the model, but the instrument of Italian independence.”* 
Here Bentinck seems to have been perfectly sincere. We 
should not underrate the impact of a Sicilian tour on a mind 
formed by a classical education. As Croce points out, down to 
the latter half of the nineteenth century educated men sent up 
the parrot cry that Naples was one of the most fertile and blessed 
countries in the world because they trusted more to the lessons 
of the past than to the evidence of their own senses.* Bentinck 
could see well enough that Sicily was in a poor way. Messina, 
which he left on the morning of December 3rd, was politically 
‘the worst disposed place I have seen’; and the ‘horrible road’ 
along which he drove to Catania spoke for the long effects of 
misrule. (Bentinck had often lectured the Prince on the inestim- 
able benefits which a good road system would bring to Sicily), 
But on the way there was Taormina in its ruined magnificence, 
which he had not seen before—a reminder that Sicily had been 
great and prosperous in happier times and might become so 
once more; though now, it seemed, only through British pro- 
tection.” 

<<<PDFPAGE 147>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
131 
At all events Bentinck seems to have acted on impulse, 
without realising what strange reading his letter would make at 
Palermo. The Prince at once told his father; both of them were 
highly alarmed and thought they saw their worst suspicions 
confirmed. Both confided their fears to Castelcicala even before 
Bentinck had had time to answer the Prince’s anxious request for 
an explanation. But they wrote separately, and phrased their 
despatches so cautiously as to throw on Castelcicala the onus of 
any action he might take in London: when it came out that Castel- 
cicala had used the ‘dream’ to demand Bentinck’s recall, father 
and son accused one another of having put him up to it. For 
the moment, however, the incident was closed: Bentinck ha- 
stened to withdraw his proposal unconditionally; and he dis- 
sociated it wholly from British policy.* But the Prince, Bentinck 
noted, even while he rejected the idea of ceding Sicily to Britain, 
had dropped the remark that anyhow the proposed compen- 
sation would not be enough. This, Bentinck thought, was only 
‘the ordinary proceeding of Italian intrigue, with the view of 
making a better bargain’;* so, although he took care not to 
raise the matter again with the Prince, he continued to nurse it 
in his mind. 
But in the meantime changes were taking place in Europe 
which soon made even less likely the realisation of Bentinck’s 
dream. Only a few days after writing the notorious letter 
Bentinck heard for the first time of the negotiations then going 
on at Naples between Murat and the Austrian agent Neipperg. 
These led, on January 11th 1814, to the Austro-Neapolitan 
treaty of recognition and alliance; and, on February 3rd, to an 
Anglo-Neapolitan armistice. The treaty Bentinck thought “im- 
politic, inexpedient and unnecessary ... a sad violation of all 
public and private principle’;—the armistice he resisted as long 
as he could—that is, until he heard from Aberdeen, the new 
Ambassador to Austria, that the British Government approved 
of the negotiations. But even when he resigned himself to going 
briefly to Naples and signing the armistice he insisted on a mere 
cessation of hostilities; he would not formally recognise Murat’s 
hold on Naples. This stubbornness annoyed the Austrians and 
Murat, but—since Aberdeen had accepted the Austrian plan too 
hastily in the first place—it restored the balance of British policy; 

<<<PDFPAGE 148>>>
132 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
and it proved convenient to Castlereagh then and later. What 
the new relationship between England and Naples did not suit 
at all was Bentinck’s own plans both for Italy and for Sicily.” 
In Sicily the ‘dream’ was now obviously out of the question 
in its original form, since it did not look as though the Bourbons 
would ever get back to Naples. ‘I was however resolved to 
stick by Sicily,’ Bentinck declared; and he thought it might 
still be possible to maintain the British protectorate de facto if 
not de jure: for while Murat was at Naples the Bourbons could 
not do without the British garrison in Sicily.* Immediately after 
the signing of the armistice, therefore, Bentinck started moving 
on two fronts—the British and Sicilian—to secure the ‘system’ 
as far as he could. 
Castlereagh by this time was already on the Continent, at 
allied headquarters. To him Bentinck sent his private secretary, 
James Graham, who was to act as his personal representative 
and deliver the despatch—already quoted—in which for the 
first time Bentinck revealed and defended the ‘dream’. Graham 
saw Castlereagh at Chatillon on February 21st, had a long talk 
with him, and handed him a memorandum outlining Bentinck’s 
views on Sicily. But Graham’s account of what was said differs 
from Castlereagh’s. 
Graham began—he afterwards recorded—by explaining the 
state of Sicily as Bentinck saw it. Though the constitutional 
system could at present be kept going only by British influence, 
yet that system had for the first time led Sicily to contribute 
effectively to the Allied cause; in any case it was not meant ‘for 
their good alone, it is principally for our own’: through it Sicily 
would become a real ally not a burden. Next Graham came to 
what Bentinck privately hoped for: at most, that Sicily should 
become ‘the Queen of our Colonies’-—and here Graham reca- 
pitulated all the arguments in favour; at the very least, that the 
British garrison should remain in Sicily, and that the Neapolitan 
and Sicilian missions should be united, with the Minister at 
Naples and a good second-in-command at Palermo. In any case, 
said Graham, Bentinck 
‘will not desert the Sicilian cause in which he has embarked, and even 
should he be left there alone without any force, to encounter difficulties 

<<<PDFPAGE 149>>>
DICTATORSHIP 
AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
133 
which, he fears, he could not overcome, he will try his utmost to preserve 
a Constitution which he has reared amidst so many dangers, and while 
permitted to remain, he will not leave his post’. 
According to Graham, Castlereagh ‘treated me, I must say, 
as a friend, and I shall always be grateful for his good humour 
on this occasion....’ But when Castlereagh dealt with the 
possible consequences of the armistice he ‘spoke... like one 
who remembered that the Rod of the House of Commons was 
suspended over his head... .’ The ‘dream,’ he pointed out, was 
now quite impracticable: ‘our engagements with Murat were 
decisive’; the Bourbons would therefore have to be compensated 
elsewhere for the loss of Naples, and nothing they were likely 
to get, even if it was padded out with money, would tempt them 
to part with Sicily. But if on the other hand Murat should 
prove treacherous and the Bourbons be restored to Naples, 
then the ‘dream’ would become ‘feasible and worthy of consider- 
ation’. In any case, even if Murat stayed on in Naples Castle- 
reagh agreed entirely that the British garrison could and should 
be maintained in Sicily after the peace. Nobody would object 
except Murat, and he could be made to acquiesce. Altogether 
Castlereagh was ‘in the best possible disposition’ about Sicily; 
‘nothing could be more flattering’ than his remarks about 
Bentinck. He also seemed to think well of the proposed joint 
mission at Naples and Palermo; at all events, he said, Bentinck 
would keep the Sicilian mission.* 
Now we come to Castlereagh’s version. Just after the inter- 
view he wrote the following brief and non-committal letter to 
Bentinck: 
‘T shall not lose sight of your suggestions about Sicily, I agree with you 
that its safety would be more than precarious if left to its own defences 
alone. It is however a most difficult problem in all its parts.”* 
When a month later, the storm broke over the ‘dream’ 
incident, Castlereagh explained his former silence by saying that 
‘When Mr Graham mentioned Your Lordship’s impressions to me 
at Chatillon . 
. 
. and afterwards sent me a memorandum upon the subject, 
considering the whole, even in Your Lordship’s view, as necessarily at 
an end, in consequence of the connexion formed with Murat, I paid 

<<<PDFPAGE 150>>>
134 
BENTINCK AND, SICILY 
little attention to the point, and in the press of other business, did not 
till yesterday read Mr Graham’s paper.”* 
What really happened at Chatillon? Graham’s report is so 
full that it is hard to believe Castlereagh did not take in the 
‘dream’ or grasp the singularity of such a proposal from a 
Minister to a sovereign. A devil’s advocate might maintain that 
Castlereagh understood quite clearly what Graham had said; 
that the annexation of Sicily had been, not indeed the aim of 
British intervention, but in the air as a possible consequence, 
more or less remote; that now it was impracticable; but that 
Castlereagh kept it in mind as ‘worthy of consideration’ if 
events should take a new turn on the Continent. Castlereagh 
certainly took the “dream’, then and later, more calmly than did
