his fellow-Ministers at home. But there is another explanation, 
It is this. Graham—a romantic young man of 21, given to 
writing long rhetorical letters—may have bored Castlereagh 
with a verbose account of Bentinck’s views (Graham himself 
reported that he had ‘insisted at great length’ on one point). 
Castlereagh had a great many other matters on his mind; he 
thought the armistice had settled the fate of Naples; probably 
he did no more than half-listen to Graham, but now and then 
uttered some encouraging platitude which misled his interlocutor 
into thinking that he strongly agreed with what was being 
said. When we keep in mind Castlereagh’s whole European 
policy this seems much the likelier explanation. But some small 
doubt must persist.* 
This was not the end of the ‘dream’. Early in March Castel- 
cicala used the incident—which the Foreign Office still knew 
nothing about—to support a demand for Bentinck’s recall. He 
got a strong rebuff when it came out that he had acted without 
specific instructions from the Prince. Liverpool, however, was 
much annoyed. The proposed annexation of Sicily he thought 
inexpedient rather than immoral, but Bentinck’s act was most 
objectionable—as he might have pointed out, Drummond had 
been recalled in 1808 for a ‘private’ indiscretion on a different 
and less serious matter. Bentinck, he thought, was obviously 
finding that ‘his new constitution and the Sicilian court are never 
likely to work together’, and the ‘dream’ was ‘a device for 

<<<PDFPAGE 151>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
135 
getting out of the difficulties in which his Whig principles had 
involved both himself and us’.* 
The despatch Castlereagh wrote to Bentinck when he heard 
of this was, however, regretful in tone rather than reproachful; 
William A’Court, who was with him at the time, reported to 
Lady William Bentinck that ‘the Correspondence, which was 
considered as so important in England, is thought of no conse- 
quence here, and there does not appear to be the slightest idea 
of complying with Castelcicala’s request’. Bentinck thanked 
Castlereagh for his ‘kindness and consideration’, and that was 
the last of the business. But although Castlereagh never made 
the ‘dream’ one of his chief grievances against Bentinck another 
grain of official discontent had gone into an accumulating store.* 
In February, however, all this was yet to come. At Palermo 
Bentinck was casting about for means of securing the Sicilian 
protectorate if Castlereagh and the British Government, as he 
had reason to fear, should prove indifferent. The Prince, he 
thought, had better be induced to make an official request for 
a British garrison after the peace; but after the ‘dream’ incident 
the Ministers, and not Bentinck himself, must take the initiative. 
All he could do was to rub in Murat’s great military power. 
In spite of this neither the Prince nor the Ministers proved easy 
to manage. The Prince, though frightened at the prospect of 
having Murat for a neighbour after the war, was reluctant to 
saddle himself with a permanent British force until he was quite 
sure that there was no other way out. As for the Ministers, 
Bentinck told Belmonte, Castelnuovo and Settimo in confidence 
of the ‘dream’ and explained the present situation fully, with 
all its uncertainties and dangers. Here they were on the eve of 
great changes in Italy which threatened not to go the way they 
had hoped; if peace came swiftly England might decide to 
clear out and then where would they be? But Belmonte ‘went 
into a long harangue upon what he supposed to be the policy 
of England without attending to the real state of the question’, 
and Bentinck ‘could not fix his attention to the subject’. In his 
annoyance he begged Orleans to remind Belmonte of 
‘the necessity of their interfering with the British Government to keep 
a force in Sicily and not to let him deceive himself that England would

BENTINCK AND SICILY 
do this of her own accord or that she would not be too happy to get rid 
of Sicily and all its troubles. 
. 
.. Lord Castlereagh had said to Lamb when 
is all this interference to cease—It was needless to fancy how it should 
be—I was fully convinced of the policy of England maintaining her 
influence in Sicily, but how did the fact really stand and had England this 
interest upon the subject—No— 
Bentinck had to throw his weight into the scales after all: he 
reminded the Prince that the Constitution must be safeguarded 
as the ‘great engine of destruction’ against French despotism in 
Italy. At length, after much dickering between the Prince and the 
Ministers, the desired Note was handed to Bentinck on February 
23rd. He was agreeably surprised at its contents, for it gave as 
reasons for the need of continued British protection in peacetime 
not only the security of Sicily, but that of her independence and 
constitution as well.* 
But if the ‘engine of destruction’ was about as safe in its 
homeland as fore-thought could make it, the prospect of putting 
it to good use on the Continent was discouraging. Before the 
armistice with Murat, indeed right up to January 18th, when the 
arrival of Aberdeen’s despatch forced him to swallow the armis- 
tice, Bentinck had considered various moves which would not 
have suited either Austria or Murat in the least. On January 6th 
he had urged the King to issue a proclamation full of such 
constitutional sentiments as would rally the Neapolitans round 
the Bourbons—and perhaps not only the Neapolitans, for, said 
Bentinck, other Italians might join them and ‘I for one should be 
sorry to see Italy parcelled out among its ancient petty sover- 
eigns.. The King, however, would have no part of it, and 
Bentinck went off feeling that ‘there was only one way of treat- 
ing them all from top to bottom without any exception il 
Bastone’. Bentinck had also thought of leaving Murat and the 
Austrians to fight it out with the Viceroy Eugéne in Lombardy 
while he, on his own account, raised an insurrection against the 
French by invading first Corsica, then Tuscany and Liguria.* 
But the armistice sent all these plans up in smoke. Bentinck now 
found himself in complete agreement with the King on the 
question of Naples; in the course of their frequent and ‘lugubri- 
ous conversations’ they commiserated with one another on the 
folly of the Allies and especially Austria in backing Murat; and 

<<<PDFPAGE 153>>>
DICTATORSHIP AND THE ‘DREAM’ 
137 
Bentinck actually encouraged the King in his refusal to consider 
giving up his claim to Naples: true, this was one matter in which 
the King needed no encouragement." The King for his part 
declared that he put his trust in ‘God, England and Bentinck’; 
but even while he said it he nursed a nagging suspicion that 
England and Bentinck, at any rate, were at the bottom of a 
plot to strip him of his possessions.* 
But the Austro-Neapolitan treaty did not affect Naples only; 
to Bentinck’s mind, it tended ‘to confirm the slavery of Italy 
whose liberation had been my constant hope’. Murat, he 
thought, had hoodwinked the Austrians—‘the worst politicians 
in the universe’—by stealing their thunder and incidentally 
Bentinck’s too: now he would be able to present himself as 
the champion of Italian independence; the people would prob- 
ably prefer him to Austria, ‘and then we had no chance of 
dictating at all’. Bentinck regretted more than ever that the 
British Government had not chosen to intervene earlier in Italy: 
‘Tf the British protection and assistance had happened to be within 
their reach, that great floating force would certainly have ranged under 
their standard. The national energy could then have been roused, like 
Spain and Germany, in honour of national independence, and this great 
people instead of being the instrument of the ambition of one military 
tyrant or another, or as formerly the despicable slaves of a set of miserable 
petty princes, would become a powerful barrier both against Austria 
and France, and the peace and happiness of the world would receive a 
great additional security—but I fear the hour is gone by’.* 
Bentinck, preparing now for joint military operations on the 
Continent with Austria and Murat, could do no more than hope 
that Italy might yet contrive her own liberation. At one point 
he even thought of going to Bellegarde, the Austrian commander, 
and proposing that Austria should take as much of Northern 
Italy as she wanted—as far as the Adda or the Oglio—while 
Bentinck proclaimed the independence of the rest under a free 
constitution: then Britain and Austria would hold joint sway 
and Murat would be ‘reduced to nothing’. The thing to do, 
however, was (he decided) not to make plans, but to keep faith 
and ‘put ourselves in the way of Fortune’.* 
On February 28th Bentinck sailed from Palermo with his 

<<<PDFPAGE 154>>>
138 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
expeditionary force, to the tune of a proclamation by the Prince 
—addressed to the very troops who were supposed to fight side 
by side with Murat’s—in which he stated once again his claim 
to Naples. On March oth Bentinck landed at Leghorn and met 
the Neapolitan generals. On the 14th he issued a proclamation 
to the Italians at large. In it he offered them Britain’s help in 
throwing off the ‘iron yoke of Buonaparte’: the help through 
which Spain had already regained her freedom, while Sicily, 
happier still, had saved herself from the ‘universal deluge’: 
‘Through the beneficent disposition of her Sovereign, she passes from 
slavery to freedom, and hastens to resume her ancient splendour among 
the independent Nations. Holland will speedily have attained the same 
object: is then Italy to remain under the yoke? Shall Italians alone contend 
against Italians, in favour of tyrant, and for the thraldom of their country ? 
Italians, hesitate no longer;—be Italians, and let Italy in arms be convinced 
that the great Cause of the Country is in her hands.’ 
The ‘warriors of Italy’ he called upon, not indeed to join 
him, but to vindicate their own rights and to be free. “Only 
call, and we will hasten to your relief; and then Italy, by our 
united efforts, shall become what she was in her most prosperous 
periods, and what Spain now is.™* 
‘Be Italians!’ Voila le grand mot laché. But more was let loose 
besides words. 

<<<PDFPAGE 155>>>

ReViLl 
Mie ENDIOPaRHE PRO LEG TORATE 
Bentinck was away from Sicily for just over three months, until 
June 8th, 1814. Brief though it was, this period decided the 
fate of Italy: at the end of February the Viceroy Eugéne was 
still defending himself in Lombardy and the French still held 
Liguria and Piedmont; by the end of April the Allies had overrun 
not only Northern Italy but France itself. Bentinck’s acts in 
this short period were in some way the culmination of his 
Sicilian proconsulship; and the fall to which they brought him 
had decisive effects in Sicily.* 
Bentinck, we may say, shewed himself at this time strikingly 
irresponsible. Now his position in Sicily over the past two years 
had been, in the most literal sense, irresponsible: he had been 
allowed and even encouraged to go his own way with only 
the vaguest of instructions, while Ministers in England looked on 
and waited to see ‘what had been done next’. Lately he had 
become the virtual dictator of Sicily. Bentinck was neither the 
first nor the last proconsul to kick over the traces when, after 
having in effect ruled a satellite country, he was suddenly told 
to obey instructions which he disliked. 
Power went to Bentinck’s head; there can be little doubt of 
that. Besides, throughout his Sicilian mission he had never quite 
given up considering it as a prelude to greater things to come 
in Italy. Now the curtain was up, and time pressed. Italy’s 
independence—possibly her unification—was in the air. Elisa 
Bonaparte in Tuscany, Melzi in Lombardy, Caulaincourt and 
Napoleon himself, all talked of ‘cette corde 4 laquelle répondent 
tous les voeux’; so did Murat, for his own purposes; so did the 
Austrian generals, though to be sure their proclamations were 
addressed to the peoples of Italy, Bentinck’s to the people. Even 
Castlereagh, in a more general way, had a little earlier described 
the war, for Metternich’s benefit, as a “war of national inde- 
pendence’ in which ‘the people are now the only barrier’.* The 
difference between these pronouncements and Bentinck’s was 
that Bentinck meant what he said. So much did he desire Italian 
independence that he jeopardised his career in a reckless and 

<<<PDFPAGE 156>>>
I40 
BENTINCK AND,.SICILY 
haphazard attempt to bring it about, though his very disciples 
in the cause—Lamb and Graham—warned him from the start 
that he would be playing a lone hand in defiance of his govern- 
ment’s policy, which in Italy was anchored to Austria's.” Now 
Bentinck’s conduct in Sicily had not been reckless: in spite of 
certain velleities—as when, in 1812, he decided for a time to 
attempt a landing in Italy—he had not in the event over- 
stepped his instructions, wide as those were. But in 1814 
it was almost as if he had knowingly staked his position on a 
gamble against heavy odds. Such gambles, when by chance 
they succeed, often look to later historians like well-laid plans, 
and their success appears in retrospect to have been if not always 
desirable, at any rate inevitable. Bentinck’s gamble did not 
succeed. 
Both his new recklessness and his stubborn attachment to the 
Italian cause came out in his wrangle with Murat. Immediately 
after Bentinck’s landing at Leghorn the two men fell out over 
who was to occupy Tuscany. Murat wanted it to cover his line 
of communication to the North; Bentinck suspected that he 
had his eye on a good deal more. The wrangle went on for a 
whole month; as it grew Bentinck attempted more and more 
openly to throw Murat out of the coalition altogether.* Murat 
was from the start a prey to contradictory influences; there is 
little point in asking whether Bentinck had some excuse for his 
initial hostility to ‘le plus perfide des Gaules’-—a man more like 
Maria Carolina than anyone he had ever seen;* whether, too, 
this behaviour did or did not encourage Murat in his duplicity 
towards the Allies. Nor is there much point in saying that the 
alliance with Murat had already become more of an embarrass- 
ment than a help, even to Austria, by the time Bentinck came 
along; nor that the Austrian generals shared Bentinck’s distrust 
of Murat if not his pugnacity; nor even that Castlereagh himself 
shortly came to describe Murat as a ‘worthless dog’ who had 
been ‘a mere calculator’, and later found Bentinck’s stiff-necked 
attitude at this time highly convenient, since it had left a way 
open for a reversal of British policy towards Naples.* The main 
points are two: first, Bentinck kept up his hostility towards 
Murat in spite of Castlereagh’s clear and peremptory orders to 
the contrary; then he did so not only because he genuinely 

<<<PDFPAGE 157>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
I4I 
despised and distrusted Murat, but because he saw in him the 
one man who might, if unchallenged, unify Italy for his own 
evil purposes. As Graham had said, ‘military tyrants are not 
hopeful subjects for conversion into patriot Kings’. But Murat 
was on the spot; he was powerful; he was attractive; he was a 
rival all the more dangerous because, it seemed, he had a good 
chance of succeeding.* 
Bentinck himself did not trouble to conceal his hopes. His 
Italian Levy had landed at Leghorn with a banner bearing the 
device ‘Italian union—national independence’; although there 
is only tenuous evidence that Bentinck actively helped the 
Carbonari and other patriotic societies,* he certainly said and 
did enough to deserve Mier’s description of him as a ‘préneur 
enragé de constitutions’, and Ferdinand’s complaint that he was 
behaving ‘like Dr Sangrado, who wished to bleed everybody 
whether they needed it or not’.* Constitution and independence 
was Bentinck’s watchword; the making of a united Italy seems 
to have been a rather hazier ideal, which might come into being 
through some process of self-determination. This is shewn by 
the most notorious episode of Bentinck’s Italian career, his 
single-handed revival of the Genoese Republic. Bentinck’s short 
campaign up the Ligurian coast ended when he took Genoa on 
April 18th, two days after fighting had stopped in Lombardy. 
A week later he issued a proclamation restoring the Genoese 
Constitution as it had stood before France swallowed up the 
Republic, but modifying it in the spirit of the old sixteenth- 
century constitution. It was Sicily all over again, with this vital 
difference—nobody 
had questioned 
Sicily’s independence, 
whether real or formal; but Bentinck knew quite well, from 
specific instructions of Castlereagh’s, that he was not to restore 
any of the old Italian sovereigns apart from Tuscany and 
Piedmont; in fact he had reason to suspect that the Allies would 
merge Genoa with Piedmont. That, of course, was exactly what 
the Allies did in the end, to the cost of the supposed ‘vertu 
anglaise’ which—as Stendhal reported three years later—this 
‘fameux manquement de foi’ was to brand in Italy as ‘pure 
tartuferie’. From new documents it appears that by this time 
Bentinck was far gone in recklessness, for he argued that at 
Genoa ‘the most popular would be to make an arrangement 

<<<PDFPAGE 158>>>
142 
BENTINCK AND>SICILY 
as definitive as possible which it would be the more difficult 
for the Allies to upset’; while at the same time he blithely found 
a loophole in the thought that ‘it was always in the hands of 
the Allies to make whatever arrangement they pleased.” 
Bentinck’s conduct, indeed, in these first confused weeks of 
peace came to look more and more like a desperate gamble. He 
encouraged the Milanese in their hopes of independence—and 
therefore in their opposition to Austria, the Power which 
Castlereagh had time and again told him to cooperate with in 
Italy. He sent troops to Corsica on what proved to be an abortive 
repetition of Sir Gilbert Elliot’s adventure there. He wished 
success to certain Neapolitan generals when they secretly ap- 
proached him and told him they hoped to force a constitution 
on Murat.* But he was not long to be allowed a free hand in Italy. 
Already at the beginning of April Castlereagh had told him 
that, while he had ‘very properly’ attempted in the past to 
free Italy by raising an insurrection—'the only system which, 
previous to the revival of the Continent, could afford a 
prospect of shaking the power of France’-—he must not, now 
that ‘a different and better order of things’ had come about, 
give the impression of being ‘actuated by “une arriére-pensée” 
inconstistent with the arrangements understood between the 
great Powers of Europe. . . . It is not insurrection we now want 
in Italy or elsewhere—we want disciplined force, under Sove- 
reigns we can trust’.” Now, a month later, the storm broke. “We 
shall have something to unravel in Corsica, and also at Genoa’, 
wrote Castlereagh to Liverpool: Bentinck’s proclamation at 
Genoa was ‘very inconsiderate’, and his dealings with the 
Neapolitan generals shewed 
‘how intolerably prone he is to Whig revolutions everywhere. I am 
confident his mismanagement at least doubled all the natural dangers of 
Murat’s rascality; and my persuasion is, if the war had gone on much 
longer in France, we should have had a most disastrous complication in 
Italy. 
... He seems bent upon throwing all Italy loose. This might be 
well as against France, but against Austria and the King of Sardinia, with 
all the new Constitutions which now menace the world with fresh 
convulsions, it is most absurd.”* 
The ‘dream’ re-emerged from brief oblivion, and added its 
weight to that of all the other official grievances against Ben- 

<<<PDFPAGE 159>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
143 
tinck. At the same time Castlereagh recollected two convenient 
facts: in September 1813 Bentinck had asked not only for home 
leave but also for his recall from the Sicilian mission. All 
Bentinck now wanted—so he told Castlereagh through his 
aide-de-camp—was a few months’ leave, and he was in fact 
anxious to stay on in Sicily; but Castlereagh replied merely that 
‘when I have the pleasure of seeing you in England, we can 
converse on this subject’. Meanwhile William A’Court would 
take over the Palermo mission. So, although Bentinck had not 
yet been officially recalled, he was already in disgrace. When he 
sailed from Genoa to Palermo at the beginning of June he knew 
that he would shortly be going home on leave; but he had good 
reason besides to suspect that this would be his last visit to Sicily.* 
He knew, too, that the appointment of A’Court marked a 
change of measures as well as of men. Castelcicala was probably 
exaggerating when he told the King that A’Court was his candi- 
date for the Sicilian mission; but he was correct in describing 
A’Court as ‘well intentioned’ and ‘hostile to popular assemblies 
in foreign countries’. When it came to Sicily the new Minister 
—who had been Secretary at Naples between the two French in- 
vasions—often sounded more Neapolitan than the Neapolitans. 
As early as February A’Court, already chosen to be Bentinck’s 
locum tenens, had recorded that to his mind the Sicilian consti- 
tution had virtually come to an end when Bentinck had sent 
Parliament packing in October 1813; in any case it was quite 
unsuited to the Sicilians ‘in their present state of ignorance and 
profligacy’. The end of the war, he said, would mean the end of 
that British preponderance which alone could make it work. 
A’Court’s secret instructions, drawn up on March oth, did not 
go quite so far: whatever the defects of the Constitution, ‘it 
ought to be the policy of the British Government, who were the 
original promoters of it, to endeavour to support it, at least in 
its essential principles’. The excessive dependence of the Crown 
on Parliament was the ‘original error’ which the British Minister 
should use his influence to correct. 
‘The principle of liberty is admitted by the existence of the Constitu- 
tion itself and until the Sicilian Patriots shall have been trained to more 
temperate notions of liberty and popular privileges the excesses of faction 
are more to be dreaded than those of authority on the part of the Crown.” 

<<<PDFPAGE 160>>>
I44 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
With most of this Bentinck could agree; but he was aware of 
one crucial fact which the instructions did not convey: as he had 
long foretold, the British Government had lost interest in their 
protectorate over Sicily. They had acquired it almost by acci- 
dent during the war; now they prepared to cast it off with, it 
seems, the same imperial carelessness. If they weighed the pros 
and cons of the matter at all they looked not at the island itself 
but at the whole new balance of power in Europe. Sicily, 
Liverpool had said apropos of the ‘dream’, would anyhow be 
very difficult to manage through the new Constitution; but 
what mattered was that 
‘the continental states are more jealous of our obtaining power in the 
Mediterranean than in any other quarter. Circumstances have induced 
them to make no difficulty about our retaining Malta, but the idea of 
our possessing Sicily by an act, which in its most favourable light must 
be considered as a douce violence, could not fail greatly to revolt them.”* 
Merely to carry on the protectorate, without questioning 
Sicily’s independence, would no doubt have been more douce a 
violence than the original ‘dream’. But with the war over it was 
too embarrassing to be worth while: that at least seems to have 
been the reasoning which the Government followed even if they 
did not put it into words. Just how Britain would disentangle 
herself from her Sicilian commitment was not yet clear. But 
Bentinck could see that his dictatorship had been a desperate 
and, as it now turned out, a pointless remedy: in trying to 
salvage the ‘system’ he had acted independently from rather 
than in opposition to his Government, for they had given him 
neither check nor encouragement. Even now they did not so 
much reject what he had done as ignore it; in practice their new 
policy meant that they wished to let the whole matter drop. 
Even before he knew of his fall from official favour, Bentinck 
had written to Castelnuovo of his disgust at what he had found 
in Italy, and his one remaining hope that he might yet ‘rendre 
quelque petit service’ to Sicily—‘et je prie le bon Dieu avec tout 
mon cceur de la rendre heureuse’. On 8 June he landed at 
Palermo. By all accounts the heyday of his recklessness was over; 
he came back chastened and dejected. None the less he now 
embarked on a last attempt to save what could be saved of the 
constitutional system.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 161>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
I45 
The situation he found was unpromising but not hopeless. 
Even if Bentinck had returned with his powers intact he would 
have faced a difficult task. For the almost irreparable break-up 
of the Patriotic movement had only been suspended during his 
dictatorship; now, with a new Parliament and a resumption of 
constitutional government in the offing, the dissensions of 1813 
reappeared, still unhealed. The general election, held in May, 
had produced—thanks to Bonanno’s energetic and unscrupulous 
‘management’—a House of Commons irreproachably consti- 
tutionalist.* But it also raised new difficulties: for the incorrup- 
tible Castelnuovo, objecting to Bonanno’s high-handed methods, 
signified his disagreement in the usual way, by withdrawing to 
his tent and refusing to attend Council meetings. More im- 
portant, the conservatives or King’s Friends still swayed the 
Peers: a complete deadlock between the two Houses was to 
be expected.* 
. 
In such circumstances Bentinck had formerly threatened to 
abolish or at any rate coerce the Upper House; that was now 
out of the question. But the very change in the political situation 
of Sicily which made impossible such bludgeoning methods 
also offered the chance of a compromise. For the end of the war 
had raised new anxieties in the minds of some of the leading 
King’s Friends: if the King were to regain Naples—they now 
saw—not only those parts of the new Constitution which they 
disliked, but the privileges of their order and the independence 
of their country, which they really cared about, would be in 
danger. They, as the King’s intimates, may well have known 
that his disposition was anything but reassuring. True, the news 
of Louis XVIII’s Charte had come as a shock to his absolutist 
convictions. But at the end of May he was still writing to 
Castelcicala that if, as he hoped, he managed to recover Naples 
‘in Paris’ —that is, by diplomatic action—he might then ‘recover’ 
Sicily from Naples.* At all events some of these conservative 
nobles—San Marco, Trabia, Campofranco—were fearful enough 
to approach Castelnuovo and Settimo secretly in April and 
propose a coalition in defence of the constitutional principle and 
of Sicilian independence. These were essential points on which 
all could agree; apparently the two groups of men did not even 
discuss their old differences over the abolition of feudalism, 

<<<PDFPAGE 162>>>
146 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
though if they were to come together that would mean that 
the Patriotic movement would from then on relapse into a 
purely aristocratic opposition to royal power. In the present 
emergency, it seems, Castelnuovo was ready to sacrifice such 
hopes as he still had of social reform for the sake of achieving 
political unity and avoiding ‘faction’. Men not measures stood 
in the way of the proposed coalition: the conservatives wished 
to throw out the Belmontists, whom they accused of jobbery; 
Castelnuovo, though willing enough to be rid of the Belmontist 
ministers—Bonanno and Carini—still refused to act without 
Belmonte himself; and the Belmontists, of course, had no wish 
to sacrifice themselves. There the matter stood when Bentinck 
returned to Palermo.* 
He found his friends in typically contrasted moods—Castel- 
nuovo so despondent that, he said, only loyalty to Bentinck kept 
him from giving up politics altogether;* Belmonte so absurdly 
confident of British protection that he saw no danger at all. 
Bentinck told all the constitutionalist leaders plainly that he 
could no longer direct the government or prevent the King 
from resuming if he wished; all Britain would undertake was 
to protect her friends from persecution. The Ministers must 
now ‘march by themselves’. Though ‘it was no longer my 
concern’, he agreed with Castelnuovo that the proposed coalition 
was necessary if not palatable; the King’s Friends, he thought, 
‘all wished the Constitution—they wished more for place—but 
place with a Constitution they would prefer to place without 
it’. The arrangement would probably not last longer than three 
months, but that would suffice to get through the parliamentary 
session and secure the Constitution once and for all. Bentinck 
therefore entered into negotiations with San Marco and his 
friends. They made difficulties; they complained of great irregu- 
larities in the recent general election and demanded a new one. 
Bentinck admitted the irregularities, but, he said, they were to 
be expected given the present state of Sicily. The government 
must go on and make it its business in future to “discourage that 
system of prepotenza by which the country had so long been 
oppressed—from the Sovereign downwards by every agent of 
every Baron and by all the agents of every Government’. Taking 
a longer view, he told them that though the Constitution was 

<<<PDFPAGE 163>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
147 
certainly in very bad straits, yet he did not altogether despair of 
its ultimate success; what Sicily needed above all was such a 
programme of practical reform as he had tried to launch: 
‘Institutions made men good or bad. Administration of Justice had 
the most influence as well upon national character as upon the tranquillity 
and good order of society—It was this which alone could rectify the 
evils of which all complained—Before we could say that the Constitu- 
tion would not succeed we must see the effect of such new institutions 
and of time—If in the new Parliament—a good judicial system—roads 
for commerce and a modification of landed possession so as to disperse 
landed property more generally—could be settled—more would be done 
for Sicily in one session than by all its Kings together for the last two or 
three centuries. I had considered the question of the state of Sicily in all 
its bearings. If the King should return to Naples, he might if England 
chose it destroy the Constitution... but . 
. 
. 
Ireally thought that as it was 
difficult for it to go on, so it wasinfinitely more difficult for it to go back.’ 
It was not, however, in the conservative camp that the real 
difficulties arose, but among Bentinck’s own friends. Belmonte 
would not budge: he still objected to a coalition and fancied that 
the government could secure a majority in the Peers without 
one. ‘Nobody but himself thought so’, Bentinck told him; 
exasperated at this wilful blindness he complained bitterly to 
Villafranca that 
‘it was all our own fault—we had to accuse nobody but our own 
littleness—folly—and mismanagement—I knew at first that the Ministry 
could not go on—Belmonte and Villarmosa I knew would not agree... 
I was heartily disgusted and should be glad to go away. I saw no chance 
of success but by mixing the Ministry.”* 
The Belmontists’ refrain was that the King—by common 
consent the real menace to the Constitution—‘must be made 
either nul or friendly’. Bentinck’s and Castelnuovo’s solution 
was to make him ‘nul’ by detaching his supporters from him. 
This the Belmontists disliked, and they accordingly began to 
think of making the King ‘friendly’ instead. 
There were signs that such an attempt might succeed better 
than past experience suggested. For at that very moment the 
King had changed his tune. Suddenly and unexpectedly—Ben- 
tinck reported—he had become 

<<<PDFPAGE 164>>>
148 
BENTINCK AND* SICILY 
‘as anxious for the establishment of the Sicilian Constitution as any 
of its warmest patriots—not from any diminished affection for principles 
of the purest despotism, or from diminished hatred for the system or 
authors of Sicilian liberty; but he has been convinced that it is only by 
the adoption of more liberal principles that he can regain the throne of 
Naples: whether from the favourable disposition of the Allied Sovereigns 
at the approaching Congress, or from the good will of his people.” 
The King had various reasons for this change of front. He 
felt the need to compete with Murat, who had lately promised a 
Constitution. Then, should he later recover Naples, the Sicilians 
might raise fewer obstacles to reunion if they could look forward 
toa joint constitution for both kingdoms. Probably most im- 
portant, on June 20th the King heard from Orleans—who had 
gone to Paris—that the Allied Sovereigns, and especially 
Alexander, disapproved of his past conduct and thought his 
return to Naples wholly dependent on the will of the people; 
they therefore advised him to emulate Louis XVIII’s liberal 
policy. “These gentlemen are very ready to give me advice,’ the 
King grumbled, ‘but not at all disposed to follow it in their own 
case.’ Still he was, it seems, convinced for a time that he had 
better put up a front of unimpeachable constitutionalism in the 
eyes of Europe; and the best way to do so was to begin at home 
in Sicily.* 
This new attitude of the King’s may help to explain the 
peculiar way in which the Sicilian political crisis was resolved 
on June 23rd. On that date the constitutionalist leaders met at 
Bentinck’s house to decide finally whether they should form a 
coalition with the King’s Friends. There was a long and heated 
discussion which led nowhere. Then Belmonte, to everyone’s 
astonishment, proposed that instead of uniting with the King’s 
Friends they should ‘amalgamate with the King’: that is, they 
should ask him either to support the Constitution or to resume 
the government, and they should offer for the sake of unity to 
alter the make-up of the Government according to his wishes. 
Nobody knew what to say to this proposal; somehow it was 
adopted.* 
It is still hard to tell what made Belmonte and the Council 
act as they did. Belmonte may well have taken the King’s new 
professions of constitutionalism at their face value. Perhaps, too, 

<<<PDFPAGE 165>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
I49 
he wished at all costs to forestall the coalition—which would 
have left his party no more than a subordinate place—and 
fancied that he could recover, with the King’s favour, the po- 
sition of Prime Minister which Bentinck had taken away from 
him in 1813. Yet there is also evidence that nobody thought the 
King would have the energy or the desire to resume the cares 
of government: so the approach to the King may originally 
have been a gesture: if approached he would have had to come 
out into the open and, perhaps, call off the Peers’ opposition. It 
may also be that neither Belmonte not the others acted from 
motives capable of logical explanation, but that, tired of argu- 
ment and unable to break the deadlock, they simply took the 
first way out that offered. As for Bentinck, he would certainly 
have preferred a coalition, and Belmonte’s move seems to have 
irritated him even more than usual. But by now he could no 
longer impose his own solution: indeed, he most likely felt like 
washing his hands of the whole business.” 
The Prince, after he had got over his initial surprise, drew up 
a memorandum telling his father of the Council’s proposal. 
But he did not merely give him the alternative of supporting or 
resuming the government; instead he seized the opportunity 
to urge the King to resume: in this way the King would prove 
‘your adherence to those liberal ideas which, as you well know, 
are the spring that can move the Allies in your favour... your 
present conduct will be the best guarantee of what you mean 
to do at Naples’. Bentinck countenanced the Prince’s memo- 
randum, though he refused to sign it himself, since—he said— 
judging from past experience he could scarcely advise the King 
to resume. He was right. The King surprised nearly everybody 
by hastening to take his son’s advice. Before resuming, however, 
he asked Bentinck to release him from the promise he had made 
in March 1813 not to do so without British consent. Bentinck 
acquiesced; but since Circello, who acted as go-between, kept 
proclaiming that his master was now ‘furieux constitutionelliste’, 
Bentinck seized his opportunity too; he dissolved the engage- 
ment, he stated, because the King had declared that he meant 
‘to cherish the Constitution’ and to adopt the liberal conduct 
‘so strongly recommended by the advice and enforced by the 
example of the highest and most august authorities’. By this 

<<<PDFPAGE 166>>>
Iso 
BENTINCK AND, SICILY 
last act Bentinck thought to provide his Government with an 
excuse for intervening in defence of the Constitution if they 
should ever be minded to do so.” 
On 
July 4th the events of March 1813 recurred with an ironical 
difference. The King drove through Palermo amid cheering 
crowds; next day he resumed the government. At the same 
time, and once again unexpectedly, he accepted the Ministers’ 
resignations. Then—as if to shew that Bentinck’s dictatorship 
had been no more than an unconstitutional interregnum—he 
brought back to power all the men whose dismissal Bentinck 
had previously forced on the Prince: that is, Ferreri and the other 
puppet Ministers who had gone out in October 1813; and the 
Court officialh—one of them Cassaro—whom Bentinck had 
deprived of their posts in April 1813. The festivities attending 
the King’s return went on for three nights. “The people shewed 
very great satisfaction’, and already the outgoing constitution- 
alists were being hissed in the streets.* 
Bentinck, in the official despatch he wrote to Castlereagh 
just after the King’s return—his last despatch from Sicily—spoke 
of his seemingly unreserved pleasure at ‘this happy termination 
of the long and embarrassing relation which has existed for so 
many years between the two governments’. In a private letter 
of the same date he gave a curious account of his feelings: 
‘The return of the King to power, under all the circumstances, I 
consider to be one of the best pieces of good luck that ever happened 
to me; and offers a pont d’or, over which I shall be most happy to make 
my retreat from Sicilian affairs. My private feelings have long made me 
desirous of leaving a situation which has materially interfered with my 
military views.—I however determined to sacrifice every view of my 
own (and for this reason did ask you to allow me to remain in Sicily) 
as long as I thought that my presence could contribute to the happiness 
of the Sicilians—I feel no longer any obligation on this score—the Sicilian 
Government has again resumed its independence; and agreeing, as all 
seem to do, in the desire to establish the Constitution and a fixed order 
of things. I should hope it will be able to go on by itself—At any rate 
our interference is no longer a matter of necessity.’ 
This is at first sight puzzling, since in fact Bentinck saw 
clearly enough—as we know from his journal—that ‘no depend- 
ence [was] to be placed’ in the King’s professions of constitu- 

<<<PDFPAGE 167>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
Isl 
tionalism.* The likeliest explanation is that in writing to Castle- 
reagh, whom he knew to be unsympathetic, he wished to put 
as good a face as possible on his personal disappointment. But 
it may be that Bentinck was still trying not to admit his own 
misgivings, even to himself and even so late in the day as this. 
If so he was soon undeceived. Of the drastic changes made in 
the Government he complained to the King and Circello that 
they betrayed ‘too much aigreur and prevention .. 
. towards 
the actual system’ and belied the King’s promise to ‘reunite all 
parties’; but he met with the bland rejoinder that the King was 
‘the most constitutional of all’: the new Ministry was in fact 
the old and, Circello implied, the only constitutional Ministry. 
He was also depressed to find in A’Court, who had landed at 
Palermo at the end of June, a self-confessed Voltairean cynic, 
hostile to all popular assemblies, confident of Castlereagh’s 
sympathy, and armed ‘with the worst opinion (not very much 
undeserved) 
of the Sicilians and with a deep conviction 
amounting almost to an irritable determination that the Consti- 
tution cannot and shall not succeed’.* Bentinck could do no 
more. According to one report, he did once contemplate 
taking some drastic step against the King, but thought better 
of it; he got A’Court to write a rather futile statement half- 
promising to defend the ‘system’ in Bentinck’s absence; shortly 
before his departure he was seen wandering in dejection through 
the countryside near Palermo. On July 16th Bentinck sailed 
from Palermo for the last time.” 
Bentinck was allowed to keep the military command in Italy 
until April 1815, when yet another ill-timed display of consti- 
tutionalism—this time in Piedmont—brought about his sum- 
mary recall. But Liverpool already thought him ‘mad’, and 
Castlereagh was at one with Metternich in wishing to keep him 
away from the Mediterranean; he had already fallen into that 
official disfavour which kept him out of the government service 
for the next thirteen years.* Looking back on his Italian and 
Sicilian career, Bentinck was unrepentant: 
‘Like Cassandra’ (he wrote to Hamilton in January 1815) ‘I preached 
in vain. I always thought that Italy alone could have decided the war 
against Bonaparte. I was very much gratified in hearing the other day 
that Bonaparte himself had said... that the British Government had 

<<<PDFPAGE 168>>>
I§2 
BENTINCK AND, SICILY 
never seemed to know its own importance in Italy. He added that we 
were held in the greatest veneration. True it is that there is no part of 
the world where the British name is equally respected. It is love and 
admiration without alloy. They look upon us as the natural friends of 
independence... who from policy and feeling cannot but be always 
inclined to protect them from internal and external oppression. You 
can have no idea how rapidly the spirit of discontent and union is spreading 
throughout Italy. Murat cannot now in my opinion be driven out.... 
Every Italian would join him ...I confess I should be glad to see the 
attempt made as far as regards Italy, because I think its independence 
would be established and besides the interest I take in the Italian people, 
I am convinced that there can be no other effectual barrier against either 
France or Austria...I assure you it grieves me to the heart to see our 
reputation and interests so unnecessarily thrown away....It seems as 
if we were anxious and in a hurry, as if it was entirely our own act to 
sacrifice and disregard all national feeling and supposed national rights. . . . 
Pardon this political digression. . . .”* 
As for Sicily, ‘if the whole Sicilian business were published 
from one end to the other,’ he wrote, ‘I should be rather glad 
than sorry ...’. He could not rid himself of his concern with 
Sicily, and he still corresponded with the few Sicilians who had 
remained his friends. He was furious, he wrote to Castelnuovo, 
when he thought of the golden chances Sicily had missed ‘faute 
de courage, de vertu, et de patriotisme. .. 
. Je vous jure que je 
suis dégotité de votre pays. S’il n’y avait pas des hommes comme 
vous et votre digne ami Ruggiero [Settimo], et quelques autres 
en fort petit nombre, je douterais presque si la Sicile pourrait 
produire quelque chose de bon. Espérons que ceux-ci serviront 
d’exemple 4 une meilleure postérité.’* 
In truth both the protectorate and the Patriotic movement 
had run their course, though outwardly their collapse dragged 
on until December 1816. Shortly after Bentinck’s departure, 
the King dissolved the Constitutionalists Commons which 
Bonanno’s ‘management’ had got together; by another general 
election he acquired a new Parliament, virtually a replica of 
that of 1813, both in its membership and in the irresponsible 
conduct it kept up throughout the winter and spring of 1814-15. 
In effect the King gave the Sicilians rope enough to hang them- 
selves. He could not afford a drastic change while his return to 
Naples remained uncertain; and he was shrewd enough to know 

<<<PDFPAGE 169>>>
’ THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
153 
—if not wise enough to adapt himself to the fact—that, however 
suicidal the behaviour of Parliament, it would not freely consent 
to the loss of Sicily’s constitution and independence.* Meanwhile 
A’Court carried out, loyally enough, his appointed task of 
trying to have the Constitution revised along the lines of Louis 
XVIII’s Charte, and of protecting Britain’s former friends from 
the King’s vengeance. The attempt was fruitless; where even 
the best-disposed man would probably have failed, limited as 
the British Minister now was to advice and remonstrance, 
A’Court found his initial contempt for the Sicilians—‘this 
infamous race’ of whom there was ‘nothing too bad to be 
said’—speedily confirmed.* 
Sicily’s fate was in fact decided not at Palermo, but at the 
Congress of Vienna, and then—when Napoleon came back and 
Murat joined him—at Waterloo and Tolentino. In May 1815 
Ferdinand dissolved Parliament for the last time and sailed to 
Naples. There ensued the piecemeal destruction of Sicily’s 
constitution and independence, until in December 1816 Ferdi- 
nand proclaimed the union of the two kingdoms and, in the 
words of a contemporary historian, made of Sicily a ‘posthu- 
mous conquest of Napoleon’:* for almost at one stroke he swept 
away every one of her native institutions—those handed down 
from Norman and Aragonese days together with those set up 
in 1812—and put in their place the centralised French system 
which Joseph Bonaparte had already introduced at Naples in 1806. 
This counter-revolution did not spring from the King’s 
personal wishes alone: he had promised Austria not to allow in 
his dominions institutions at variance with those of Austria’s 
Italian territories.* This engagement, and its consequences, were 
quite in line with Castlereagh’s policy of giving Austria a free 
hand in Italy; but they were scarcely in line with Britain’s 
Sicilian policy over the last few years. Hence the last act had to 
wait on complicated negotiations among the British, Austrian, 
and Neapolitan Governments, the result of which was that—for 
the benefit of the British Parliament—there was inserted in the 
decrees of 1816 a reference to a Sicilian Parliament which had 
in fact ceased to exist. By this means—so A’Court maintained— 
the whole course of British intervention in Sicily was made to 
‘offer a plain, honourable, and consistent tale’.* 

<<<PDFPAGE 170>>>
154 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
The Sicilian adventure was over. Already in 1815 the British 
garrison had been withdrawn and A’Court had left Palermo 
for Naples. 
Belmonte had died in Paris in 1814; he had gone there, it 
seems, in the hope of winning Castlereagh’s support for the 
Constitution. He was mourned by the two men—Bentinck and 
Castelnuovo—whom in his life he had most often exasperated. 
As for Castelnuovo, he tried for a while longer to salvage 
something of the ‘system’ by cooperating with the King. But 
after May 1815 he withdrew into private life, steadily refusing 
to pay taxes to an unconstitutional government except under 
duress, and bequeathing, at his death in 1822, £25,000 to anyone 
who should induce the King to restore the Constitution.” 
Ruggiero Settimo, indeed, lived to see it briefly restored in 1848, 
and Sicily herself united with the rest of Italy in 1860. 
But if Britain’s Sicilian adventure was over, could it be quite 
undone? Castlereagh apparently thought it could. In June 1821, 
after Laibach, Bentinck presented a motion in the House of 
Commons calling on the Government to intervene in defence 
of Sicilian liberties. The point he—and later Sir James Mac- 
kintosh—made was that by her intervention in 1811-14 Britain 
had pledged her “good faith and national honour’ in support of 
those liberties: she ought at the very least to see that the Sicilians 
kept the liberties they had enjoyed before that intervention, 
instead of leaving them infinitely worse off than she had found 
them. Castlereagh’s reply was that in any case it was a mere 
‘oratorical flourish’ to talk of a Sicilian Constitution, old or 
new; that of 1812 was one of the most unsuitable ever devised, 
and he ridiculed its makers for having tried to ape British 
institutions—why, they had even, he believed, sent for the 
measurements of the table he was leaning upon. As for the 
union with Naples, he “felt some lurking impression’ that Sicily, 
far from feeling aggrieved, ‘would be happier in consequence... 
that union was calculated to raise her to whatever importance 
Naples might be supposed to possess’. The heart of his argument 
concerned ‘the nature of the connexion of England and Sicily’: 
“Although Government had always felt a strong esteem and affection 
for that country, yet it was not merely on that account, or to secure the 
happiness of the people, that British troops were stationed there. It was, 

<<<PDFPAGE 171>>>
THE END OF THE PROTECTORATE 
JESS 
in fact, a military occupation . . 
. with the exception of having guaranteed 
to the Sicilian people, not to form a constitution, but to protect that 
part of the dominions of the King of the Two Sicilies, this government 
had entered into no arrangements of an express nature with them. ... 
It was true that while the British troops were in the country it was found 
necessary that a strong interposition should take place on the part of the 
British power, for the purpose of impressing on the mind of the Sicilian 
Government the propriety of supporting a constitution. If this had not 
been done, the government could not have gone on, and the place would 
not have been fit for a military station. The consequence of this inter- 
position was, that the noble lord [Bentinck] was “mixed up” in a great 
variety of remonstrances [a laugh]... but he certainly never did feel 
that in resorting to those remonstrances, the noble lord was doing any- 
thing more than what was necessary for his military occupation of the 
island. He never supposed that the noble lord was entering into any 
arrangements with respect to the Sicilian constitution.’ 
Any new interference by the British Government would 
therefore be ‘unjustifiable and impracticable’. The House sup- 
ported him by 69 votes to 35.* 
Castlereagh was of course right in one sense—perhaps the 
only sense that Governments can safely understand or attend to, 
though we may wonder. Given his broad European policy 
from 1814 onward, he could not possibly have gone on main- 
taining a protectorate or shoring up a constitution that nowhere 
fitted in with that policy. To the greater end of European 
balance and peace he had to sacrifice not only Sicily’s half- 
formed aspirations, which doubtless he cared little about, but 
some small part also of Britain’s name. For if morality enters at 
all into the history of international affairs—and keep it out 
though we may at the door it flies in at the skylight—a great 
Power is surely at once unwise and wrong if it fails to ‘connect’: 
if, that is, it assumes like Castlereagh that it can interfere in the 
affairs of a small Power, alter by its weight the course of events, 
and then withdraw and discard responsibility as though nothing 
had happened. Something does happen, and something did 
happen in Sicily. No doubt the reforming movement was weak 
and weakly rooted; but it was there, and the British intervention, 
by carrying it to deceptive heights which it could probably not 
have measured by its own strength alone, made sure that the 
ensuing fall would seem all the deeper. True, ifs and ans are no 

<<<PDFPAGE 172>>>
156 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
part of history; we cannot say that if something had not gone 
wrong something else would have gone right. But if causes 
cannot here be easily disentangled—or the British intervention 
set apart from the Sicilian movement when in fact both acted 
on one another—no more can we disentangle their remoter 
effects and say that this cause mattered while that was incidental. 
The crisis of these years was an historical event; the conflicts 
that shaped it, and whose course it in turn shaped, perpetuated 
themselves, partly in the upheavals of 1848 and 1860, but still 
more in a divided and half-petrified society, and in that moral 
divorce between Sicily and the continental Power that rules it 
which has affected so much of the island’s history, even down 
to the present day. No one who knows how much there is in 
that history of bitterness and frustration can feel quite satisfied.

APPENDIX*A 
WILLIAM DRUMMONDS MISSION TOSICI™N 
1806-1808 
While the peace negotiations of 1806 were still going on, Fox 
had reminded Holland of ‘the great importance of Sicily, which 
you, young one, very much underrate’.* After the failure of 
the negotiations, the Grenville Ministry had to decide on a 
long-term Sicilian policy. Neither Sicily nor its King could be 
abandoned to French aggression; but something had to be done 
to counteract the ‘unfortunate’ and ‘disgusting’ behaviour of 
the Court, to which the outgoing Minister, Hugh Elliot, attri- 
buted the loss of Naples.* Besides, Grenville himself considered 
that England must continue to protect Sicily and Sardinia after 
a peace, ‘and thus make of all these islands in the Mediterranean 
an important chain of stations highly useful to us both in war 
and in commerce’.* 
The new Minister, William Drummond, was accordingly 
sent out with instructions to conclude a treaty giving Britain, 
among other things, the right to garrison Messina in peacetime, 
and the commercial status of most-favoured nation. He was to 
secure Sicily’s fullest cooperation in the war: therefore, Howick 
wrote, ‘whenever you may see the common safety endangered 
by the operation of causes likely to defeat this necessary end, 
you will not fail to interfere with the whole weight of your 
authority and influence. ... Observing upon all occasions the 
respect which is due to an ancient and independent government, 
you will endeavour... to prevent any waste of the resources 
which Sicily can furnish for its own defence; whether it shall 
arise from improvident laws and privileges, or from the cor- 
ruption of the public authorities. 
... The large sacrifices which 
His Majesty has made and continues to make for the support of 
the King of the Two Sicilies gives you an undisputed right to 
interpose with your advice, even if that right were not to be 
found in the consideration which you owe to the interests of 
His Sicilian Majesty Himself.’ The resources of ‘so fertile an 
island’, wisely used, might even prove sufficient to its own 
defence: to this end Drummond was particularly to urge, though 

<<<PDFPAGE 175>>>
WILLIAM DRUMMOND’S MISSION TO SICILY 
159 
‘with proper delicacy and caution’, reforms in financial and 
military administration. As for Naples, the British Government 
could give no absolute pledge that its recovery would be made 
a condition sine qua non of a peace, but they undertook to do 
their best to secure it.* 
Drummond arrived at Palermo in December 1806 to find 
the Court in even greater turmoil than usual. The aged favourite 
Sir John Acton had been ‘wormed out’ of the government three 
months earlier by the Queen’s Franco-Neapolitan faction, who 
now held undisputed control. Acton’s and Elliot’s advice had 
been to consolidate the defence of Sicily before embarking on 
an attempt against Naples; but the Queen wished, as she said, 
‘d’entamer la chose, de faire une explosion générale’, and she 
encouraged indiscriminate risings by the masse (brigands) in 
Calabria. She was furious at the British generals’ refusal to fall 
in with her plans, though by her own admission the Sicilian 
army was in a deplorable state, and could have given little help. 
Moreover, Drummond found convincing though not conclusive 
evidence that she had been intriguing with Russia for a strong 
force to be sent to Sicily, and was even then negotiating secretly 
with France. “The reason for this detestable treachery’, he wrote, 
‘arises partly from the abhorrence of the Queen and her minions 
for everything English; and partly from the belief that France 
is stronger than England, and that sooner or later France will 
become masters of Sicily. ... We must remember the enemies 
with whom we have to contend both without and within the 
island of Sicily.’ The British Commander-in-Chief at Messina, 
Gen. Henry Fox, was a sick and elderly man, and in his disgust 
with the Court he scarcely troubled to keep up relations with 
them. Sir John Moore, his second-in-command, thought the 
inhabitants were ripe for revolt against their hated government: 
‘if we do not, as they wish, rescue them from it, they will throw 
themselves into the arms of France. By what means we are to 
do this I shall not say, but the possession of Sicily is a most im- 
portant object, whether as a military post or as a valuable colony. 
If the Court of Palermo and the present government continue... 
I doubt if we shall hold it. Were they removed and the island 
declared English we should, with the hearts of the people and 
the energy which I think they could still be brought to display 

<<<PDFPAGE 176>>>
160 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
in a cause they approved, be able to maintain it against the 
world’. To cap the crisis, the Sicilian Government rejected the 
draft treaty in language which Drummond found evasive and 
even ‘apparently hostile’. Faced with this situation, he stopped 
payment of the subsidy and wrote home for instructions. 
Howick, after consultation with other Ministers, drafted a 
reply instructing Drummond to achieve four main objects: ‘rst. 
The conclusion of the treaty according to the original projet; 
2ndly: The removal of obnoxious and suspicious persons from 
the Councils of the Court; 3rdly. The admission of Sicilians to 
places of trust and confidence, or in other words, a Sicilian 
administration; 4thly. The enlargement of the powers of the 
British Commander in Chief over the Sicilian Army.’ The 
fourth point was ‘indispensable’. As for the second, Drummond 
was if possible to obtain for himself a discretionary power to 
remove undesirable foreigners from Palermo. The Court must 
be induced to pay attention to the Sicilians’ needs ‘and even to 
their wishes’; if necessary they should be persuaded to call an 
extraordinary Parliament, for ‘without the exertions of the 
Sicilians, His Majesty [George III] was aware that his efforts to 
preserve Sicily must prove fruitless’. Drummond was allowed 
considerable latitude as to the means of carrying out his instruc- 
tions. Amicable methods were best: he might remonstrate with 
the Government, advise the King, and engage in talks with the 
Sicilian nobles—Belmonte was suggested as a likely man. If 
need be Drummond could go on withholding the subsidy: and 
if any danger immediately threatened Sicily’s independence, he 
and the British commanders were to decide on measures to 
save it. ‘Sicily’, Howick wrote, ‘must not directly or indirectly 
fall under the control of France.’* 
Howick’s draft is dated March 1807. The Ministry of All the 
Talents fell on the 25th of that month, and the instructions were 
never sent out. Canning reversed Howick’s policy completely. 
He removed from the draft treaty, as ‘unseasonable’, the clauses 
giving Britain commercial privileges and a peacetime garrison: 
in his instructions to Drummond he confined himself to saying 
that Britain had a claim on the King of Sicily’s confidence and on 
his cooperation in the war.* 
Meanwhile Drummond had anticipated his government’s 

<<<PDFPAGE 177>>>
WILLIAM DRUMMOND’S MISSION TO SICILY 
I6I 
change of policy by a change of heart of his own. He was by 
way of being a student of political philosophy; he knew the 
Court from his residence as Minister at Naples from 1801 to 
1803; and he had come to Palermo ‘most strongly prejudiced 
against the Sicilian government, and not without the intention, 
if I found my opinions confirmed by experience, of proposing 
measures which would have in fact rendered Sicily a province 
of Great Britain’. 
Now, however, he found that there was not 
one patriotic party, but any number of factions, jealous of each 
other, though ‘all united in desiring to maintain those feudal 
rights, those instruments of tyranny by which they oppressed 
the peasantry’. Corruption and superstition reigned everywhere; 
of political vision he found very little. “So many reports were 
spread concerning the fate of the island, so many fine projects 
of change were suggested, so many splendid plans of reform 
were imagined, and so many little half-developed embryos of 
revolution began to shew themselves, that neither the nobles nor 
the people seemed to know very well to what government they 
were to belong. Every man who had the leisure built his political 
system according to his own fancy, and to suit his own conve- 
nience.... The Sicilians desired to receive laws from others, rather 
than to make laws for themselves. Some wished the French, and 
some the English to govern here, for men who are not for their 
country may be for anybody.’ Only unequivocal proofs of the 
Court’s ‘treachery’ would have justified him in overthrowing 
it; but such a revolution, besides being distasteful to him, could 
only succeed with the cooperation of the Sicilian people. Any 
reform of this “Constitution seven hundred years old in every 
species of political malady’ must therefore attack feudal privilege 
as well as Court misrule; it was bound to be difficult and 
dangerous; above all it must be carried out by the Sicilians them- 
selves without foreign interference. “The seat of legislature 
should rather be a Sicilian Parliament than a British camp.” 
Drummond therefore decided to come to an understanding 
with the Court, even before he had received fresh instructions. 
He agreed to changes in the draft treaty; and he resumed pay- 
ment of the subsidy on receiving the King’s promise that the 
Queen and the French émigrés would no longer have any share 
igoven the rnment, and that all important business would be 

<<<PDFPAGE 178>>>
162 
BENTINCK AND SICILY 
transacted among the King, Circello, and himself. The Queen 
seemed at first to acquiesce in this arrangement. ‘She even 
promised that if I would shew her any degree of confidence she 
would endeavour to introduce the English Constitution into 
Sicily. I will undertake to do this without Her Majesty’s assist- 
ance, which I am not particularly disposed to require.” 
Drummond’s assurances that ‘the Queen’s faction rules no 
longer, either in appearance or in reality’, met with derision 
from the staff officers at Messina, who thought the Court had 
merely hoodwinked him. Moore continued to protest that the 
Court was interested in Naples only and cared nothing for 
Sicily, and that an invasion of Italy would be useless ‘unless we 
had command of the King’s person, and unless the Queen and 
all her emissaries were removed, and we had the complete 
direction of the government. Nothing short of this would give 
confidence to the people or induce them to join us, and without 
the people it was vain to hope for permanent success in Italy.’ 
He talked of expelling the Queen to Trieste: ‘this was never 
proposed to be adopted’, he admitted, 
“... but only men- 
tioned . . . if possible to be done, it would be the surest way to 
quiet Sicily.’ Even when after a few months the King again 
eclipsed himself, and the Queen openly returned to the Council, 
Drummond went on asserting that her influence was exagger- 
ated: she herself, he said, was a mere dupe of the French party; 
the King was a firm friend of England, and would remain one 
if he was not frightened by the revolutionary chatter among 
the officers at Messina; and he, Drummond, had good hopes of 
persuading him presently to effect a gradual reform through 
Parliament, and to remove the Queen once more from the 
government. Belmonte he dismissed as a “Gallicised smatterer’, 
and he complained that the ‘speculators’ and advocates of whole- 
sale reform made his mission of persuasion difficult. After Moore 
had been recalled, partly as a result of his obstreperous conduct, 
Drummond claimed that ‘perfect union’ now prevailed among 
the Court, the commanders, and himself: he expected great 
things of the Court’s decision to raise a Sicilian militia of 40,000 
men, and declared that Sicily was at last coming out of her 
lethargy. Yet his optimism was punctured again and again by un- 
pleasant discoveries. He found that the ‘infatuated Queen... with 

<<<PDFPAGE 179>>>
WILLIAM DRUMMOND’S MISSION TO SICILY 
_163 
her usual inconsistency’ was intriguing with the Russian Minister 
for an arrangement whereby Ferdinand would be restored to 
Naples, and France would receive Sicily as compensation: he 
then had to bombard Circello with Notes until he had forced 
the government to break with Russia and hand the Minister his 
passports. His suspicions were aroused by intelligence of the 
Queen’s clandestine correspondence with the Continent; he was 
particularly annoyed by a secret proclamation of Moliterno’s, 
intended for circulation on the continent of Italy, and intercepted 
by the British officer in command at Capri, in which Moliterno 
bade the Italians rise, proclaim Ferdinand King of Italy, and 
drive out all foreigners. Drummond protested to the King and 
Circello, but their reassurances failed to convince him. Still, he 
had committed himself too far to retreat: and he continued on 
the whole to pin his hopes on the King.* 
Drummond was in a difficult position. The Court clamoured 
for an invasion of Italy, a plan he approved: but it had constantly 
to be put off during 1807 and 1808, either because the British 
garrison was depleted, or a French invasion was feared, or the 
state of the Continent made it impossible. The Court knew, 
besides, that the British Government still would not commit 
themselves to the recovery of Naples at a peace. So Drummond 
got little thanks from the Court, and little support from anyone. 
It was, one may say, Drummond’s vanity in thinking he could 
manage the Court by his own influence alone, without support, 
which led to his downfall. He had already irritated Canning by 
forcing, on his own initiative, a break between Sicily and Russia. 
A few months later he caused serious embarrassment to the 
British Government by furthering, again on his own initiative, 
and unofficially, the Court’s claim to the Regency of Spain. 
Canning recalled him at once, in September 1808.”

‘... A présent, Monseigneur, je viens vous présenter les réves

j'ai mieux connu la Nation Sicilienne, sa situation future a été 
objet de mes sérieuses réflections, et de mes vives inquiétudes. 
L’indépendance de la Sicile, et la séparation des deux Couronnes 
a été voté par le Parlement et quoique la Nation soit divisée sur 
d’autres points, cependant elle est unanime contre toute connec- 
tion avec le Royaume de Naples; et je doute que la réunion 
puisse encore s’opérer sans une grande effusion de sang. Il est 
certain que l’entreprise deviendra de jour en jour plus difficule. 
Mais il est clair en méme temps que l’administration du 
Gouvernement de la Sicile sans l’appui d’une force indépendante 
sera presque impossible. La Nation ne peut pas étre confi¢e a 
elle méme. Il y aurait a craindre tous les causes de la Révolution 
Francaise. 
La Nation donc pour le moment ne peut pas se gouverner 
elle méme, ne veut pas étre gouvernée par les Napolitains et 
il ne conviendra pas je m’imagine 4 l’ Angleterre de continuer le 
frais de l’entretien d’une force protectrice aprés la paix générale. 
Voici donc, Monseigneur, mon réve. Laissez le soin de la 
Sicile 4 Angleterre, qui peut seule, j’ose dire, la gouverner 
avantageusement, et pour la Sicile et pour le Royaume de Naples. 
Au roi de Naples la Sicile n’a jamais été d’aucun avantage. Elle 
a été au contraire un embarras. Le Roi n’en a jamais tiré un 
soldat, et les revenues n’ont pas méme suffi pour payer les troupes

pas gagné de l’argent. Dorénavant la dépense doit augmenter 
et V.A.R. peut bien juger si les revenues de I’'Isle peuvent étre 
augmentées dans la méme proportion.

GRAHAM'S ACCOUNT OF HIS CONVERSATION 
WiLHVCAS 
LER BAGH 
‘... We then discussed the subject of your private letter, and 
I detailed to His Lordship your plan for the permanent occu- 
pation of Sicily as a British Territory: he said in the case where 
it was built on the restoration of Ferdinand to the Crown of 
Naples, our engagements with Murat were decisive: he himself 
had no idea, neither did he desire, that those engagements should 
be violated: if however Murat still refused to cooperate and 
remained unsatisfied with the explanation Your Lordship was 
directed to give him, then, if we could recover Naples, your 
plan became feasible and worthy of consideration. 
In the case of an indemnity, he feared it was not practicable; 
for no State we could give as compensation for Naples and no 
sum of money we could offer would, he conceived, be sufficient 
temptation for the relinquishment of such an island as Sicily. 
I then insisted at great length on the extreme impolicy of 
evacuating it at a peace, and urged the Treaty with Murat as 
an imperative reason why Sicily never could be safe without 
a British garrison. 
Iam happy to say His Lordship coincided most entirely in 
your opinion; he saw no difficulty in obtaining the consent of 
the King of Sicily to the arrangement; and the Allies would 
readily allow us to act in this respect as we chose. France at any 
other moment would have been an insurmountable obstacle; 
but humbled as she was, no objection of hers would avail, and 
ina peace he would particularly guard against any compromise 
of the question. 

<<<PDFPAGE 183>>>
DOCUMENTS CONCERNING THE ‘DREAM’ 
167 
The only difficulty he foresaw was in our Treaty with Murat, 
who, precisely because he would wish for an occasion to conquer 
Sicily, would violently object to our occupation of it: but this 
might be met by an article, on which the British Government 
would insist, stipulating that Murat should consent to any 
agreement which Great Britain might conclude with the King 
of Sicily on this point. 
On the whole His Lordship entered most cordially into your 
view of this question and promised not to lose sight of it during 
the negotiations....I have no doubt, therefore, My Lord, if 
the present negotiations should happily terminate in a peace, 
that your hopes for Sicily will not be disappointed. . . .’ 
(James Graham to Bentinck, 7 March 1814, B.P.)

APPENDIX(CG 
BENTINGRS-GONDU 
GC IVATEGENOA 
By the middle of April 1814 Bentinck’s army had reached the 
outskirts of Genoa after a short campaign up the Riviera by way 
of Sarzana and Nervi. On the 18th the Genoa garrison sur- 
rendered; two days earlier the armistice of Schiarino Rizzino 
had brought to an end the Viceroy Eugéne’s able defence of 
Lombardy against the Austrians and Murat. Paris had fallen, 
but the war in France was not yet over officially.” On April 
26th Bentinck issued a proclamation in which he declared that, 
in accordance with the wishes of the people, and with the prin- 
ciple—‘recognised by the High Allied Powers’—of restoring the 
old sovereigns, the Constitution of the Genoese Republic was 
re-established as it had stood in 1797, ‘with those modifications 
which the general wish, the public good, and the spirit of the origi- 
nal Constitution of 1576, seem to require’. The Austrian generals 
had been proclaiming the return of the old sovereigns all over 
Northern Italy; but Bentinck had been enjoined by Castlereagh 
on March 30th not to commit himself to the restoration of any 
of them except the King of Piedmont and the Grand-Duke of 
Tuscany. The result of Bentinck’s act was, of course, that Britain 
had to repudiate it and, by endorsing the annexation of Genoa to 
Piedmont, to confirm her reputation as Perfidious Albion.* 
Bentinck, when he came to justify himself, told Bathurst 
that it was all very well to settle the fate of Genoa at a peace, but 
‘Thad to provide for a state of war. My duty commanded me to ensure 
the success of the war in Italy, which the weakness of the Austrian army, 
and the treachery of Murat, might at any time bring to a conclusion the 
most unfavourable to the Allied arms... .. The fact that Genoa as a mili- 
tary place was untenable without more troops than Bentinck disposed 
of made it ‘indispensable for me to secure the tranquillity and cordial 
cooperation of the Genoese. Without these the further advance of the 
British force became hazardous and in case of reverse, the preservation 
of what I had, precarious.—No other measure as I thought but the one 
I adopted could have effected these objects. . . 
. cannot but still continue 
to think that the form of the Provisional Government was not only 
advisable but necessary.”* 

<<<PDFPAGE 185>>>
BENTINCK 
’S CONDUCT AT GENOA 
169 
Bentinck’s private campaign diary tells a different story. On 
April 22nd—he recorded—he told a deputation of Genoese 
officials that he would set up a provisional government, ‘their 
final disposition necessarily depending upon the decision of the 
high Allied Powers’. During the next three days he discussed 
the matter with some of the leading Genoese—Pareto, Serra, 
Durazzo—and found them aristocratically inclined but ‘liberal 
enough’. On April 26th, the date of the Proclamation, he wrote: 
‘Tt was a question whether in the Proclamation it should be stated 
that this arrangement was to continue until the Allies should otherwise 
order—but it was considered that the most popular would be to make 
an arrangement as definitive as possible which it would be the more 
difficult for the Allies to upset. The union with Piedmont as supposed 
likely was most of all offensive to the Genoese. With the Milanese they 
would not equally dislike to be annexed—but their original independence 
seemed the universal wish—I thought it politic for Great Britain that is 
for her interests and influence that the wishes of the people should receive 
her consideration while it was always in the hands of the Allies to 
make whatever arrangement they pleased.”* 
We may never know just what Bentinck thought he was 
doing; to judge from the muddle-headedness exhibited in this 
curious document, he scarcely knew himself.

landlords’ agents or chief tenants. Michele Amari estimated that the Third Estate was at 
this period no larger, or very slightly larger, than the nobility: preface to N. Palmeri, 
Saggio storico-politico sulla costituzione del Regno di Sicilia (Lausanne, 1847), Xxxi. 
PAGE 3 
a constitutional conflict between Crown and Parliament. The power of the nobility may be 
judged by the fact that, numbering as they did about 2,300 out of a population of 
about 1,500,000, they held under their jurisdiction (according to the latest, and in- 
complete, survey, that of 1747-8) 282 towns and lands (feudi) as against 85 in the Royal 
Domain, and 780,688 inhabitants as against 395,967 (Palermo, then the second city 
of Italy, with 180,000 inhabitants, was not included in the survey). There was no up- 
to-date register of fiefs, duties, boundaries etc.; taxes had therefore to be assessed on 
the basis of riveli, i.e. declarations of value by the occupiers. One of the most important 
points in Caracciolo’s programme of reform was the carrying out of a thorough 
Catasto (survey) of all lands; it was also, for obvious reasons, the point over which the 
Viceroy and the barons in Parliament had their most serious clash: E. Pontieri, II 
Tramonto del Baronaggio Siciliano (Florence, 1943), 92 ff., 222 ff. (This work gives 
(pp. 195-283) the most up-to-date and elaborate account of Caracciolo’s Viceroyalty; 
cf. also B. Croce, ‘Il Marchese Caracciolo’, in Uomini e Cose della vecchia Italia (Bari, 
1927); F. De Stefano, Storia della Sicilia (Bari, 1948), 274-7; R. Romeo, II Risorgimento 
in Sicilia (Bari, 1950), 50-65.) 
those rights were still unquestioned. The Sicilian Parliament was made up of three Estates 
(Braccia), the Militare (barons), Ecclesiastico, and Demaniale (communes of the Royal 
Domain). Of the great powers it had held under the Aragonese, it had by the 18th 
century lost the legislative and judicial; but it kept those of voting donativi (i.e. taxes 
designed to supplement the revenue the Crown enjoyed of its own right from customs, 
dues, etc.; there were ordinary donativi—a variety of taxes making up a fixed revenue, 
voted as a matter of course by successive Parliaments—and extraordinary donativi pro- 
vided for emergencies) ; 
of petitioning the Crown; and of electing the Deputation of the 
Realm—a delegate body supposed to supervise the collection and administration of 
the revenue, and the enforcement of laws, during the four-year interval between 
Parliaments. Actually, until the 1780’s these powers had been accepted as merely 
formal. The Deputation was in practice nominated by the Crown, as was the Braccio 
Demaniale itself. It was never suggested that conditions should be attached to votes of 
supply. Voting in the first two Braccia was by fiefs, so that their numbers were not 
large; the Demaniale was smaller still. Voting by proxy was allowed. In 1810, the 
Militare had 277 members, the Ecclesiastico 60 to 66, the Demaniale 44: Pontieri, 
op. cit., 127-45; G. Bianco, La Sicilia durante I’ occupazione inglese (Palermo, 1902), 32-6. 
lately been pointed out. By Romeo, op. cit., 19-26. The recent works of Romeo and. 
Pontieri have thrown much light on the economic background of the patriotic 
movement.

later and ‘unnatural’ growths. Gregorio combated the interpretation of the Norman 
Conquest according to which Roger had been no more than primus inter pares, as: 
well as the prevailing interpretation of the Aragonese capitularies Si aliquem and 
Volentes, which allowed almost unlimited alienation of, and succession to, fiefs. He 
held that fiefs were historically precarious and their enjoyment dependent on services. 
rendered to the Crown: in default of a (restricted) succession, they reverted to the 
Crown, and they could not be alienated. This contention was embodied by the Viceregal 
Government in a Pragmatic of 1788 which, however, does not seem to have had 
much effect. Gregorio also contended that Parliament was no more than the Norman.
