Catania, where a new class of middling landowners was rising; it was largely from 
this class that the few ‘Jacobins’ of the 1790’s and the leaders of the later ‘Democratic’ 
movement of 1813 were drawn: Romeo, 78ff., 107, 114. 
too foreign to be compatriots. ‘If [an Italian from countries other than Naples] came to 
Sicily ... he was confused with visitors from beyond the Alps, and given the name 
of foreigner, unless he had been born at Naples, in which case he did not seem worthy 
of that honourable title’: M. Amari, preface to 4th ed. of La Guerra del Vespro Siciliano 
(Florence, 1851).

(Bibl. Com. Pal. MSS. Qq H 115 n. 10). Both these writers disliked the 1812 
Constitution and the British intervention; both upheld the independence of Sicily 
against Neapolitan intrusion. Tognini goes so far as to assert that in twice recon- 
quering Naples from his refuge in Sicily, King Ferdinand, like the Normans before 
him, proved that Sicily was the natural seat of government of both kingdoms.

This might be taken as shewing that the decision to intervene was not made only 
on grounds of security, and that the Government must have accepted at least in part 
Bentinck’s thesis that Sicilian reform would mean a better chance of Italian insur- 
rection (cf. Crawley, 259 n. 3). Wellesley’s instructions, however, speak only of 
the inadequate defence of Sicily—especially the unreliability of the Court’s troops— 
and the persistence of internal disaffection.

The King filled his Journal, from the very start of the Vicariate, with complaints 
of his son’s disobedience. The Queen cared little who heard her aspersions on the 
Prince’s conduct—‘Bete qu’il est—she cursed him in all languages’: Journal, 18 Apr. 
1812. Bentinck was astonished to learn that the Prince continued to write affectionate 
letters to his mother, often twice a day, when ‘she hates him, he fears her!’: Journal 
23 Apr. 1812. He could not understand this peculiarly Mediterranean kind of family 
feeling, which could combine mutual exasperation with mutual devotion, and made 
the Prince—a man in his thirties, himself a father—as submissive as a child before 
his parents.

new Vespers against the English. Bentinck to Wellesley, 19 Mar. 1812, FO 70/51. Cassetti

<<<PDFPAGE 201>>>
NOTES 
185 
question of the queen’s ‘treachery’ is difficult (and largely unprofitable) to disentangle. 
There is evidence that she was in correspondence with Murat and possibly with France. 
There is also evidence that at various times she thought of giving up Sicily in exchange 
for her husband’s restoration to Naples. On the other hand, there can be no doubt 
that the marriage of her granddaughter Marie-Louise (which aroused such suspicion 
among the British in Palermo) genuinely horrified her (see her letters to Ruffo, to 
the Empress Maria Ludovika, and to the Emperor Francis, 22, 23 Mar., 7 Apr. 1810, 
in Corti, 768-9, 772). But eighteen months later, after Bentinck’s first remonstrances, 
she was ready to take refuge in Vienna (as she had often thought of doing before) 
and to ask Napoleon’s leave to do so; she hinted that the Emperor should intercede 
with Napoleon to reconcile him with King Ferdinand and with herself (to the 
Emperor Francis, 30 Aug., to Ruffo, 9 Sep. 1811, in Corti, 614). At the same moment, 
however, she was thinking of calling in the Russians (see p. 26 and note). In fact she 
had no settled plan at all—only a settled desire to recover Naples—and it seems most 
likely that by this time her mind was too unstable to allow her to follow any coherent 
policy.

BENTINCK AND SICILY 
(Castelcicala reminded the British Government that they did not care much for 
Parliamentary Reform at home). Webster (i, 78) says the King first suggested the 
English Constitution. This is a plausible interpretation of Bentinck’s despatches (see 
p. 49 and note), but it is contradicted by Bentinck’s Journal and by the King’s own 
Giornale d’ Affari, which Webster could not have seen. In fact the King went on 
denying his son’s right to effect any kind of reform through Parliament. Only in 
August 1812 did he start insisting on the pure English Constitution—after Parliament 
had voted the Bases of the new Constitution. The deep-laid schemes which Webster 
attributes to him were not his but the Prince’s (see p. 50). All constitutions were 
anathema to the King; if he later clung to the English it was as a pis-aller. 
the prospect for reform was bleak. Journal, 26-29 May 1812; Bentinck to Castlereagh, 
30 June 1812, FO 70/51. This long despatch has been used as the chief source for the 
account which follows of the political events of June 1812. Sometimes Bentinck’s 
journal has been used to disentangle the chronology, and to fillin gaps in the despatch.

his brother at Palermo. Journal, 29, 31 May 1812. Bentinck was aware of Aci’s part in

person; instead he merely wrote that the Royal Family had become enamoured of 
the English Constitution.

PAGE 74 
so humbugged by the Hereditary Prince as Belmonte himself. Journal, 30 June, 24 July, 
6 Aug., 14 Oct., 15, 27 Dec. 1812; Balsamo, 72-6, 80-1. According to Balsamo, 
Belmonte, though ‘at bottom he was good and generous and valued Castelnuovo’, 
was led by vanity, ambition and the flattery of his friends into a belief that Castelnuovo 
was a ‘democrat’, influenced by French ideas and hostile to the nobility. (Balsamo 
was, of course, Castelnuovo’s lieutenant, but he is on the whole fair to Belmonte). 
Bentinck, for his part, sometimes wondered whether Belmonte was not still somewhat 
afraid of the Royal Family and therefore reluctant to press the Prince too hard. 
Orleans certainly thought that Belmonte’s most unpleasant period of captivity at 
Favignana had sapped his nerve as well as ruined his health. If so Belmonte had every 
excuse for caution: after the 1799 atrocities at Naples, he could expect no clemency 
from the Court if British support was once withdrawn from him—his and his friends’ 
heads ‘would be the first to go’: Journal, 27 July 1812.

the deficit remained almost as large as before. Balsamo, 87; Bianco, 127-8, 145-6; Niceforo, 
xliv, 115-19. Castelnuovo had reported a standing deficit of 777, 576 oz., or a little 
over £500,000 at 13s. to the ounce (expenditure 2,493,810 oz., revenue 1,716,234 0z.). 
After the Budget the ostensible deficit was 168,402 0z.; but of the supply voted by 
Parliament, 264,000 oz. assigned to the Army was supposed to come from old credits 
which the government had found it impossible to collect. One of the chief diffi- 
culties was that the land-tax passed by the 1810 Parliament could still be assessed 
only on the basis of declarations by the owners (riveli) in the absence of an adequate 
land register.

inutile de lutter contre le torrent. Bentinck to Bunbury (private) 31 Aug. 1812, to Bathurst

thought they would come’ (they did not): Journal, 28 Aug. One of the conditions

would always be the same, because the British influence if they made the conquest 
would always direct with ease the will of the people’: Journal, 22 Dec. 1812; cf.

the ‘private ends’ of Austria and Russia. Bentinck to Bathurst (secret) 24 Feb.; the Hereditary 
Prince to the Prince Regent, enclosed with Belmonte to Castelcicala, 23 Feb., FO 
70/61; Bentinck to Bathurst (confidential) 7 Apr., Bathurst MSS. 230; Lamb to 
Hamilton, 25 May, FO 70/62 (‘I fear there is... enough to get me the name of a 
most troublesome fellow’).—It has been a commonplace with a number of Italian 
historians, both contemporary and modern, that the secret societies (especially the 
Carboneria) which made their appearance in the last years of the Napoleonic regime 
were subsidised with ‘British gold’ and encouraged by swarms of British agents 
throughout Italy: cf. for instance P. Colletta, Storia del Reame di Napoli (Capolago, 
1834), bk. VIII, ch. xlix; A. Luzio, La Massoneria e il Risorgimento Italiano (Bologna, 
1925), 1, 118-9, 162-6; O. Dito, Massoneria, Carboneria e altre societa segrete (Turin, 1905), 
208-9; Capograssi, op. cit., ch. xi. Such statements are generally based, if at all, on 
informers’ reports (during the Restoration period), hearsay, and inference. Capograssi 
even speaks (p, 184) of Bentinck’s ‘innumerable agents scattered throughout the 
peninsula’—whereas as late as the spring of 1813 Bentinck decided, because he had 
hitherto been unable to establish any satisfactory communication with Italy, to set 
up three intelligence agents at Malta, Vienna and Ponza for the purpose (Bentinck 
to Castlereagh, secret, 21 Mar. 1813, in M.H. Weil, Le Prince Eugene et Murat (Paris 
1902), i, 433-4); and a ‘Memorandum of the Secret Missions under Lord William 
Bentinck’ (drawn up by his secretary, 24 May 1813: PRO GD/7/1) confirms that 
these were the only agents dealing with Italy. Moreover, neither Bentinck’s des- 
patches nor his private papers supply any evidence that he was ever in direct contact 
with the secret societies before 1814. The indirect contacts he did have can be listed 
as follows: 1. The Archduke Francis and his supporters: their plans came to nothing, 
at any rate in Italy, and after 1812 little is heard of them. 2. One Alessandro Turri, 
who approached Bentinck in 1811 and apparently stayed in Sicily until 1814. He 
claimed to represent a secret society affiliated to the Freemasons; Bentinck distrusted 
him at first, though later on he employed Turri to write propaganda articles for the 

<<<PDFPAGE 215>>>
NOTES 
199 
Gazzetta Britannica and had him draft his Leghorn Proclamation in March 1814 (cf. 
page 138 and note). But otherwise Turri’s activity seems to have produced nothing 
beyond a flock of memoranda; there is no sign that Bentinck took his grandiose 
schemes for an Italian Patriotic army any more seriously than he had taken Moliterno’s 
(Turri even went into the question of what colour the proposed army’s uniforms 
should be, and sought to ingratiate himself with the Nation of Shopkeepers by 
assuring Bentinck that his scheme would not cost Britain a penny, and that she might 
later reap commercial advantages in an independent Italy). In fact it appears from 
a sarcastic comment by C. A. A’Court, Bentinck’s A.D.C. (endorsement of Turri to 
Bentinck, Leghorn, 18 Apr. 1814, B.P.) that Turri was thought something of a joke 
at H.Q. (Bentinck to Wellington, 14 Nov. 1811, B.P.; Turri’s papers, ibid.). 3. One 
Giuseppe Palumbo, who approached the British Commander at Ponza in April 1813 
on behalf of a society in the Kingdom of Naples; he was advised to put off his plans 
for insurrection, and no more is heard of him (Coffin to Bentinck, secret, 23 Apr. 
1813, FO 70/58). 4. A few individuals who gave Bentinck information on Italy, 
without claiming to represent any organisation. This is meagre evidence. Of course 
lack of evidence is not disproof: Bentinck may have had greater contact with the secret 
societies than his papers reveal; and there is no doubt that towards the end of the war 
the societies tended to look to Britain for guidance, whether they got it or not. But 
in the absence of stronger evidence it seems reasonable to surmise that Bentinck never 
had any direct or reliable contact with the Italian patriots until he himself went to 
Italy in 1814; that this situation was practically inevitable given the state of communi- 
cations between Sicily and the Continent; and that his belief in the Italians’ desire and 
capacity to rise against the French was based much less on any knowledge of what 
was actually going on in Italy than on his own hopes and intuitions.

<<<PDFPAGE 219>>>

203 
the British Constitution could be carried to a dangerous length. Sicily is now for 
knowledge and morals what was England two centuries back. Too much liberty is 
for the Sicilians what would be a pistol or a stiletto in the hands of a boy or a madman’:

no date) must date from Oct. or Nov. 1813—to judge from internal evidence and 
from references to it in the letter quoted and in Balsamo’s Memorie, 155-6—though 
Bianco (pp. 183-4) dated a copy he found in the Palermo Archives July 1813. It was 
after the 1813 Parliament, not before, that the Constitutionalists began to feel the 
need of a stronger executive power. Some of the changes proposed by Balsamo are 
curious: he suggests that taxes should be divided into perpetual (‘independent from 
any parliamentary grant’), ordinary (army estimates), and extraordinary (emergencies) 
—only the latter two kinds to be voted by Parliament; and then, after this return to 
the old Sicilian Constitution, he goes on to say that ‘the Parliament must retain the 
full power of altering or changing any perpetual or ordinary tax’!

only through British protection. Bentinck to Douglas, Syracuse 11 Dec. 1813, GD 7/1; 
fragment of Journal (on loose sheet), 2, 3, Dec., B.P. Of Taormina Bentinck noted: 
‘The most beautiful view I ever saw—Aetna covered with snow on one side—the 
sea on another and the country immediately adjacent most bold and picturesque.’ 
He was no poet. But the sight may well have affected him. Other travellers of the 
time were all the more ready to keep in mind Sicily’s ancient glories because the 
island, it seems safe to say, was not then so backward compared to the rest of Europe 
as it became afterwards. Maj.-Gen. Cockburn, who kept comparing Sicily with 
Ireland, thought that although its poverty and squalor were appalling it was none 
the less the better off of the two: A Voyage to Cadiz... in 1810 and 1811 (1815), i,

we can converse on this subject. C. A. A’Court to Lady Wm. Bentinck, Paris 5 May, in 
Gallavresi, ‘Rivoluzione lombarda’, 130; Castlereagh to Bentinck, 7 May, Castlereagh 
Corr. x, 17. It was in this letter that Castlereagh told Bentinck that ‘It is impossible 
not to perceive a great moral change coming on in Europe... the principles of 
freedom are in full operation. The danger is, that the transition may be too sudden 
to ripen into anything likely to make the world better or happier. We have new 
constitutions launched in France, Spain, Holland and Sicily. Let us see the result 
before we encourage farther attempts. The attempts may be made, and we must 
abide the consequences; but I am sure it is better to retard than accelerate the operation 
of this most hazardous principle which is abroad. In Italy, it is now the more necessary 
to abstain, if we wish to act in concert with Austria and Sardinia. Whilst we had to 
drive the French out of Italy, we were justified in running all risks; but the present state 
of Europe requires no such expedient; and, with a view to general peace and tran- 
quillity, I should prefer seeing the Italians await the insensible influence of what is 
going on elsewhere, than hazard their own internal quiet by an effort at this moment.’

giving up politics altogether. On 11 June Castelnuovo, who had not yet seen Bentinck, 
wrote to his friend Settimo: ‘You must have noticed as you left me last night that 
I was bewildered rather than angry—let me tell you that ever since then, until 
40’clock this morning, I stayed up thinking over this culmination of all our troub- 
les. ...’ Finally he decided to write to Bentinck and tell him that if he, Bentinck, 
decided to countenance the present conduct of the government (i.e. of the Belmontists) 
then he would withdraw into private life rather than put up any opposition. ‘Labouring 
as my mind was under the thought that I might perhaps displease, however slightly, 
a man to whom we owe so much, and under the necessity of forcing myself not to 
trust my pen with any stronger expression, I was almost unable to put a single idea 
down on paper, and I scarcely know how I managed to write those few lines .. .’:

30 June, to Castlereagh, 4 July, FO 70/64. A’Court’s statement said that despite the 
difference of opinion between them, he would take no step ‘tending to overthrow the 
arrangements made by Lord William Bentinck’; as Bentinck seemed to think that non- 
intervention on England’s part was tantamount to active opposition to the ‘system’, 
A’Court promised to support it by every means short of violence. All this, however, 
he undertook only because Bentinck’s absence and his own tenure were still officially 
temporary; and it was subject to the Government’s instructions: A’Court’s Memo- 
randum to Bentinck, 15 July, B.P. In fact A’Court was almost certain that Bentinck 
would not return: to Hamilton (private) 3 July, FO 70/6s. 
out of the government service for the next thirteen years. Liverpool to Bathurst, 12 Sep. 
1814, Bathurst MSS., 286. In October Castlereagh asked Bathurst to stop Bentinck 
(who had spent the summer in England) from going out to Italy again: ‘the dread 
of his Whiggish and revolutionary politics in Italy would add to the alarms of 
Austria. . 
. 
. I have had an express representation from Metternich on this subject, and 
I am sure, if his Lordship’s appearance in this quarter can ever be wise or useful (of 
which I entertain considerable doubts), it can only be in the event of hostile measures 
being actually decided on, and being on the point of execution’: to Bathurst (private) 
4 Oct. 1814, ibid., 295. To which Bentinck, who was already in Paris, replied that 
he would rather resign his command than ‘sanction an imputation ... not less 
unfavourable to my honour than to my judgment. . 
. . If Florence is thought too near 
the Austrian States, I can as easily remove to Rome, and I will answer for His Holiness 
not objecting either to my religion or my politics, tout heretick et revolutionnaire as 
I may be’: Bentinck to Bathurst (private) 23 Oct., ibid., 301. (Bentinck had made a 
friend of the Pope by giving him aid and comfort while he was making his way 
from Northern Italy to Rome at the end of the war.) The occasion that precipitated 
Bentinck’s final recall was an approach which he and William Hill, the Minister at 
Turin, made to the Piedmontese Minister Vallaise, urging that the King of Piedmont 
should grant a constitution and so raise Northern Italy against Murat: Bathurst to 
Bentinck (private) 26 Apr. 1815, B.P.; cf. F. Lemmi, ‘La Restaurazione dello Stato 
Sardo nel 1814-15’, in Universita di Torino, Méiscellanee della Facolta di Lettere e 
Filosofia, ser. 2 (1938). Bentinck’s protest against his recall (to Bathurst, 18 June 
1815—see p. 168 and note) defended his conduct since 1811: by his exertions, he said, 
Sicily, which had never before contributed to the Allied cause, had been enabled to 
send 12,000 men to Spain in 1812, and a further 12,000 to Italy in 1814; his hostility 
to Murat had been justified by later events; his explanation of the Genoa episode 
appears in Appendix C. He accordingly asked either ‘to be told in what my crime 
has consisted, and to be heard in my defence’, or else to be allowed to stay in the 
government service. Bathurst’s reply (20 June 1815) was formal and non-committal 
(B.P.). Already, when the news of Bentinck’s recall had first come out, his friend 
Pellew (now Lord Exmouth), who shared all his views on Italy, had written to him: 
‘I can perceive the evil finger of Lord C... gh throughout. . . . It is possible you may 
trace the noble Lord’s disinclination towards you to causes more remote when he 
was not so popular as he is now with Canning under his thumb’: to Bentinck, 23 Apr. 
1815, B.P. Bentinck was not employed again until 1828, when he became Governor 
of Bengal. As late as 1824 his notoriety had not faded: ‘They are in the greatest fuss 
now for fear Lord William Bentinck should be appointed ... High Commissioner 
of the Ionian Islands. Metternich said all the Italian Sovereigns, and first among them 
the Emperor himself, would protest loudly against it. They have a greater horror of 
Lord William than of any other radical in Europe and last year, when Lady William 
was in Italy, they never allowed her to get a single letter from Lord William’:

Sicily’s constitution and independence. See the King’s very clear-headed estimate of Sicilian

speedily confirmed. A’Court to Bentinck (private) 24 July 1814, B.P. The history of 
Sicily between Bentinck’s departure in July 1814 and the King’s return to Naples 
in May 1815 
is dealt with at length by Bianco, 251 ff. He is mistaken, however, in 
stating that A’Court supported the King’s reactionary policy from the first. It is clear 
from the King’s Giornale d’ Affari that he was much annoyed at what he considered 
A’Court’s unwarranted interference on behalf of the ‘persecuted’ constitutionalists. 
Actually, that ‘persecution’ amounted to little more than the loss of places and 
official favour. 
a ‘posthumous conquest of Napoleon’. L. Blanch, ‘Luigi de’ Medici’, 166. By the decrees of 
December 1816 Ferdinand changed his title to Ferdinand I, King of the Kingdom 
of the Two Sicilies; the whole existing system of local government was abolished, 
and seven new provinces set up each under an Intendente, with new municipal councils 
to be nominated by the King. Conscription was introduced. The annual revenue 
was fixed at the 1813 level (which, however, had included the British subsidy); any 
increases would have to be ‘approved by Parliament’. Previously, the Sicilian flag, 
the separate Sicilian army, and the freedom of the Press had been abolished: Bianco, 
278-94. 
Austria’s Italian territories. Strictly speaking, the secret Austro-Neapolitan treaty applied 
to Naples only; but Austria objected as much to a constitutional system in Sicily as 
in Naples. 
a plain, honourable, and consistent tale. A’Court to Stewart, 6 Noy. 1816. The course of

comment is that this was “one of the most equivocal [episodes] in which Castlereagh 
took part’.
